Treosulfan
Treosulphan, dihydroxybusulfan, CAS # 299-75-2, Ovastat®

Injection, lyophilized
(1 gm, 5 gm)
Antineoplastic
Small molecule
Alkylating agent
Treosulfan is an alkylating agent. DNA alkylation is thought to be
responsible for the cytotoxic activities of treosulfan. Treosulfan
depleted hematopoietic stem cells and showed immunosuppressive and antitumor activity in mouse models of leukemia.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (NCT00822393) was a multicenter, randomized, active-controlled trial
comparing treosulfan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for
allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old with AML in complete
remission or MDS, Karnofsky performance status > 60%, and hematopoietic cell transplantation
comorbidity index [HCT CI] score > 2 for patients < 50 years old. The patients were randomized
to receive treosulfan 10 g/m² daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours
on days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2,
and hematopoietic stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was
administered as well to patients with unrelated donors.
The study was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of event-free survival
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

10

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
(EFS) with 2 years of follow-up after transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse,
graft failure, or death. The noninferiority margin on the hazard ratio scale was 1.3. If
noninferiority was demonstrated, then the primary endpoint would be tested for superiority.
Overall survival (OS) was a secondary endpoint, but there was no alpha allocated to testing OS.
The trial was stopped at the second interim analysis when EFS noninferiority was achieved.
Since the trial continued enrolling until the results of the interim analysis were known, an
additional "last analysis" was conducted using all enrolled patients (N = 570). Based on the last
analysis, the Applicant concluded that the results supported noninferiority for EFS, and in
sequential testing, superiority of treosulfan for EFS (based on 224 events). Superiority of
treosulfan for EFS was not demonstrated at the second interim analysis, though the boundary
for superiority based on the updated number of EFS events was crossed at the last analysis.
However, FDA did not consider the trial to have demonstrated superiority of treosulfan for EFS
at the last analysis, as this analysis was not pre-planned and had been requested by FDA.
During review of the original NDA submission, a Complete Response was issued on 30 July 2021
as the review team was unable to confirm the results of the primary endpoint analysis since the
raw data needed to verify the components of the EFS endpoint were incomplete. In the
subsequent resubmissions (21 April 2022, 30 April 2024), the Applicant submitted all available
CBC data and some bone marrow results. In the current resubmission, the Applicant submitted
the results of all bone marrow results including original reports.
For FDA’s analysis of efficacy, the analysis set consisted of all randomized patients. This
included 570 patients with a median age of 70 years (range 31, 70); 61% were male. Race and
ethnicity data were not collected. Sixty-four percent of patients had AML. Ninety-seven
percent of patients received peripheral blood stem cells.
Study MC FludT.14/L Trial II was considered a positive study based on the primary endpoint of
EFS (non-inferiority p-value = 0.0000164 as compared to an efficacy boundary of 0.000149).
Based on FDA-adjudication of EFS events, the hazard ratio for EFS was 0.65 (95% CI: 0.50, 0.85)
in the ITT population. However, the definition of EFS used by the Applicant reflected a
composite of efficacy (relapse and death) and safety (graft failure) measures. In the accepted
definition of EFS for AML, graft failure is not considered an efficacy event. For patients with
AML in CR, the applicable endpoint would be relapse-free survival (RFS). For patients with MDS
not in CR, achievement of remission might reflect the effectiveness of treatment, but the
protocol definition of EFS does not take into account failure to achieve remission and the data
collection was not sufficient to reliably adjudicate achievement of CR in the MDS population.
Therefore, the clinical meaningfulness of the EFS endpoint as defined in the protocol is unclear
for both the AML and MDS populations. Additional analyses of EFS and related endpoints
(including RFS) were conducted to explore the robustness of the result to definition and
population. The results of these additional analyses consistently favored the treosulfan arm.
(b) (4)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
While Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was not designed to test OS, this endpoint is the gold
standard for establishing efficacy in oncology. Thus, FDA relied on OS, defined as the time from
randomization until death from any cause, to establish the efficacy of treosulfan. The HR for OS
(Cox proportional hazards model, stratified by donor type and risk group) compared to busulfan
was 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90) in the ITT population, 0.73 (95% CI: 0.51, 1.06) in patients with
AML, and 0.64 (95% CI: 0.40, 1.02) in patients with MDS. Despite the limitations of the study
design and available data, the results appear consistent with superiority overall. However,
since there was no prespecified alpha-spending plan for OS, no p-values will be reported.
Supportive evidence for the effectiveness of treosulfan as a component of a preparative
regimen was based on the clinical evidence from the dose-finding study MC-FludT.6/L which
accrued a total of 55 patients, including 19 with AML and 6 with MDS. Study MC-FludT.6/L
achieved its primary objective to evaluate the feasibility and tolerability of different dose levels
of treosulfan prior to allogeneic HSCT. In addition, the 1-year OS outcomes for patients with
hematologic malignancies who received the treosulfan-based preparative regimen at 10
g/m2/dose, 12 g/m2/dose, and 14 g/m2/dose were 70%, 78%, and 53%, respectively. Although
FDA does not usually use landmark OS in a single-arm trial to support efficacy given the
potential for bias, the findings in MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible in two dose cohorts (10 g/m2
and 12 g/m2), the results in the 10 g/m2 cohort were consistent with that of the treosulfan arm
of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (1-year OS: 79%), and the results in the 14 g/m2 cohort were also
consistent with the results in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (1-year OS: 67%). The
consistency of these findings reduces concerns for bias and supports the conclusion that the 10
g/m2/dose treosulfan is effective as part of a preparative regimen.
On the basis of biology of AML and MDS and the mechanism of action of treosulfan, the efficacy
of treosulfan 10 g/m2/dose can be extrapolated from the studies in adults to pediatric patients.
(b) (4)

the recommended dose for pediatric patients
with AML or MDS is 10 g/m /dose of treosulfan based on a full extrapolation of efficacy from
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and PopPK approach.
2

In summary, MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was a positive study based on its primary endpoint, and the
overall results demonstrate the efficacy of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan as a component of
conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. Treosulfan-based preparative regimens prior to
allogeneic HSCT in patients with AML or MDS result in a survival advantage (HR 0.67 [95% CI:
0.51, 0.90]) compared to busulfan-based preparative regimens. The recommended indication
for treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) is: “for use in combination with fludarabine as a preparative
regimen for allogeneic HSCT in adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and older with AML
(1.1) … [or]… MDS (1.2).”

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
AML and MDS have high rates of morbidity and mortality, and allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) is required for
curative therapy in patients with high-risk disease. Currently, there are no drugs approved for use as a preparative regimen prior to allogeneic
HSCT for AML or MDS, and regimens are not standardized. There is a need for additional conditioning regimen options for patients with AML
and MDS proceeding to allogeneic HSCT.
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (NCT00822393) was a multicenter, randomized, active-controlled trial comparing treosulfan to busulfan in combination
with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old with AML in complete
remission or MDS, Karnofsky performance status > 60%, and hematopoietic cell transplantation comorbidity index [HCT CI] score > 2 for
patients < 50 years of age. Patients were randomized to receive treosulfan 10 g/m² daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6
hours on days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and hematopoietic stem cell
transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as well to patients with unrelated donors.
The study was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of event-free survival (EFS) with 2 years of follow-up after
transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death. The noninferiority margin on the hazard ratio scale was 1.3. If
noninferiority was demonstrated, then the primary endpoint would be tested for superiority. Overall survival (OS) was a secondary endpoint,
but there was no alpha allocated to testing OS.
Study MC FludT.14/L Trial II was considered a positive study based on the primary endpoint of EFS (non-inferiority p-value = 0.0000164 as
compared to an efficacy boundary of 0.000149). Based on FDA-adjudication, EFS showed a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.65 (95% CI: 0.50, 0.85) in the
ITT population. However, EFS as defined by the Applicant reflects a composite of efficacy (relapse and death) and safety (graft failure)
measures, and the clinical meaningfulness of this definition is unclear for both the AML and MDS populations. Additional analyses of EFS and
related endpoints (including RFS) were conducted to explore the robustness of the result to definition and population. The results of these
(b) (4)
additional analyses consistently favored the treosulfan arm which supported the conclusion of a positive trial,
Although Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was not designed to test OS, this endpoint is the gold standard for establishing efficacy in oncology.
Therefore, FDA relied on OS, defined as the time from randomization until death from any cause, to establish the efficacy of treosulfan. The HR
for OS (Cox proportional hazards model, stratified by donor type and risk group) compared to busulfan was 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90) in the ITT
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

13

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
population, 0.73 (95% CI: 0.51, 1.06) in patients with AML, and 0.64 (95% CI: 0.40, 1.02) in patients with MDS. Despite the limitations of the
study design and available data, the results appear consistent with superiority overall. However, since there was no prespecified alphaspending plan for OS, no p-values will be reported.
Adverse events with treosulfan were similar to those observed with busulfan-based conditioning. Common TEAEs (≥20%) included
musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection, and vomiting. Of those, musculoskeletal pain (12% higher), edema (11%
higher), and infection (5% higher) occurred at a rate at least 5% higher in the treosulfan arm than the busulfan arm. The most common grade 35 AEs (≥5%) in the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia, infection, hypertension, stomatitis, and musculoskeletal pain. Of those, infection
occurred at a rate of >5% compared to the busulfan arm (6% higher in the treosulfan arm).
MC-FludT.14/L Trial I which randomized patients to treosulfan-based conditioning using 14 g/m2/dose vs busulfan-based conditioning was
terminated early due to delayed hematopoietic recovery and increase early treatment-related mortality (10.2% vs 5.3% in the treosulfan and
busulfan arms, respectively). FDA's analysis of the final data cut (1-year follow-up) showed no evidence of a treatment effect for this higher
treosulfan dose compared to the busulfan-based regimen for the overall population (OS HR 1.15 [95% CI: 0.78, 1.69]) or when assessed
individually for patients with AML or with MDS. The results of this study also confirmed that higher doses of treosulfan result in increased early
morbidity and mortality in addition to higher transplant-related and non-relapse mortality. These findings warrant inclusion of a Warning and
Precaution in the USPI since the 14 g/m2 dose is published in the literature and approved for the pediatric population in some countries.
Based on the biology of AML and MDS and the mechanism of action of treosulfan, the efficacy of treosulfan 10 g/m2/dose can be extrapolated
from the studies in adults to pediatric patients, and safety data for treosulfan in combination with fludarabine are available for patients ages 1
(b) (4)
year and older.
the recommended dose for pediatric patients with AML or MDS
is 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan based on a full extrapolation of efficacy from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and Population PK approach.
Given the observed OS benefit and with risk mitigation strategies in place in labeling, the clinical benefit of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan in
combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT appears to outweigh the risks in adult and pediatric patients 1 year
of age or older with AML or MDS.

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
Treosulfan is not currently marketed in the United States and has no history of being marketed
in the United States.

Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
Please refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021)
for summary of presubmission/submission regulatory activity prior to July 2021.
•

NDA 214759 received a Complete Response Letter on 7/30/2021- FDA noted that the
Applicant had not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of treosulfan as part of a
preparative regimen for allogenic hematopoietic stem cell transplant. FDA was unable to
confirm the primary endpoint of EFS because all complete blood cell (CBC) and bone
marrow results were not submitted to support adjudication of the endpoint. Additionally,
because of the missing CBC and marrow results, FDA was not able to complete an
assessment of whether the results of the study would support the indications for treatment
of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS. To address this issue, FDA
asked the Applicant to submit the CBC and marrow results through the complete
(b) (4)
assessment period for EFS for MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. FDA also noted that
OS cannot be used as the sole basis for approval since the
statistical analysis plan for Study MC-FLudT-14/L trial II neither include Type 1 error for OS
nor alpha allocation for multiple OS analyses. Since no alpha was reserved for independent
OS analysis and the EFS endpoint was unable to be verified due to lack of CBC and marrow
data noted above, FDA concluded that without verification of the EFS analysis, any OS
analysis could only be considered exploratory. To independently address the issues with OS,
FDA recommended submitting data from an adequate and well-controlled trial of treosulfan
vs busulfan with a prespecified analysis of OS for each indication with control of Type 1
error.

•

Type A Meeting 11/23/2021- FDA and the Applicant discussed the Applicant’s plan to
evaluate and present additional bone marrow and CBC results gathered through the
complete assessment period for EFS via the MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. FDA again asked for
clarification for how missing assessments were handled. FDA also noted that there is no
acceptable definition for EFS in MDS and that RFS more accurately reflects the AML patient’s
clinical circumstances. Discussion was also had on clinical studies to address clinical
pharmacology issues include QTc assessments.

•

Response to CR submitted 4/21/2022- Applicant submitted updated laboratory and
chimerism data with sensitivity analyses aimed at addressing missing assessment data

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Excerpted from FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
“From a pharmacology/ toxicology perspective, there are no outstanding issues that would
prevent the approval of treosulfan in patients undergoing allogeneic hematopoietic stem
(b) (4)
cell transplantation
with AML or MDS; however, there is one nonclinical postmarketing requirement (PMR).”
“During the review of the NDA, it was determined that the nonclinical data for treosulfan
submitted to the NDA in studies or literature for which the Applicant has the right to
reference did not provide convincing evidence of DNA alkylation and breaks as the
mechanism of action (MOA) of treosulfan. The PMR is to conduct an in vitro study to show
treosulfan binds to DNA and causes DNA breakage. As the recommendation has been a
complete response (see Section 1.2), the pharmacology/toxicology discipline recommends
the Applicant conduct and submit the results of the study at the time of resubmission.”
Excerpted from FDA Pharmacology/Toxicology Review of NDA 214759 (11 October 2024):
“To address this issue, the Applicant conducted genetic toxicology studies, and submitted
the results to demonstrate the genotoxicity of treosulfan. Genetic toxicology studies
included an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay and an in vitro chromosomal
aberration assay.” “Overall, the study results showed that treosulfan is a genotoxic
compound.”
See reviews referenced above for additional details including review conclusions for
Sections 5.2 to 5.5.

and diepoxide metabolites … are not meaningfully metabolized by CYP enzymes. Of
note, the t1/2 values for positive control compounds for the respective rCYPs were very
short. No labeling update is needed based on these results.”

8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II
Trial Design
Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was a phase III open-label, randomized clinical trial to compare
treosulfan-based conditioning therapy with busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning (RIC)
prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with AML or MDS
considered ineligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. The first patient was enrolled on
13-Jun-2014 and the last patient completed 25-Jan-2018.
Per statistical analysis plan, “MC-FludT.14/L was designed as a multicentre, multinational,
randomised, active-controlled, group-sequential phase III trial to show non-inferiority of
Treosulfan-based conditioning therapy compared to Busulfan-based reduced-intensity
conditioning (RIC) prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with
Acute Myeloid Leukaemia (AML) or Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) considered ineligible to
standard conditioning regimens.”
The study was designed as a non-inferiority trial with a primary endpoint of event-free survival
(EFS) within 2 years. The study population consisted of adult patients with AML or MDS who
were not eligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. Patients were randomized to receive
either reduced-intensity conditioning with busulfan and fludarabine, or the treosulfan and
fludarabine.
Treatment Plan Overview
Patients were randomized to receive either RIC with the control (reference) arm of busulfan +
fludarabine, or the test arm of treosulfan + fludarabine (Table 2).
Table 2: Treatment Overview of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
All patients received fludarabine (30 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -2. Patients on the control arm
received busulfan (0.8 mg/kg) on days -4 and -3. Patients receiving busulfan received antiseizure prophylaxis. Patients on the treatment arm received treosulfan (10 mg/m2/d) on days -4
to -2. Additionally, patients who received a MUD received ATG. The type of ATG and dosing
varied by country.
GVHD prophylaxis was identical in both arms. All patients received level-adapted cyclosporine
from Day -1 through Day +100 and methotrexate on days +1, +3, and +6, with folic acid rescue.
Please refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021)
for further details regarding the study design.
Study Endpoints
Please refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021)
for the full list of endpoints and results. This review focuses on the assessment of the eventfree survival (primary endpoint) and the overall survival.
Selected Protocol-Specified Endpoints
Primary Endpoint: Event-Free Survival (within 24 months post-transplantation)
Per the statistical analysis plan, the primary efficacy endpoint was “event-free survival (EFS)
within 2 years after transplantation measured from time of end of HSCT (= day 0) to time of
event. The associated time span is defined as the interval from day 0 to relapse, graft failure or
death (whatever occurs first). The date of the event is either the documented date of
relapse/progression or the date of death or the date of primary (date of examination of
neutrophilic granulocytes) or secondary graft failure (date of examination of neutrophilic
granulocytes).”
Statistical Reviewer’s Comment
The following key issues were identified with event-free survival, as specified in the statistical
analysis plan:
1. Day Zero: Transplantation (HSCT). The period of regulatory interest starts from
randomization.
2. Timeframe: Within 24 months. The period of regulatory interest is not restricted to 24
months.
3. Events: Sufficient data to confirm the outcomes were missing in the initial submissions.
FDA adjudicated the events with the additional submitted data. See the Additional
Endpoints per FDA Assessment section below for further details.
4. Variables used in the Cox-proportional hazards model: risk group, donor type, center.
Including center is not appropriate (see the Statistical Issues section).

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

30

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
5. Interpretation: Interpretation of the treatment effect based on event-free survival is
challenging. The Applicant utilized a definition for EFS in this trial that was not standard
and had not been discussed with FDA prior to the study. For AML studies, FDA has
accepted an EFS endpoint that represent the time from randomization until the time of
an event. Events are considered induction treatment failure, relapse for those who have
induction treatment success, or death. The eligibility criteria for this study required
patients with AML to be in remission prior to transplant. Therefore, for the AML patients
a Relapse-Free Survival (RFS) endpoint would have more accurately reflected the events
within this clinical context. This analysis was requested as discussed below. Additional
issues with the Applicant’s AML EFS event definition were the inclusion of primary or
secondary graft failure as events and the inclusion of cytogenetic and molecular relapse
events without evidence of morphological relapse.
EFS is not an established clinical benefit endpoint for new drugs for MDS and has not
been used for regulatory purposes in MDS. As with the AML patients, the Applicant’s
MDS EFS event definition included the use of primary or secondary graft failure as
events, which is a nonstandard EFS event. Moreover, the MDS EFS definition did not
include treatment failure as an event even though most MDS patients were not in
remission (and were not required to be) at that time of transplant. Additionally, patients
were not formally assessed for response after transplant with post-transplant
assessments focusing on engraftment instead. Therefore, the interpretation of relapse
events for the MDS patient was unclear since the patients were not formally assessed for
remission.
Issues 1-4 were addressed by FDA by defining a different endpoint: event-free survival (from
randomization). See the Additional Endpoints per FDA Assessment section below for further
details.
Overall Survival (within 24 months post-transplantation)
Per statistical analysis plan, overall survival was defined as “the time length between day 0 and
the date of death due to any cause within the 24-month study period (i.e. excluding postsurveillance evaluation).”
Day Zero: Transplantation (HSCT)
Timeframe: within 24 months
Statistical Reviewer’s Comment
The following key issues were identified with the overall survival, as specified in the statistical
analysis plan:
1. Day Zero: Transplantation (HSCT). The period of regulatory interest starts from
randomization.

Additional Endpoints per FDA Assessment
This section lists additional endpoints used for regulatory consideration, as defined and
adjudicated by FDA.
Event-Free Survival (post-transplantation)

the primary endpoint with several distinctions:
Day Zero: Transplantation
Timeframe: Unrestricted
Events: Adjudicated by the FDA
Event-Free Survival (post-randomization)
We refer to the EFS (post-randomization) herein as the endpoint with the same definition as
the primary endpoint with several distinctions:
Day Zero: Randomization
Timeframe: Unrestricted
Events: Adjudicated by the FDA
This was considered as the primary endpoint for review purposes. The FDA EFS adjudication
process is summarized below.
•
•
•
•
•

All EFS events were adjudicated as defined in the protocol including use of the protocol
definitions of relapse for AML, “relapse” of MDS (including relapse and progression),
and graft failure (including primary and secondary GF).
Patients without a prior event were censored at last follow up.
The timing of events from PARAMCD=EFSSBITT in the updated adtte.xpt data file was
compared to the source documentation.
Relapse was confirmed based on the laboratory, bone marrow, and source
documentation data resulting in updates to either the time of relapse or the occurrence
of relapse for some patients.
Review of the updated bone marrow data identified subjects whose baseline bone
marrow results indicated bone marrow blast counts above the inclusion criterion for
either AML or MDS. These patients were censored at randomization.

Grafapex (treosulfan)
Relapse-Free Survival (AML)
RFS was not an original trial endpoint. However, RFS is an acceptable regulatory endpoint in
studies for treatments of patients with AML in remission. FDA defines RFS, for the purposes of
this review, as the time from randomization to the date of morphologic relapse or death from
any cause, whichever occurs first. In the study population with AML:
•
•
•

FDA adjudicated baseline remission status based on laboratory and bone marrow results
as described above for EFS.
The start date was revised to be date of randomization rather than end of HSCT.
Censoring was applied at the time of events not included in FDA’s definition of RFS (i.e.,
graft failure, cytogenetic relapse, molecular relapse) due to lack of consistent follow-up
and documentation of morphologic relapse after these events had been called.

Overall Survival (post-randomization)
For the regulatory consideration purposes, the overall survival is defined as the time from
randomization until death due to any cause (not limited by a 24-month period). We refer to this
FDA-defined endpoint as overall survival (post-randomization) herein.
Day Zero: Randomization
Timeframe: Unrestricted
This is the main endpoint of interest from the regulatory perspective. No alpha was allocated,
and no testing boundary was set for overall survival hypothesis testing. Please refer to the FDA
Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for further details.
Analysis Populations
The protocol-specified analysis population for the primary analysis of EFS was the full analysis
set (FAS), defined as all randomized patients who were treated at least one time with the study
medication and with at least one documented efficacy parameter. Herein we will refer to all
randomized patients as the intention-to-treat (ITT) analysis set.
Statistical Analysis Plan
Please refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
Per protocol, the primary analysis of EFS utilized a Cox model “(stratified by centre) with donor
type (MUD vs. MRD), prognostic risk group (I vs. II) and treatment as factors”. The primary
analysis was intended to test non-inferiority based on EFS, with a non-inferiority margin of
HR=1.3. Study MC-FLudT1.4 Trial II was designed with three interim analyses for efficacy and
futility, planned to occur at 9.4%, 28.5%, and 49.7% information fraction (described in further
detail in Section 8.1.2. The associated boundaries were based on the primary analysis of EFS,
such that the trial would be stopped if efficacy or futility was met for the NI test of EFS.
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

33

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
If the test of non-inferiority for EFS was found to be significant, a test of superiority would be
conducted. Per protocol, “In case of statistical significance, superiority with respect to the
primary study endpoint will be tested based on the points to consider on switching between
superiority and noninferiority (CPMP/EWP/482/99).” This corresponds to a test of superiority at
the alpha level utilized for the NI test.
As noted above, the statistical analysis plan included center as one of the stratification factors.
For the review purposes, this factor was excluded from the models used to assess additional
endpoints per FDA assessment due to the excessive number of levels (See the Statistical Issues
section for details).
Statistical Reviewer’s Comment: The 30 July 2021 FDA Multidisciplinary Review noted issues
with the report submitted to justify the NI margin (inconsistency of EFS definition across studies,
inconsistency of trial populations, lack of identification of a trial with a population exchangeable
with Study MC-FludT1.4 Trial II). Furthermore, issues concerning the relevance of EFS in the
(b) (4)
enrolled population are detailed above.
Protocol Amendments
Please refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
Study Results
Preamble:
The following is a brief summary of the Study MC-FludT.14/L (Trial II) background, as described
in the Clinical Study Report (v1.0, 5 March 2020):
“The trial was planned as a group-sequential trial with 3 interim analyses. The first formal
interim analysis was planned to be performed after 45 events or 220 patients to allow for a
broad review of the benefits and risks of the dose reduction and change of the treatment
regimen implemented with amendment 3 of the trial. Stopping early due to proof of efficacy or
futility was unlikely within this interim analysis due to the low information fraction within this
analysis. Further interim analyses were planned after 137 and 239 events occurred, or after 460
and 700 patients were randomised. The final analysis was planned after 481 events or inclusion
of 930 patients at the latest.
When reviewing the results of the second interim analysis, the DMC recommended to stop
recruitment for this trial since the primary objective – the proof of non-inferiority of treosulfan
compared to busulfan – had already been achieved. The previous CTR (V 1.0 dated 15-Sep2017) described the results from the 476 patients available at that time and constitutes the
confirmatory analysis of this trial. This updated report describes the results of the last analysis

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

34

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
of all 570 patients enrolled in the trial including post surveillance data. Of these, 551 patients
qualified for the FAS. In total, 234 events were observed representing an information fraction
of 48.6% based on the 481 events planned for in the final analysis foreseen in the Clinical Trial
Protocol if the trial had not been stopped prematurely.”
FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) identified the
following issues with the submitted data, among others, which resulted in a Complete
Response:
•

•

“The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS).
Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were
unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of
EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.
Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able
complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the
indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS.”

This review focuses on the evaluation of efficacy in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. The evaluation
of efficacy relied on the additional data submitted with the application to address the
deficiencies identified in the Complete Response. The FDA adjudication of the submitted data is
summarized in Section 8: Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation.
Study MC-FludT-14/L Trial II was stopped at the second interim analysis with 168 EFS events
observed in the FAS population. A total of n=476 patients were randomized at this time; n=460
were included in the FAS. In a Type C meeting with FDA convened on 17 Sep 2019, FDA
conveyed concerns regarding the low information fraction associated with the second interim
analysis. FDA recommended the interim and final analyses be submitted for review. Following
this meeting, the Applicant submitted a final CSR, dated 05 March 2020. The date of the last
patient completed was 25 Jan 2018 in this report.

The key questions that this review of Study MC-FludT/14/Trial II is aiming to address are the
following:
1. Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II reached its primary objective based on the event-free
survival outcome, as adjudicated by the FDA?
2. Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrated an advantage on overall survival and
other efficacy outcomes for the treosulfan study group over the busulfan study group?
3. Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrated favorable benefit-risk for the proposed
indications for patients with AML and for patients with MDS?

Grafapex (treosulfan)
EFS Adjudication
As noted above, the Applicant defined EFS as the time from transplant until an event of disease
relapse, graft failure, or death, whichever occurs first. Disease relapse for AML was considered
morphologic relapse, cytogenetic relapse, or molecular relapse. Disease relapse/progression for
MDS was considered recurrence of marrow or peripheral blasts >5% if they were >5% at study
entry and the patient had a marrow CR after transplant, reappearance of cytogenetic
abnormalities with a ≥ 5% decline in donor chimerism, or reduction in peripheral counts
(decrease of ≥ 50 % from maximum response level (after engraftment) in granulocytes or
platelets or reduction in HgB concentration by ≥ 1.5 g/dl from maximum response level (after
engraftment) or transfusion dependency) accompanied by absolute decline in donor chimerism
by ≥ 5 %. The Applicant included either primary graft failure or secondary graft failure as an EFS
event. During review of the initial submission, FDA attempted to verify if Study MC-FludT.14/L
Trial II met its primary endpoint as planned, completed, and reported by the Applicant. The
clinical team noted that they required more comprehensive laboratory data and bone marrow
data to complete an adjudication of the primary EFS endpoint as defined by the Applicant. Since
this information was not initially collected as part of the study, the Applicant had to request the
missing information from the individual study sites.
The Applicant conducted an additional database lock on 16 Mar 2018 and characterized all EFS
events observed (regardless of timing). Once collected, the Applicant utilized an external
hematologist to adjudicate the EFS events based on the newly collected data (Table 5 below).
FDA review noted two subjects with missed events. One patient had a missed secondary graft
failure event that went on to receive a second transplant who had been previously censored at
last follow up without an event. The other patient had a missed late relapse that had been
previously censored at last follow up. The remaining six adjustments noted during the
Applicant’s review of the new data were to update the timing of relapse events based on the
first evidence of relapse in the source documentation.

With this current submission, FDA was able to review the source documentation for labs and
bone marrow reports assembled by the Applicant and adjudicate EFS per the Applicant’s
definition. This included screening laboratory data for evidence of relapse or graft failure events

follow-up showing the patient was well and without relapse almost a year after the previously
reported date. This patient was changed to censored at last follow up. These adjudications
were submitted to the Applicant who agreed with FDA’s justifications for the updates.

Event-Free Survival (within 24 months post-transplantation)
The hazard ratio for EFS, as performed at the interim analysis in the FAS population with 168
events was 0.65 (95% CI: 0.47, 0.90). The p-value for non-inferiority testing was 0.0000164,
which was significant as compared to the efficacy boundary of 0.000149. The p-value for
superiority testing was 0.0051268, which did not cross the efficacy boundary. This endpoint had
224 events at the last analysis, which would correspond to a 46.6% information fraction. The
efficacy boundary based on this information fraction would have been 0.00097. The hazard
ratio for EFS, as defined in the protocol and performed at the last analysis, within 2 years posttransplantation, using Cox proportional hazards model with risk group and donor type, was 0.64
(95% CI: 0.49, 0.83), p-value (one-sided) for superiority was 0.0004 (efficacy stopping boundary
was 0.00097).
Statistical Reviewer’s Comment: The 224 events corresponding to the “last” analysis are based
on additional events observed for the primary endpoint (EFS within 24 months posttransplantation). While this endpoint met the superiority boundary at this time, this does not
account for the previous analysis, though the alpha spent at the prior analyses was small. This
boundary is hypothetical – it corresponds to a trial which would have conducted an interim
analysis at this time. This analysis was not planned but requested by FDA. While the boundary is
useful for providing context for the p-value, it does not represent the traditional boundary
utilized for demonstrating efficacy.
Event-Free Survival (post-transplantation)

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

41

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
FDA conducted additional analyses on EFS to assess its robustness to 1) adjudication of events
and 2) the 2-year analysis window. These analyses use data from the final CSR, which includes
events occurring after the 2-year analysis window. The analyses that follow do not use site as a
stratification factor, as this resulted in a high number of strata (site/center factor has 22 levels).
In the final CSR date 16 Mar 2018, the Applicant reported the hazard ratio for EFS (posttransplantation), unrestricted to any period, as 0.65 (95% CI: 0.49, 0.84). With FDA adjudication,
the hazard ratio for EFS (post-transplantation), unrestricted to any period, was 0.65 (95% CI:
0.50, 0.85), p-value (one-sided) was 0.0007 (Table 9).
Table 9 shows the summaries. These EFS events include post-surveillance events, i.e., those
which occur beyond 2 years post-HSCT. Per the final CSR, this represents “an information
fraction of 48.6% based on the 481 events planned for in the final analysis foreseen in the
Clinical Trial Protocol if the trial had not been stopped prematurely. The information fraction of
48.6% corresponds to an event-driven alpha level of 0.001262 within this last analysis according
to the O`Brien-Fleming α-spending function.” The database lock for the last analysis occurred
on 16 March 2018.

reported at the interim analysis was 0.61 (95% CI: 0.42, 0.88) with 82/240 (34%) of events in the

included in the interim CSR.

last analysis was 0.63 (95% CI: 0.47, 0.86) with 107/283 (38%) of events in the busulfan group
and 72/268 (27%) events and in the treosulfan group (adjusted for donor type and risk group as
stratification factors). Seventy-three percent (95% CI: 67%, 78%) of patients in the treosulfan

Clinical Reviewer’s Comment: As shown above, the adjudicated EFS results analyzed by FDA
either within 24 months of transplant or with an unrestricted timeframe resulted in results
which were consistent with the per protocol definition and analysis of EFS. While Study MC
FludT.14/L Trial II met its primary analysis of EFS, the use of an EFS endpoint that comprised
(b) (4)
nonstandard EFS events of unclear clinical benefit made these results unsuitable
. It
is important to note that a properly defined EFS endpoint could support findings of efficacy for
AML with active disease (U.S. Food and Drug Administration, 2022). However, since the patients
with AML enrolled on Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were in remission, an RFS endpoint would
better capture the clinical benefit in this context (because failing to achieve remission was not a
risk for these patients and is a key event for an EFS endpoint). FDA analyzed RFS for patients
with AML in an exploratory analysis detailed below. For patients with MDS, while recent criteria
propose a definition for EFS in MDS, the clinical meaningfulness of these definitions have not
been established. Available data indicate that CR remains the response endpoint associated
with the greatest long-term benefit, and a definition of EFS that captures failure to achieve
remission might be an acceptable endpoint. However, the protocol EFS definition did not include
failure to achieve remission as a component of treatment failure and the frequency of CBC and
bone marrow data collection did not allow for reliable assessment of remission status. Without
this remission assessment, it is less clear how to interpret subsequent disease
relapse/progression events. Ultimately, the OS best captured clinical benefit of treosulfan as
discussed below.

Clinical Reviewer’s Comment on MRD vs MUD: As noted in the analysis above and during the
initial review of the data, the benefit demonstrated in the OS endpoint appears to be driven by
the patients utilizing a MUD for transplant. It is not clear why this might have been observed,
but it is most likely that this apparent difference is a chance finding since the imbalance in the
number of events was small. Clinically, patients receiving a transplant from a MUD versus MRD
have differing competing risks post-transplant of GVHD, infection, and relapse that are all
affected by the different donor type. There will be a higher risk of GVHD with MUD but greater
chance of a graft versus leukemia effect (lower relapse chance). Moreover, the need for antithymocyte globulin (ATG) to be added to the transplant conditioning regimen of MUD
transplants to reduce the higher risk of GVHD is at the expense of increased infectious risk and
the potential for a decreased graft versus leukemia effect. Within these competing and varying
risks, it is unclear how the use of treosulfan versus busulfan would have further altered these
risks to only benefit the patients receiving a MUD transplant. However, it is important to

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

52

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
consider that the trial was not designed to test treosulfan against a placebo control; rather it
was designed to show the efficacy of treosulfan as an alkylating agent used in conditioning
compared to an active control arm of busulfan conditioning.

Statistical Reviewer’s Comment: These results suggest that the positive result observed at the
interim analysis was not driven by either the AML or MDS populations.
Subgroup Analyses: Relapse-Free Survival (AML Population)
FDA conducted an ad hoc analysis of RFS in patients with AML. As noted above, a properly
defined EFS endpoint could support findings of efficacy for either AML or MDS. However, since
patients with AML enrolled on Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were in remission, an RFS endpoint
better captures the potential benefit in this clinical context (because failing to achieve
remission, which is a key event for an EFS endpoint, was not a risk for patients already in
remission). Additionally, the Applicant’s inclusion of graft failure as an EFS event complicates
interpretation of the EFS endpoint. Graft failure is assessed in all trials concerning HSCT;
however, graft failure is considered a safety issue since this is a complication of the transplant
procedure.
RFS events for AML were adjudicated by FDA based on the EFS adjudications noted above. RFS
was defined as the time from randomization until morphologic relapse or death from any
cause, whichever comes first. The Applicant included three criteria for AML relapse in their EFS
definition: hematologic, cytogenetic, and molecular. However, only hematologic relapse criteria
are fit for regulatory considerations. Moreover, investigation of the data showed that little
follow up data was available for patients once an event of molecular or cytogenetic relapse was
called. Similar issues with lack of follow up data were noted for patients with primary or
secondary graft failure. Therefore, to achieve the EFS to RFS adjudication in the AML
population, FDA censored patients at the time of molecular or cytogenetic relapse and primary
or secondary graft failure since follow up data for morphological relapse was not routinely
collected after these events were called. This is shown conceptually in Table 15 and with
detailed patient adjudications in Table 16 below. The Applicant agreed with FDA adjudications
for the AML RFS analysis.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
8.1.3 Integrated Review of Effectiveness
Methods
The Applicant proposed an indication for treosulfan "for use in combination with fludarabine as
a preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult
and pediatric patients > 1 year old with acute myeloid leukemia (AML)” and “…with
myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS).” The proposed dose of treosulfan was of 10 g/m2/dose for
(b) (4)
use in the combination described.

The clinical development program in AML and MDS included two randomized control trials,
three single-arm trials, one dose-escalation study, and two registry studies (See Table 35 in FDA
Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021)). All clinical trials were
conducted outside the US. The pivotal trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was relevant to determining
efficacy in the intended population. In addition, MC-FludT.14/L Trial I and MC-FludT.6/L were
used to support the proposed 10 g/m2 treosulfan dose. Other studies included in the
submission were reviewed but were not considered to have contributed to assessment of
efficacy for the proposed indications. See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA
214759 (30 July 2021) for full list of studies and limitations of those studies.
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II
Overview
The Applicant proposed to use Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Trial 14 - II; treosulfan 10
g/m2/dose) to support the proposed indication. Trial 14 - II was a multicenter, randomized,
active-controlled trial comparing treosulfan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a
preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old
with AML or MDS, Karnofsky performance status > 60%, and age ≥ 50 years or hematopoietic
cell transplantation comorbidity index [HCT CI] score > 2. The patients were randomized to
receive treosulfan 10 g/m² daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours on
days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and
hematopoietic stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as
well to patients with unrelated donors.
In general, a randomized controlled trial with a survival-based endpoint is appropriate to
support an indication for a drug in a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. FDA frequently
requires two trials to determine efficacy, but one trial might be sufficient if the trial is
adequately designed and well-conducted, and the results are internally consistent, clinically
meaningful, and statistically persuasive.

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

59

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Overview of Issues with Data Collection
The following issues regarding data collection were noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review
and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
•

“The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS).
Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were
unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of
EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.

•

Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able
complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the
indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS.”

Additional data were submitted by the Applicant in the current resubmission. FDA adjudicated
time-to-event outcomes based on these data. Relapse was confirmed based on the laboratory,
bone marrow, and source documentation data resulting in updates to either the time of relapse
or the occurrence of relapse for some patients.
The extent of the submitted data is limited. Per Complete Response to the FDA Complete
Response Letter 30 July 2021, page 10: "… the collection of some of the marrow data was
considered not mandatory under the study protocol MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, particularly beyond
Day +100 and in case of lack of an event. However, in some instances, bone marrow was
collected beyond Day +100 and details of these assessments are provided." Given the lack of
reliable data collection beyond Day 100 (and “in case of lack of an event”), outcomes relying on
such data beyond Day 100 might be challenging to interpret.
Overview of Issues with Trial Design
FDA noted several issues with the design of Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. These include:
• Inclusion of two distinct clinical populations, MDS and AML, without a pre-specified plan
to formally test each subpopulation.
• The definition of the primary endpoint EFS, which included non-efficacy events such as
graft failure and whose applicability to the AML and MDS subpopulations is unclear.
• The primary objective of non-inferiority for EFS, with a NI margin lacking sufficient
justification.
• Unusual choices for the primary analysis, including use of an analysis population of FAS
rather than the ITT and inclusion of a high number of stratification levels (due to
inclusion of site as a stratification factor).
• Lack of a pre-specified testing plan for OS.
• Multiple early interim analyses for efficacy which limited the overall available
information on secondary endpoints.

The results show favorable outcomes for treosulfan compared to busulfan in the ITT population
across multiple efficacy endpoints as discussed below.
Primary Endpoint
Event-Free Survival
Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was stopped early for efficacy at 35% (168/481 planned events in
the FAS population) information fraction of EFS. Early stopping for efficacy might result in an
overestimation of a treatment effect. The hazard ratio for EFS, as performed at the interim
analysis was 0.65 (95% CI: 0.47, 0.90). The p-value for non-inferiority testing was 0.0000164,
which was significant as compared to the efficacy boundary of 0.000149. The p-value for
superiority testing was 0.0051268, which did not cross the efficacy boundary. Results for EFS at
the last analysis suggested the p-value would have crossed the corresponding superiority
boundary, though this analysis was ad-hoc and the corresponding boundary hypothetical in
nature.
Per the statistical analysis plan, the primary efficacy endpoint was “event-free survival (EFS)
within 2 years after transplantation measured from time of end of HSCT (= day 0) to time of
event. The associated time span is defined as the interval from day 0 to relapse, graft failure or
death (whatever occurs first). The date of the event is either the documented date of
relapse/progression or the date of death or the date of primary (date of examination of
neutrophilic granulocytes) or secondary graft failure (date of examination of neutrophilic
granulocytes).” This is not a standard definition used for regulatory considerations in AML or
MDS populations. Per FDA's Acute Myeloid Leukemia: Developing Drugs and Biological Products
for Treatment Guidance for Industry, EFS is defined as the "time from randomization to the
date of: induction treatment failure, relapse for those who have induction treatment success
(e.g., complete remission), or death from any cause, whichever comes first". Interpretation of
the results based on the protocol-defined EFS outcome may be challenging.
Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II reached its primary objective based on the event-free
survival outcome, as adjudicated by the FDA?

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

62

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II reached its primary objective of demonstrating that EFS (posttransplantation, within 2 years) outcomes were non-inferior on the treosulfan arm compared to
the busulfan arm. Data were submitted in support of adjudication of this endpoint for this
submission. After adjudication, FDA found that the results for EFS as originally reported were
largely consistent with those that were adjudicated.
Results were similar for other definitions of EFS. The hazard ratio for EFS (post-randomization,
without time restriction) was 0.65 (95% CI: 0.50, 0.85), indicative of a positive treatment effect
of treosulfan compared to busulfan.
As noted above, EFS as defined per protocol includes both safety and efficacy events and is not
aligned with FDA guidance. Furthermore, FDA guidance for the treatment of AML states that
EFS may be considered as an efficacy endpoint in populations not in remission, but that RFS is
the appropriate for populations that are in remission at baseline. As the AML subpopulation
was in remission at baseline and the MDS subpopulation was not, any conceptual “event-free”
survival endpoint would likely differ for these two subpopulations. Therefore, EFS results in the
(b) (4)
enrolled population has no clear interpretation.
Secondary and Other Endpoints
Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was stopped at the second interim analysis based on a significant
test of EFS non-inferiority. While superiority of EFS was an alpha-controlled, secondary
objective, the associated statistical test did not meet significance at the second interim analysis.
Furthermore, OS was not specified as an endpoint to be tested. For trials with important
secondary objectives, a common design is one which continues to follow for the secondary
objective even if the primary objective is met. This approach was not utilized in Study MCFludT.14/L Trial II. Thus, the results of secondary endpoints must be considered in light of these
limitations.
Overall Survival
For Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II , the analysis of OS was considered exploratory. FDA assessed
OS in the ITT population with using the full follow-up period. No alpha-controlled hypothesis
testing was prespecified for the overall survival or for testing AML and MDS subgroups. Not
controlling for multiplicity might result in erroneous conclusions.
Per statistical analysis plan, OS is defined as “the time length between day 0 and the date of
death due to any cause within the 24-month study period (i.e. excluding post-surveillance
evaluation).” Day zero is the time of allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. This is
not a standard OS definition used for regulatory purposes, which typically uses 1)
randomization as the start of the period of interest 2) has no restriction on follow-up (i.e., no
limit set to 24 months). Since the OS is both safety and efficacy endpoint, the regulatory
interest is in the full unrestricted survival period starting from randomization, referred to OS
(post-randomization) herein.
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

63

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrated an advantage on overall survival and other
efficacy outcomes for the treosulfan study group over the busulfan study group?
Table 19 shows hazard ratios and 95% confidence intervals for the OS in the ITT population as
well as in AML and MDS populations separately. These results are based on the final CSR with
database lock 16 Mar 2018. Based on the reverse Kaplan-Meier estimate, median follow-up
time for OS (post-randomization) was 29.6 months (range: 0, 54.5) in the busulfan group and
29.5 months (range: 0, 52.5) in the treosulfan group. The hazard ratio for OS (postrandomization) was 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90) in the ITT population, demonstrating an OS
advantage in the treosulfan group compared to the busulfan group. The upper bounds of the
95% confidence intervals slightly exceed one in the AML and in the MDS subgroups, suggesting
that some residual uncertainty regarding efficacy in these subgroups remains. Nonetheless, the
results for patients with AML and MDS are consistent with the overall results of the study.
In Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, treatment with treosulfan-based conditioning resulted in
improved OS compared to busulfan-based conditioning in the ITT population. Since FDA verified
that Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was a positive study based on its EFS primary endpoint, FDA
could consider the OS endpoint as an acceptable endpoint for assessment of the efficacy of
treosulfan for use in transplant conditioning. Unlike EFS, OS was not subject to the same
interpretation issues based on the events included in the EFS definition and incomplete capture
of follow up monitoring. Moreover, since monitoring for OS is simpler, there are fewer concerns
for missed events compared to an EFS endpoint. The timing of OS events is unambiguous
compared to EFS events and because patients can be reliably censored at the time of last follow
up/known well without concern for a missed event occurring prior to that last follow up
encounter.
Overall survival is both safety and efficacy endpoint and remains the gold standard in clinical
trials. The results show a substantial treatment effect on the overall survival in the full study
population. However, alpha-controlled hypothesis testing was not prespecified. Therefore, pvalues should not be included in the USPI. To communicate the full survival functions in
treosulfan and busulfan groups in patients with AML and, separately, in patients with MDS,
including Kaplan-Meier plots in the USPI is recommended, along with the treatment effect point
estimates and confidence intervals.
Additional Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant prespecified several secondary endpoints discussed in the original review, but
these largely consisted of safety endpoints or endpoints that could not be used in support of
the OS results to demonstrate the effectiveness of treosulfan for treatment of AML or MDS.
For treatment of patients with AML transplanted in remission, RFS would be the usual endpoint
to support the OS findings. To investigate RFS in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, FDA reviewed the
available EFS data and determined how to appropriately convert the Applicant’s EFS analysis
into a traditional RFS analysis. This showed that the hazard ratio for RFS in the AML patients
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

64

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
was 0.72 (95% CI: 0.51, 1.03). This is also well-captured by the RFS at 24 months which showed
estimates of 59% (95% CI: 50%, 55%) versus 69% (95% CI: 61%, 75%) for busulfan and
treosulfan, respectively. This suggests that when focused on the RFS events of hematologic
relapse or death, that treatment with treosulfan showed a lower risk of these events compared
to treatment with busulfan further supporting the findings of efficacy in the AML population
demonstrated in the OS analysis.
For treatment of MDS not in remission, EFS (defined appropriately) or CR that is durable might
be suitable to assess activity. However, because CBC and marrow examination data were not
collected systematically to assess achievement of remission in this protocol, achievement of CR
and/or treatment failure as an event for EFS cannot be assessed.
Subpopulations
Has Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrated favorable benefit-risk for the proposed
indications for patients with AML and for patients with MDS?
Disease
FDA considers treatment of AML and MDS to be two distinct indications which would typically
warrant separate sources of substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval. Study
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II enrolled both populations with no plan to formally assess them
separately. In addition, the sample size for each subpopulation was not based on power
considerations or to ensure a certain number of patients in each subgroup. These features
limited the ability Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II to establish substantial evidence for either
disease alone.
However, FDA considered it may be reasonable to assess efficacy in a combined ITT population
of AML and MDS provided the endpoint used had equivalent interpretation for both diseases,
which the OS analysis provided. For trials with such mixed populations, it is critical that a
sufficient number of patients in each subpopulation be enrolled so that differential treatment
effects can be identified and described. This appears to have been the case for Study MCFludT.14/L Trial II, which enrolled 365 patients with AML and 205 patients with MDS.
As noted above, the definition and relevance of EFS to each setting is unclear. While the
treatment effect on EFS appears to be similar in the two subpopulations, FDA does not consider
this as direct evidence of effectiveness overall or for either subpopulation. FDA found the OS
results to be similar across the two populations, with a hazard ratio of 0.73 (95% CI: 0.51, 1.06)
for the AML population and 0.64 (95% CI: 0.40, 1.02) for the MDS population (Table 19). Taken
together, these observations suggest 1) that the positive result observed in Study MCFludT.14/L Trial II was not driven by either the AML or MDS subgroup and 2) that the observed
treatment effect on OS was not driven by either the AML or MDS subgroup.
Given these considerations, FDA considers Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II to have demonstrated
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
favorable benefit-risk for the proposed indications for patients with AML and for patients with
MDS.
Race and Ethnicity
Data on race and ethnicity were not collected for any of the studies included in this NDA. This
deficiency leaves some uncertainty about the generalizability of the efficacy outcomes with
respect to the US population. While the results from the European region of multiregional trials
have generally been similar to North American results (Wilson, J NCCN, 2022) and, based on
treosulfan’s mechanism of action as an alkylating agent, there is no reason to believe that the
benefit-risk profile demonstrated in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II would be expected to differ by
racial or ethnic background, the lack of racial or ethnic reporting in this application did not
allow for assessment of efficacy or safety results by race and ethnicity. Additional information
is needed to ensure there are no unexpected differences in the benefit-risk profile in US racial
and ethnic groups. To address this, the Applicant has agreed to assess the PK and exposure
profile and registry-based outcomes by race and ethnicity in PMC #1 and PMC #2, respectively.
Donor Type
In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, 76% of patients had unrelated donors and 24% had related donors.

As noted during the original review, the treatment effect appears to be driven by the

appears consistent whether they have AML or MDS. For patients with related donors, the HR is

basis for this observation is unclear. Higher HR estimates for the MRD group in the AML
subpopulation are likely due to a few influential events observed after two years of follow-up
(e.g., see Figure 8). Regarding the MRD group in the MDS subpopulation, since less than 10
events in each treatment group were observed in this subgroup, the results are likely unreliable
due to few observed events. There is insufficient information to assess the potential lack of
effectiveness. The results should be interpreted with caution.

Excerpted from FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
Study MC-FludT.6/L was a multicenter single-arm dose-escalation trial to test the tolerability of
treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched
sibling, 1 antigen-mismatched sibling, or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were
>= 18 years old with a "chemosensitive" hematological malignancy indicated for HSCT but who
were considered at high risk for toxicities with standard MAC regimens. Patients were treated
in sequential cohorts (n=15 each) at treosulfan dose levels of 10 gm/m2, 12 gm/m2, or 14
gm/m2. The aim of the protocol was to determine a dose and schedule for later trials. The
primary efficacy endpoint was hematopoietic recovery. Overall survival was an additional
endpoint.

treatment effect for the overall population or when assessed individually for patients with AML

The results suggest that the effectiveness for the treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2/day x 3 days in

Clinical TL Comment: Clinical evidence from Study MC-FludT.6/L support the benefit of
treosulfan for use as part of a preparative regimen. The 1-year OS outcomes for patients with
hematologic malignancies who received the treosulfan-based preparative regimen at 10
g/m2/dose were consistent with those in the treosulfan arm of the pivotal trial. Although FDA
does not usually use landmark OS in a single-arm trial to support efficacy due to concerns
regarding the potential for bias, the findings in MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible in the two lower
dose cohorts in the study (see Table 21). Additionally, the results of MC-FludT.6/L were
reproducible across studies (see Table 21 and Table 22): 1-year OS in the 10 g/m2 cohort of
MCFludT.6/L was consistent with that of the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and
similarly, 1-year OS in the 14 g/m2 cohort was consistent with the results in the treosulfan arm
of MC-FludT.14/L Trial I. Together, the consistency of these findings reduces concerns of bias
and increase the confidence that the observed OS outcomes in MC-FludT.6/L reflect the clinical
benefit of 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan as part of a preparative regimen. Furthermore, the results
of these studies also support a dose-dependent effect of treosulfan on OS, with worse OS seen
with the treosulfan 14 g/m2/dose compared to lower doses of treosulfan in MC-FludT.6/L and
compared to the busulfan regimen in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I.

8.1.4 Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
The results of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II demonstrate the efficacy of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan as a
component of a preparative regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. Patients not eligible for
myeloablative conditioning were randomized to either treosulfan-based or busulfan-based
reduced intensity conditioning. Patients who received treosulfan-based conditioning had longer
OS compared to patients who received busulfan-based conditioning (HR 0.67 [95% CI: 0.51,
0.90]). These findings are supported by clinical evidence from Study MC-FludT.6/L where the 1year OS outcome in patients who received the 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan-based preparative
regimen was similar to that in the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. Although FDA does
not usually use landmark OS in a single-arm trial to support efficacy given the potential for bias,
the OS findings in MC-FludT.6/L were reproducible in the two lower dose cohorts and were
consistent with the OS results for patients treated with treosulfan across two other trials. The
consistency of these findings reduces concerns for bias and supports the conclusion that the 10
g/m2/dose treosulfan is effective as part of a preparative regimen.
Extrapolation of efficacy to the pediatric population is reasonable based on the biology of AML
(b) (4)
and MDS and the mechanism of action of treosulfan as an alkylating agent.
the recommended
dose for pediatric patients with AML or MDS is 10 g/m /dose of treosulfan based on a full
(b) (4)
extrapolation of efficacy from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and Population PK approach.

Treosulfan-based preparative regimens prior to allogeneic HSCT in patients with AML or MDS
result in a survival advantage compared to busulfan-based preparative regimens; however, it is
not clear where treosulfan will be placed in clinical practice. The recommended indication for
treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) is: “for use in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen
for allogeneic HSCT in adult and pediatric patients 1 year of age and older with AML (1.1) [or]
MDS (1.2).”

Categorization of Adverse Events
Adverse events were reported down to the verbatim term. The adverse events were coded
using MedDRA version 20.0 for all studies and for the pooled safety population. Where
indicated in the tables or text, some adverse events are presented as grouped terms. Regimenrelated toxicity (RRT) is defined as directly caused by the conditioning regimen. Bearman’s RRT
criteria are used unless otherwise specified. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs)
included events that started from the start of study drug administration through HSCT day +30,
unless otherwise indicated.

Routine Clinical Tests
8.1.6 Safety Results
Deaths
In the full safety database, there were 14 deaths within 30 days of the last dose of treosulfan
and 57 deaths (43 additional deaths) within 100 days of the last dose of treosulfan.
In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) from fatal adverse reactions due to
treosulfan by Day +30; there were two additional deaths that were adjudicated as transplantrelated mortality. In the busulfan arm, there were two deaths prior to Day +30, both of which
were adjudicated as adverse reactions due to busulfan. In the treosulfan arm, there were 17
deaths (6%) by transplant day +100, compared to 19 deaths (7%) by transplant day + 100 in the
busulfan arm. However, we note that for six patients, we did not have sufficient information to
adjudicate the cause of death.
Serious Adverse Events
See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
Significant Adverse Events
See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
When evaluating TEAEs by Preferred Term (PT), the most common TEAEs were those expected
after allo-HSCT. The most common adverse reactions (≥10%) on the treosulfan arm of MCFludT.14/L Trial II included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema, infection,
vomiting, rash, diarrhea, headache, febrile neutropenia, abdominal pain, hypertension,
hemorrhage, fatigue, constipation, tachycardia, and hepatotoxicity. The most common grade 35 ARs (≥10%) on the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia and infection. Rare but potentially
serious events that occurred on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included
ventricular arrythmias (including tachycardia and extrasystoles; N = 5, 1.852%), erythema
multiforme (N = 8, 2.963%), palmar-plantar erythrodysaesthesia syndrome (N = 5, 1.852%), and
cardiac failure (N = 8, 2.963%).

8.2.5

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues

Treosulfan Dose (14 g/m2)
Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I used a treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2 for patients randomized to receive
treosulfan. This study was stopped early due to increased transplant-related morbidity and
mortality observed in the treosulfan arm. Treatment-emergent adverse events through HSCT
day +30 are shown in Table 26. There were increased TEAEs of hepatotoxicity, stomatitis,
musculoskeletal pain, infection, and febrile neutropenia in the treosulfan cohort, including
grades 3-5 TEAEs.

8.2.6

Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability

No COAs were included in this application.
8.2.7

Safety Analyses by Subgroups

See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for additional
subgroup analyses.
Drug-Donor Interactions
Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients undergoing both matched
related donor and matched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT. TEAEs were assessed in the
treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II in the related and unrelated donor populations. There
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

75

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
were more TEAEs observed in patients with matched unrelated donors, though this may be due
to the difference in patient numbers between these two groups. When evaluating Grade 3-5
TEAEs, infection was more common in patients with a matched related donor (N = 11, 17%)
than in those receiving a matched unrelated donor (N = 24, 12%).
A comparison between the treosulfan and busulfan groups was performed. All-grade
musculoskeletal pain, edema, infection, and hemorrhage were more common in patients with
matched related donors receiving treosulfan-based conditioning. Of note, grade 3-5 infection
was higher in the treosulfan group than the busulfan group (17.46% vs 4.412%, p-value =
0.022).
8.2.8

Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials

Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
As MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, in which patients randomized to treosulfan received a dose of 14 g/m2,
was stopped early because of increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality, a
comparison was performed between all adult patients who received 10 g/m2 and those who
received 14 g/m2 of treosulfan. We observed higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity,
infection (including febrile neutropenia), and musculoskeletal AEs in patients receiving the 14
g/m2 dose of treosulfan, as seen in Table 27.

Clinical TL Comment: As noted in the original review, across the safety population, patients who
received 14 g/m2/dose of treosulfan experienced substantially more toxicity, including infectious
complications, GI toxicity, and hepatotoxicity, compared to those who received the 10
g/m2/dose of treosulfan. In MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, the higher rates of early fatal adverse
reactions confirm an increased risk of morbidity and mortality with the 14 g/m2 dose of
treosulfan. These findings warrant inclusion of a warning and precaution in the USPI.
8.2.9

Additional Safety Explorations

Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
An analysis of the SOC “Neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (including cysts and
polyps)” in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II revealed one patient in the treosulfan arm and one patient in
the busulfan arm with these events.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Because of the proposed mechanism of action of treosulfan, pregnancy and reproduction are
discouraged. There have been no studies or literature reports of subjects who became pregnant
while receiving treosulfan, or of children who were born of parents who were receiving
treosulfan.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
Two pediatric studies were submitted as part of this application. All pediatric patients received
doses of treosulfan that were equivalent to 14 g/m2 dosing. Safety analysis was performed
comparing the pediatric patients to the recommended dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2). The

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

77

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
majority of pediatric patients received thiotepa in addition to fludarabine and treosulfan for
their conditioning. Analyses comparing pediatric and adult studies (Cohorts 4 and 1) excluded
patients who received thiotepa.
An analysis between the treosulfan arm and the busulfan arm in the Phase 2 randomized study
MC-FludT.16/NM was conducted. Nearly all of the patients in this analysis received a
preparative regimen of either treosulfan or busulfan, fludarabine, and thiotepa. The majority of
TEAEs were similar between the two arms. Notable exceptions included higher rates of
abdominal pain, rash, pruritis, hemorrhage, and infusion-related reactions in the treosulfan arm
of this study.
Pediatric patients from both MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M who received a preparative
regimen of treosulfan + fludarabine (Cohort 4) were then compared to adult patients who
received the recommended treosulfan-based conditioning regimen (Cohort 3, 10 g/m2). The
pediatric patient numbers are small, as the majority of patients received a preparative regimen
that included thiotepa and are excluded from this analysis. The analysis revealed higher rates of
hepatotoxicity and gastrointestinal toxicities in the pediatric patients who received a dose
equivalent to 14 g/m2 compared with adult patients who received the 10 g/m2 dose.
Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
See FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021).

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
