8.2.11

Integrated Assessment of Safety

The primary data in support of safety for the proposed indication of treosulfan came from study
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, in which 270 patients with either AML or MDS were exposed to
treosulfan as part of a conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. All patients received two
doses of treosulfan on days -4 and -3 prior to HSCT, at a dose of 10 g/m2/dose.
The study population was monitored for death, adverse events, serious adverse events, and
laboratory abnormalities. By Day +30 following HSCT, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) on the
treosulfan arm, all due to fatal pulmonary adverse reactions, as compared to 2 deaths (0.7%) on
the busulfan (control) arm.

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

78

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Adverse events with treosulfan were mainly similar to those observed with busulfan-based
conditioning. Common TEAEs (≥20%) included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia,
nausea, edema, infection, and vomiting. Of those, musculoskeletal pain (12% higher), edema
(11% higher), and infection (5% higher) occurred at a rate at least 5% higher in the treosulfan
arm than the busulfan arm. The most common grade 3-5 AEs (≥5%) in the treosulfan arm were
febrile neutropenia, infection, hypertension, stomatitis, and musculoskeletal pain. Of those,
infection occurred at a rate of >5% compared to the busulfan arm (6% higher).
Table 28. Summary of Major Safety Events from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II through Day +30
Treosulfan (N = 270)

Source: FDA analysis, ISS population (ADSL, ADAEGT2 datasets), clinical study report

Safety data supporting the dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) were from studies MC-FludT.6/L,
MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trials I and II. In this comparison, 290
patients were dosed at 10 g/m2/dose, and 305 patients were dosed at 14 g/m2/dose.
Higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity, infection (including febrile neutropenia), and
musculoskeletal AEs were reported in patients receiving the 14 g/m2 dose of treosulfan
compared to the 10 g/m2/dose.
In the pediatric studies, very few patients under the age of 1 year received a regimen without
thiotepa. This limits the conclusions regarding the safety of the proposed treosulfan treatment
regimen to patients 1 year and older. Higher rates of hepatotoxicity and GI toxicity were
reported in pediatric patients who received 14 g/m2/dose-equivalent of treosulfan compared to
(b) (4)
adults who received the 10 g/m2 dose.

8.3

Statistical Issues

This NDA has received two separate Complete Responses, dated 30 July 2021 and 15
September 2022. These Complete Responses were issued mainly for inadequate data collection
and study design. Key issues:
• The Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was stopped early for efficacy at 35% information
fraction of EFS. Early stopping for efficacy might result in an overestimation of a
treatment effect.
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

79

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
•
•
•
•

No alpha-controlled hypothesis testing was prespecified for the overall survival. Not
controlling for multiplicity might result in erroneous conclusions.
The EFS definition per protocol is different from the definition typically used for
regulatory consideration. Clinical and regulatory relevance of the EFS endpoint, as
defined in the protocol, is questionable.
Adequate data were not submitted to confirm the EFS results in the previous
submissions. Additional data were submitted, but the reliability of data is likely limited
beyond Day 100.
The full study population consists of two heterogeneous subpopulations that usually
have separate indications: AML and MDS. As there were no prespecified hypotheses
testing and alpha allocation for these subpopulations, residual uncertainty remains
regarding the benefit observed in these subpopulations.

Refer to the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021) for
further details. The relevant statistical issues are detailed below.
Data Collection
The following issues regarding data collection were noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review
and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021):
•

•

“The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS).
Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were
unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of
EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.
Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able
complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the
indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS.”

The extent of the submitted data is limited. Per Complete Response to the FDA Complete
Response Letter 30 July 2021, page 10: "The applicant would like to respectfully submit that the
collection of some of the marrow data was considered not mandatory under the study protocol
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, particularly beyond Day +100 and in case of lack of an event. However, in
some instances, bone marrow was collected beyond Day +100 and details of these assessments
are provided." Given the lack of reliable data collection beyond Day 100 (and “in case of lack of
an event”), outcomes relying on such data beyond Day 100 might be challenging to interpret.
Event-Free Survival Definition
Per statistical analysis plan, the primary efficacy endpoint is “event-free survival (EFS) within 2
years after transplantation measured from time of end of HSCT (= day 0) to time of event. The
associated time span is defined as the interval from day 0 to relapse, graft failure or death
(whatever occurs first). The date of the event is either the documented date of
relapse/progression or the date of death or the date of primary (date of examination of
neutrophilic granulocytes) or secondary graft failure (date of examination of neutrophilic

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

80

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
granulocytes).” This is not a standard definition used for regulatory considerations in AML or
MDS populations. Per FDA's Acute Myeloid Leukemia: Developing Drugs and Biological Products
for Treatment Guidance for Industry, EFS is defined as the "time from randomization to the date
of: induction treatment failure, relapse for those who have induction treatment success (e.g.,
complete remission), or death from any cause, whichever comes first". Interpretation of the
results based on the protocol-defined EFS outcome may be challenging.
Overall Survival Definition
Per statistical analysis plan, OS is defined as “the time length between day 0 and the date of
death due to any cause within the 24-month study period (i.e. excluding post-surveillance
evaluation).” Day zero is the time of allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. This is
not a standard OS definition used for regulatory purposes, which typically uses 1)
randomization as the start of the period of interest 2) has no restriction on follow-up (i.e., no
limit set to 24 months). Since the OS is both safety and efficacy endpoint, the regulatory
interest is in the full unrestricted survival period starting from randomization, referred to OS
(post-randomization) herein.
No Alpha Control and No Prespecified Boundary for Overall Survival Testing
Overall survival was one of the secondary outcomes. The OS results show a relatively high
magnitude of a treatment effect with the hazard ratio equal to 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90; 193
events) in the AML and MDS population. No alpha was allocated for testing overall survival and
no boundary for the interim analysis was prespecified. However, these results were considered
to reflect a meaningful improvement on the gold-standard endpoint in oncology.
Subgroups
The full study population consists of two distinct populations: patients with AML and patients
with MDS. While the OS (post-randomization) results appear to be supportive of the benefit in
AML and MDS subgroups, there was no prespecified alpha-controlled hypothesis testing for
these subgroups and the upper bounds of the 95% confidence intervals exceed one, suggesting
that some residual uncertainty about the treatment effect in each of the subgroups remains.
Additionally, further subgroup of interest included donor type: MUD vs MRD. As noted in the
FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021), “Due to differences
in minor and other unmeasured major antigens, HLA-matched unrelated donors have a higher
degree of histoincompatibility with the recipient than HLA-identical sibling donors, so the
minimum intensity of the preparative regimen needed for successful HSCT may differ by donor
type.” Higher HR estimates for the MRD group in the AML subpopulation are likely due to a few
influential events observed after two years of follow-up. Regarding the MDS subgroup, since
less than 10 events in each treatment group were observed in this subgroup, the results are
likely unreliable due to few observed events. Harm to patients with MRD cannot be ruled out.
The subgroup results should be interpreted with caution.
Differences in Treatment Regimen
As noted in the FDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation of NDA 214759 (30 July 2021),
Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

81

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)
there were differences in treatment regimens between study groups:
• “Trial 14 - II compared fludarabine in combination with 3 days of treatment with
treosulfan to a combination with 2 days of busulfan. Any conclusions regarding
superiority would need to take into account the difference between arms in the number
of days of treatment with treosulfan vs the number of days of treatment with busulfan.
• Additionally, in Trial 14 - II, patients with unrelated donors also received ATG in the
preparative regimen. ATG dosing varied by geographic region. Any conclusions related
to the subgroup analysis by donor would need to take into consideration the differences
in treatment regimens by donor type”
The differences in the treatment regimen might have an impact on the treatment effect
interpretation.
Stratification Factors
Per statistical analysis plan for the overall survival, “Cox proportional hazards regression model
stratified by centre with donor type (MUD vs. MRD), risk group (I vs. II) and treatment as factors
will be fitted.” The site factor has 22 levels, the donor type factor has two levels, and the risk
group factor has two levels. Including the site in a Cox proportional hazards model would result
in small or zero number of events in some strata, making the interpretation of the results
challenging (e.g., see Silcocks (2012)). Furthermore, this practice is not typically recommended
for trials with few patients per center. Per ICH E9 (page 17), “In some trials, for example, some
large mortality trials with very few subjects per center, there may be no reason to expect the
centers to have any influence on the primary or secondary variables because they are unlikely
to represent influences of clinical importance. In other trials, it may be recognized from the
start that the limited numbers of subjects per center will make it impracticable to include the
center effects in the statistical model. In these cases, it is not considered appropriate to include
a term for center in the model, and it is not necessary to stratify the randomization by center in
this situation”. For this reason, the results reported herein and in the USPI use a Cox
proportional hazards model stratified by donor type and risk group.
Proportional Hazards Assumptions
The treatment effects in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were quantified primarily with the hazard
ratios. The hazard ratios are estimated with a Cox proportional hazards model, which relies on
proportional hazards assumptions. A plateauing effect or crossing curves can be seen on most
Kaplan-Meier plots for the EFS and the OS outcomes. A plateauing effect or crossing curves
might indicate that the proportional hazards assumption does not hold. Additional analyses
were conducted to quantify the treatment effects with various alternative statistics that do not
rely on the proportional hazards assumptions. The results of these analyses do not change the
conclusions based on the hazard ratios, suggesting favorable outcomes for the treosulfan
compared to the busulfan.

Version date: April 2019
Reference ID: 5515086

82

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation [NDA 214759]
Grafapex (treosulfan)

8.4

Conclusions and Recommendations

The review team was able to verify or clarify the efficacy and safety endpoints as provided by
the Applicant. The efficacy of 10 g/m2/dose treosulfan as a component of a conditioning
regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT was established based on improvement in overall survival
with HR 0.67 (95% CI: 0.51, 0.90) demonstrated in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and on
supportive evidence provided by Study MC-FludT.6/L.
The safety of treosulfan 10 g/m2/dose was generally similar to that of busulfan in Study MCFludT.14/L Trial II with a nominally higher incidence of musculoskeletal pain and infection in the
treosulfan arm. One major safety concern is the high morbidity and mortality with the 14
g/m2/dose treosulfan regimen established in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I and supported by Study MCFludT.6/L. These findings warrant inclusion of a Warning and Precaution in the USPI since this
dose is published in the literature and approved for the pediatric population in some countries.
The efficacy of treosulfan at 10 g/m2/dose was extrapolated to pediatric patients undergoing
HSCT for an AML or MDS based on the efficacy demonstrated in the adult pivotal study and
(b) (4)
safety was established based on data from pediatric studies in patients 1 year and older.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
Recommended Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments:
PMR #1
Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to evaluate a serious potential risk of increased drug
toxicity and determine an appropriate, safe dose of treosulfan in patients with moderate renal
impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate or creatinine clearance 30-59 mL/min). This
trial should be designed and conducted in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled
“Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and
Impact on Dosing and Labeling.”
PMR #2
Conduct a clinical trial to characterize the serious potential risk of QTc prolongation (i.e.,
exclude large mean increases of >20 msec in the QTc interval) with treosulfan and its active
metabolite. Design and conduct the study in accordance with the ICH E14 Guidances for
Industry titled, E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc and E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval
Prolongation and Proarrhythmic Potential for Non-Antiarrhythmic Drugs Questions and
Answers (R3) Guidance for Industry.
PMR #3
Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to characterize the serious potential risk of increased
drug toxicity and further characterize the safety of treosulfan by evaluating the
pharmacokinetics of the active monoepoxide intermediate [(2S,3S)-1,2-epoxybutane-3,4-diol-4methanesulfonate] in adult patients after administration of treosulfan.
PMC #1
Conduct a clinical trial to assess the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan among U.S. racial and
ethnic groups. This study should characterize the exposure (including pharmacokinetics data) of
treosulfan.
PMC #2
Conduct a registry-based observational study to assess outcomes by race and ethnicity for
patients undergoing allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation using treosulfanfludarabine as the preparative regimen. Include subgroup analyses by race and ethnicity for
overall survival, nonrelapse mortality and relapse-free survival.

Conduct an in vitro study to evaluate treosulfan and epoxide metabolites as substrates
of major cytochrome P450 enzymes consistent with FDA guidance.

Vicky Hsu, Ph.D.
Ankit Shah, Ph.D.

The clinical pharmacology of treosulfan was extensively reviewed in the original submission
(Unireview link, DARRTS 7-30-21). Briefly, the Applicant is seeking approval of treosulfan as
part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation
(alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes
(MDS). The proposed dosing regimen is treosulfan 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4,
-3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6
through -2) prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT; Day 0).
Pivotal evidence to support efficacy in the proposed population is based on the results of Study
MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. Key review issues include the adequacy of the proposed treosulfan
dosing regimen in adult patients, pediatric extrapolation to identify a treosulfan dosing regimen
appropriate for pediatric patients ≥1 year of age, and evaluation of the Applicant’s proposals
for patients with organ impairment. The Applicant provided pharmacokinetic studies in adults
and pediatric patients, a population PK model of treosulfan, and a dose-response evaluation in
adults to support the dose selection in the general population and specific populations. The
1

Reference ID: 5471631

Applicant did not provide adequate data to resolve other review questions including
characterization of treosulfan metabolism and effects of treosulfan on QTc interval.
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology did not recommend approval then because of issues
regarding the veracity of the primary study endpoint.
A summary of key review issues and recommendations from the original clinical pharmacology
review of NDA 214759 is provided below:
REVIEW ISSUE

RECOMMENDATIONS / COMMENTS

General dosing
instructions

The recommended dosage regimen of treosulfan is 10 g/m2/day for 3
consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with fludarabine 30
mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic
hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (Day 0).
Pediatric Patients >1 year of age: The recommended dosage regimen of
treosulfan is the same for all patients >1 year of age. Efficacy in pediatric
patients >1 year of age was extrapolated from adults and dose selection
was supported by PK and safety data from Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and
MC-FludT.17/M. Treosulfan exposure after administration of 10 g/m2 in
pediatric patients with BSA <0.7 m2 and with BSA 0.7 to <1.1 m2 are 11%
and 5% higher, respectively, compared to adults. Exposure-response
analyses for safety did not indicate that an 11% increase in exposures
would result in significant increase in the probability of common toxicities.

Dosing in patient
subgroups
(intrinsic and
extrinsic factors)

Renal Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients
with mild renal impairment. An additional study will be necessary to identify
a safe dose of treosulfan for patients with moderate hepatic impairment. A
study in patients with severe renal impairment is not feasible and will not be
required.
Hepatic Impairment: No dosage modification is recommended for patients
with mild hepatic impairment. Studies in patients with moderate or severe
hepatic impairment are not needed, as these patients are typically excluded
from transplantation.
The previous clinical pharmacology review identified 5 potential post-marketing requirements /
commitments (PMR / PMCs), which are still applicable and should be issued for this NDA (see
Table below).

metabolized by CYP2C8. The labeling has been updated to reflect this information. In vitro
metabolism studies for other metabolites are currently ongoing (relates to PMR 2, above).
This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The PMRs and PMC listed
above should be issued to the applicant.

1

Executive Summary

1.1

Introduction

Moran Choe, PhD

GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) is an alkylating drug. The Applicant, Medac GmbH, submitted
a Class 2 Resubmission of the original NDA 214759. The Applicant is seeking
marketing approval for GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) for the use in combination with
fludarabine as a preparative regimen for alloHSCT in adult and pediatric patients 1 year
of age and older with AML. The current submission is a resubmission of the original
NDA that was submitted under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and
Cosmetic Act on August 11, 2020.

1.2

Brief Discussion of Nonclinical Findings

The relevant nonclinical pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, and toxicology studies for
treosulfan to support NDA 214759 were previously reviewed in the Multidisciplinary
Review and Evaluation (Unireview; DARRTS date: July 30, 2021) for the initial
submission of NDA 214759. During the review of the NDA, it was determined that the
nonclinical data for treosulfan submitted to the NDA in studies or literature for which the
Applicant has the right to reference did not provide convincing evidence of DNA
alkylation and breaks as the mechanism of action (MOA) of treosulfan or to address
genotoxicity potential of the drug. The FDA issued a Complete Response Letter due to
Clinical deficiencies. In the letter, FDA recommended the Applicant conduct and submit
the results of nonclinical studies to address the comment from Pharmacology/
Toxicology discipline.
To address this issue, the Applicant conducted genetic toxicology studies, and
submitted the results to demonstrate the genotoxicity of treosulfan. Genetic toxicology
studies included an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay and an in vitro
chromosomal aberration assay.
In the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay, treosulfan tested at up to 3160 μg per
plate, with or without metabolic activation, caused a concentration-dependent
mutagenic effect in the Salmonella typhimurium strains. In the in vitro chromosomal
aberration assay using human lymphocyte cultures, treosulfan tested at up to 50 μg/mL
showed clastogenic properties with respect to chromosomal or chromatid damage with
or without metabolic activation. Overall, the study results showed that treosulfan is a
genotoxic compound.

1.3

Recommendations

1.3.1

Approvability

There are no Pharmacology/Toxicology approvability issues with the proposed
GRAFAPEX (treosulfan) for Injection drug product for the proposed indication.

NDA 214759

Moran Choe, PhD

Additional Non Clinical Recommendations

None.
1.3.3

Labeling

Appropriate labeling changes have been made to the United States Prescribing
Information (USPI) to be consistent with the Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule
(PLLR) format and current labeling practices. A sentence regarding the genetic
toxicology studies has been added under Section 13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis,
Impairment of Fertility.

2.2

Review and Evaluation for NDA 214759 submitted to DARRTS on July 30, 2021.

3

Genetic Toxicology

3.1

In Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells (Ames)

Mutagenicity study of treosulfan in the Salmonella thyphimurium and Escherichia
coli reverse mutation assay (in vitro)
Study no.: 38665
Study report location: 4.2.3.3.1
Date of study initiation: September 17, 2021
GLP compliance: Yes
QA statement: Yes
Drug, lot #, and % purity: Treosulfan Lyophilisate 1000 mg,
F190859A, 98.9%
Key Study Findings
•
•

•

Treosulfan was positive for mutagenicity in the reverse mutation assay.
Treosulfan induced a concentration-dependent mutagenic effect and at least 2fold increase in the number of revertant colonies for test strains TA100 and
TA1535 with or without metabolic activation. No mutagenic effect was observed
for the test strains TA98, TA1537 and WP2 uvrA.
Cytotoxicity was observed in all tester strains at 3160 μg/plate, resulting in a
reduction in the number of spontaneous revertant colonies. No test item
precipitation was observed.

Methods
Strains: 4 strains of Salmonella typhimurium
(TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537) and
1 strain of Escherichia coli (WP2 uvrA)
Concentrations in definitive study: 10, 31.6, 100, 316, 1000 and 3160 μg/plate
Basis of concentration selection: cytotoxicity at ≥3160 μg/plate in tester strain
TA100 with and without S9 mix
Negative control: sterile deionized water
Positive control: 2-aminoanthracene, 2-nitrofluorene,
sodium azide, 4-nitroquinoline-N-oxide,
benzo[a]pyrene
Formulation/Vehicle: sterile deionized water
Incubation & sampling time: 50 hours at 37°C

Study Validity
The mean number of revertant colonies observed for the negative/solvent control was
comparable to historical control data obtained from the previous studies. All positive
controls used induced the expected increase in the mean revertant colonies relative to
the negative/solvent control, thereby confirming the responsiveness of the strains and
the sensitivity of the test system.

In vitro assessment of the clastogenic activity of treosulfan in cultured human

Key Study Findings
•
•

•

Treosulfan was positive for clastogenicity in the in vitro chromosome aberrations
assay in human peripheral lymphocytes.
Treosulfan induced 1.0 - 9.0% of chromosomal aberrations (excluding gaps) of
the cells at concentrations of 3.125 - 50 μg/mL medium (4-hour exposure) and
1.3 - 8.4 % of chromosomal aberrations at concentrations of 3.125 to 25 μg/mL
medium (24-hour exposure) in the absence of metabolic activation.
Treosulfan induced chromosomal aberrations (excluding gaps) of the cells at
concentrations of 3.125 - 50 μg/mL medium (4-hour exposure) in the presence of
metabolic activation.

Cell line: human peripheral lymphocytes
Concentrations in definitive study: 3.125, 6.25, 12.5, 25, and 50 μg/mL +S9
activation (4 hours) and -S9 activation (4 or
24 hours)

S9 activation
Negative control: sterile deionized water
Positive control: cyclophosphamide monohydrate (+S9),
mitomycin C (-S9)
Formulation/Vehicle: sterile deionized water
Incubation & sampling time: 4 and 24 hours in the absence of activation
system and 4 hours in the presence of
activation system.

Study Validity
The positive controls induced a significant increase in the percentage of cells with
structural aberrations. The percentage of cells with structural aberrations in the negative
and positive controls was comparable to the historical control data.

DIVISION OF HEMATOLOGIC MALIGNANCIES 1
Application Number

(b) (4)
Proposed Trade Name
Nonproprietary Name
Treosulfan
Dosage Form and Strength Injection, lyophilized (1000 mg, 5000 mg)

Intravenous
Proposed Dosing Regimen 10 g/m² body surface area (BSA) per day as a two-hour

Proposed Indication
As part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic
hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult
patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and
myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS).
Team Leader
Donna Przepiorka, MD, PhD
Regulatory Background: NDA 214759 was submitted 8/11/2020, and a CRL was issued on
7/30/2021. A resubmission with response to the CRL was received 4/21/2022, and an
Acknowledge Incomplete Response Letter was issued on 5/16/2022 due to deficiencies in the
datasets. SDN 65 includes a response to the CRL and responses to the issues in the 5/16/2022
letter.
Background: During review of the datasets in preparation for endpoint adjudication, a number
of implausible laboratory results were identified. An Information Request was sent to the
Applicant on 9/1/2022. The Applicant clarified several typographical errors that needed to be
corrected. Two issues remained unresolved:
1. In lb.xpt, there were 86 instances where the marrow blast percentage (BLAST) was < 1 %
and in some cases displayed as low as 0.01 %, numbers too low to be plausible for a blast
count by morphology. In their 9/6/2022 response, the Applicant indicated that "Documented
values <1%, however, are obviously not related to classical microscopic morphological
assessments of bone marrow smears or histological BM specimens. It is reasonable to
assume that the study sites performed, e.g., multiparameter flow cytometric (MFC) detection
of minimal residual disease (MRD) of bone marrow aspirates to detect leukemic blasts and
documented these results to the best of their abilities."
Review: The Applicant's response supports the uncertainty regarding whether the data for the
parameter BLAST is a true marrow blast count. As such, this data file is unreviewable for the
purposes of endpoint adjudication.
2. In adchim.xpt, there are 245 cases in which there is a result for a marrow chimerism study,
but there are no marrow results in adlb.xpt based on the data element for parameter BLAST.
1

Reference ID: 5046234

In their 9/6/2022 response, the Applicant indicated that "The questioned marrow results
(leukemic blasts in bone marrow) are not available." They further asserted that marrow
results were not required if there were no signs of relapse or progression and that additional
marrow results were in adcefa.xpt.
Marrows on Day +28 and Day +100 were required by protocol independent of whether there
were signs of relapse. Assessment of the discordant cases indicated that 179 of the missing
were from Day +28 or Day +100, and that the data available in adcefa.xpt accounted for only
24 of the missing Day +28 or Day +100 cases.
Review: From the presence of marrow chimerism data without concordant marrow blast
counts, one can conclude that there are still data that exist but are not submitted in
accordance with the protocol.
3. In the table on page 65 of the Complete Response to the CRL received in SDN 65, the
Applicant indicated that presence or absence of dysplasia was not a data element collected,
and that the Cheson 2006 criteria were used.
Review: The Cheson criteria require analysis of outcomes with and without incorporating the
presence of dysplasia. Consequently, it may not be possible to assess the overall response rate,
the endpoint used for regulatory decision-making, for the subgroup of patients with MDS.
Summary Statement: Comment #1 of the CRL advised that submission of the CBC and
marrow results through the assessment period for EFS would be needed to confirm that the
results of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II. The current version of the dataset is incomplete,
and the parameter for the marrow blast count contains misleading information.
Recommendation: Acknowledge Incomplete Response Letter
Letter-Ready Comments
1. You have not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of treosulfan as a part of a
preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation for adult and
pediatric patients with AML or MDS for the following reasons:
a. A major element of the response criteria for both AML and MDS is the percentage of
blasts on morphological examination of the marrow. In response to our 9/1/2022 query
regarding implausible results in lb.LBSTRESN for LBTESTCD==BLAST, you indicated
that this variable may include results of MRD assays rather than the percentage of
marrow blasts. As such, there is insufficient information to confirm the primary endpoint
of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.
b. In response to our 9/1/2022 query identifying cases where there were results for marrow
chimerism studies in adchim.xpt but no results for blasts in lb.xpt on the same date, you
indicated that marrow results were not required in the absence of signs of relapse, and
that additional blast percentages were submitted in adcefa.xpt. According to the Flow
2

Reference ID: 5046234

Chart / Clinical and Laboratory Tests in the protocol for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II,
disease status by marrow examination was required for at least Day +28 and Day +100.
Assessment of the discordant cases indicated that 179 of the missing cases were from
Day +28 or Day +100, which would be required by protocol independent of whether
there were signs of relapse. Additionally, the data available in adcefa.xpt accounted for
only 24 of the missing Day +28 or Day +100 cases. Therefore, it appears that there is still
substantial missing data.
To address this issue, submit a pdf of the source documentation for all marrow results (i.e.,
copies of the pathology reports) through the complete assessment period for EFS for MCFLudT.14/L Trial II, submit a revision of lb.xpt (and all dependent data files) to separate out
results of the MRD assays from the percentage of marrow blasts, and submit a revision of
adnewrel.xpt to include the results for percentage of marrow blasts from both lb.xpt and
adcefa.xpt. Whether the results will be adequate to support approval of both indications
will be a review issue.

Antineoplastic
Small molecule
Alkylating agent
Treosulfan is an alkylating agent. DNA alkylation is thought to be
responsible for the cytotoxic activities of treosulfan. Treosulfan
depleted hematopoietic stem cells and showed immunosuppressive and antitumor activity in mouse models of leukemia.

NDA 214759 for Grafapex was submitted under the 505(b)(1) pathway for the indication "as
part of conditioning treatment prior to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation
(alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes
(MDS)" using a treosulfan dose of 10 g/m² per day combination with fludarabine based on the
results of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II. The Applicant also proposed to include in the USPI
(b) (4)
Section 14 a description of

1.2

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

A complete response is recommended under 21 CFR 314.125(b)(5) due to lack of substantial
evidence of effectiveness.

11

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II (NCT00822393) was a multicenter, randomized, active-controlled trial
comparing treosuflan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen
for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old with AML or MDS,
Karnofsky performance status > 60%, and age ≥ 50 years or hematopoietic cell transplantation
comorbidity index [HCT CI] score > 2. The patients were randomized to receive treosulfan 10
g/m² daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours on days -4 and -3 in
combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and hematopoietic
stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as well to
patients with unrelated donors.
The study was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of event-free survival
(EFS) with 2 years of follow-up after transplantation. Events were defined as disease relapse,
graft failure, or death. The noninferiority margin on the hazard ratio scale was 1.3. If
noninferiority was demonstrated, then the primary endpoint would be tested for superiority.
Overall survival (OS) was a secondary endpoint, but there was no alpha allocated to testing OS.
The trial was stopped at the second interim analysis when EFS noninferiority was achieved.
Since the trial continued enrolling until the results of the interim analysis were known, an
additional "last analysis" was conducted using all enrolled patients. On the basis of the last
analysis, the Applicant concluded that the results supported noninferiority for EFS, and in
sequential testing, superiority of treosulfan for EFS (48.6% information level). Superiority of
treosulfan for OS was not demonstrated at the second interim analysis but was at the last
analysis.
The review team was not able to confirm the results of the primary endpoint analysis, since the
raw data needed to verify the components the EFS endpoint were incomplete. Additionally, in
the absence of a prespecified plan for testing OS for superiority with control of Type 1 error, the
results of the OS analysis were not considered substantial evidence of effectiveness.

1.3

Benefit-Risk Assessment

In the absence of evidence to conclude effectiveness, a benefit-risk assessment cannot be
formulated.

1.4

Patient Experience Data

Patient Experience Data Relevant to this Application
X

Patient experience data were not submitted as part of this application.

Grafapex (treosulfan)

1.5

Comments to Applicant

1. You have not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of treosulfan as a part of a
preparative regimen for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation for adult and
pediatric patients with AML or MDS for the following reasons:
•

The primary endpoint of Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II was event-free survival (EFS).
Because all complete blood cell (CBC) and marrow results were not submitted, we were
unable to independently confirm the results of the analysis of the primary endpoint of
EFS for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II.

•

Additionally, because of the missing CBC and marrow results, we were not able
complete our assessment of whether the results of the study would support the
indications for treatment of patients with AML and for treatment of patients with MDS.

To address this issue, submit the CBC and marrow results through the complete assessment
period for EFS for MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. Whether the results would be adequate to
support approval of both indications will be a review issue.
2.

(b) (4)

for the following reasons:

OS cannot be used as the sole basis for approval

•

The statistical plan for Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II did not include control of Type 1
error for overall survival (OS).

•

There was no prespecified boundary for OS interim analyses and no alpha allocation for
multiple OS analyses. Therefore, p-values are nominal, and analysis of OS is considered
to be exploratory.

To address this issue, submit data from an adequate and well-controlled trial of treosulfan
vs busulfan with a prespecified analysis of OS for each indication with control of Type 1
error.
Additional Comments
Clinical/Statistical
1. Treatment of AML and treatment of MDS are considered different indications. In order to
obtain approval for both indications, you would need to demonstrate substantial evidence
of effectiveness for each individually.

13

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
2. Your subgroup analyses of EFS and OS suggest a substantial difference in treatment effect
by donor type. Clarify how the results support your claim of noninferiority of treosulfan for
HSCT from both related and unrelated donors (with the latter using ATG).
3. You conducted your efficacy analyses in the Full Analysis Set (FAS). For a randomized trial
with EFS and/or OS endpoints, the analyses should be conducted on the intent-to-treat (ITT)
population.
4. For Study MC-FludT.14/L II, the variable RACE in ADSL has no data element for any of the
study subjects. Consequently, the submission did not provide adequate information to
ensure that the study results are applicable to the US population. Consider conducting a
study to assess outcomes by race and ethnicity for patients undergoing allogeneic HSCT
using treosulfan-fludarabine as the preparative regimen. Include subgroup analyses by race
and ethnicity for overall survival, nonrelapse mortality and relapse-free survival with at least
3 years of follow-up.
(b) (4)

5.

Clinical Pharmacology
6. Address the following in the NDA resubmission:
a. Provide data to determine an appropriate safe dose of treosulfan for patients with
moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30-59 mL/min). This should be conducted in
accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled “Pharmacokinetics in Patients with
Impaired Renal Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and
Labeling.”
b. Provide data to support the evaluation of the effects of treosulfan on the QTc interval.
This should be done in accordance with ICH E14.
c. Provide data evaluating treosulfan and its epoxide metabolites as substrates of
metabolizing enzymes in vitro.
d. Provide data evaluating the pharmacokinetics (PK) of treosulfan active metabolites in
adult patients.
e. Provide data evaluating the effects of race and ethnicity on treosulfan PK.

Grafapex (treosulfan)
Nonclinical
7. We recommend that prior to the resubmission of the NDA, you conduct an in vitro study
demonstrating DNA alkylation and breakage with treosulfan to adequately characterize the
alkylating and genotoxicity properties and determine the potential serious risk of
reproductive and developmental toxicity and carcinogenicity with treosulfan. This study
should be submitted to the NDA with the resubmission.

2

Therapeutic Context

2.1

Analysis of Condition

Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and high-risk myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) have high rates
of morbidity and mortality.
2.1.1 Acute Myeloid Leukemia
AML is the most common type of acute leukemia in adult patients, with an incidence of 21,450
patients in the US in 2019. Fifty percent of patients with AML die of their disease. The incidence
of AML increases with age. As the population of the United States ages, the incidence and
median age of AML continues to increase. The median age of diagnosis is approximately 68
years [1]. Older patients often do not tolerate intensive chemotherapy, underscoring the need
for new therapies.
AML is a life-threatening condition. Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT)
is required for curative therapy of high risk AML and IPSS-R intermediate, high, and very high
risk MDS [2, 3]. A meta-analysis of 24 trials evaluating allogeneic HSCT, autologous HSCT, and
chemotherapy alone for AML identified a significant reduction in hazard of death if allogeneic
HSCT occurs in CR1 as compared to autologous HSCT or chemotherapy alone (hazard ratio 0.9,
95% CI 0.82-0.97) [4]. Further analysis revealed that the benefit of allogeneic HSCT was
significant only in intermediate-risk (HR 0.76) and poor-risk (HR 0.69) AML, but not in favorablerisk (HR 1.06) AML. Additionally, the combined HR for relapse-free survival was 0.8 (0.74-0.86)
across all risk groups and 0.78 (0.73-0.84) for intermediate and poor-risk patients.
2.1.2 Myelodysplastic Syndrome
MDS encompasses a group of hematopoietic stem cell disorders, which have dysplasia in
myeloid, erythroid, or megakaryocytic lineages and are associated with cytopenias [5]. The
median age of diagnosis is 70 years [5]. The most recent SEER 5-year age-adjusted incidence
15

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
rate (2014-2018) is 4.2 per 100,000, with a higher incidence in men (5.8 per 100,000 compared
to 3.1 per 100,000 in women) [6].
Risk classification of MDS is performed using the Revised International Prognostic Scoring
System (IPSS-R). Patients are classified as having very low, low, intermediate, high, or very high
risk MDS based on bone marrow cytogenetics, bone marrow blast percentage, and cytopenias
[7]. Patients with intermediate, high-risk, or very high-risk MDS based on IPSS-R require
allogeneic stem cell transplantation for curative therapy [8].

2.2

Analysis of Current Treatment Options

Currently, no drugs are approved for use as a preparative regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT for
AML or MDS.
Drugs used in the standard of care for preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT in AML and MDS
include, but are not limited to, cyclophosphamide, busulfan, fludarabine, melphalan, ATG, and
radiation therapy. Table 1 provides comparisons of various HSCT preparative regimens from
1996-2020.

Abbreviations: HSCT: hematopoietic stem cell transplant; AML: acute myeloid leukemia; Cy: cyclophosphamide;

secondary AML.

2.2.1 Myeloablative conditioning
Preparative regimens for allogeneic HSCT are not standardized and range greatly from
institution to institution and country to country. Myeloablative conditioning (MAC) results in
complete eradication of host hematopoiesis and severe immunosuppression. Severe
myelosuppression may directly kill leukemia stem cells, and AML is further attacked by donor
cells, causing a graft versus leukemia (GVL) effect. The most common myeloablative preparative
regimens are cyclophosphamide (120 mg/m2) and total body irradiation and busulfan (14-16
mg/kg) and cyclophosphamide (120-200 mg/m2). However, MAC is associated with high rates of
transplant-related morbidity and mortality, particularly in older patients with pre-existing
comorbidities [9].
17

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
2.2.2 Reduced intensity conditioning
Reduced intensity conditioning (RIC) regimens have lower rates of transplant-related morbidity
and mortality, but they range in their myelosuppressive and immunosuppressive
characteristics. In general, the CIBMTR defines RIC regimens as TBI ≤ 5 Gy (single dose) or ≤ 8 Gy
(fractionated), busulfan doses ≤ 9 mg/kg, or melphalan doses ≤ 150 mg/m2. The most
frequently used RIC regimens in a 2015 publication included fludarabine with either low-dose
TBI or an alkylating agent [10]. A T-cell depleting agent such as ATG or Campath is used often,
especially for matched unrelated donors.
2.2.3 MAC versus RIC
Table 1 shows a comparison of HSCT conditioning regimens over the last 25 years. For patients
with AML, MAC regimens are generally not significantly different from each other [11-13].
Comparison of MAC vs RIC for patients with AML has not led to consensus. A 2017 publication
of a study conducted by the Blood and Marrow Transplant Clinical Trials Network (BMT CTN)
showed decreased transplant-related mortality with RIC HSCT. The study population included
patients with MDS or AML aged 18-65 with HCT-CI ≤ 4. While overall survival (OS) was similar
between the groups, MAC HSCT resulted in a significantly decreased risk of relapse-free survival
(RFS) compared to RIC HSCT [14]. Interestingly, patients with MDS did not have a difference in
RFS. However, multiple regimens were available for both RIC and MAC HSCT, with no one
standard of care. Sixty-four percent of MAC HSCTs were Flu/Bu4, 30% were Bu/Cy, and the
remainder were Cy/TBI. RIC HSCT regimens included Flu/Bu2 (81%) and Flu/Mel (19%) [14].
Other studies have not shown differences in OS or event-free survival, however [15-19]. For
AML patients with detectable minimal residual disease, MAC may result in improved survival
[20].
Within RIC regimens, two of the most commonly used conditioning regimens are fludarabine +
busulfan and fludarabine + melphalan. The CIBMTR published an analysis of their transplant
registry, comparing 622 AML patients who received a fludarabine + melphalan RIC HSCT and
791 AML patients who received a fludarabine + busulfan RIC HSCT. Fludarabine + melphalan
was associated with higher transplant-related mortality but lower relapse risk; overall survival
and leukemia-free survival were similar between the two regimens [1].
2.2.4 Patient Selection for RIC
While RIC HSCTs are safe and have efficacy, particularly in an older, less fit population, MAC
regimens continue to confer a benefit in survival and relapse. However, as the age of AML
diagnosis increases, more AML patients are undergoing RIC HSCT. While there are no standard
guidelines for which patients should receive RIC vs MAC, one trial made the following
recommendations for RIC [14]:
1. Patients >65 years of age OR
2. Patients with HCT comorbidity index (HCT-CI) scores ≥ 3.
18

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
HCT-CI scoring includes categories for age and a number of potential comorbidities in different
organ-system classes. A multi-institutional retrospective analysis of approximately 3000
patients validated the use of the HCT-CI score for TRM. Age >20 was associated with increasing
risk of TRM, with statistical significance for all patients 40 years of age and older. An HCT-CI of
0, 1, or 2 resulted in no statistical significance in the hazard ratio for survival; survival rates
were >60% at two years for both RIC and MAC. An HCT-CI score of 3 or 4 resulted in two-year
survival of 50% for RIC and 45% for MAC; an HCT-CI ≥ 5 had two-year survival rates of 35 and
29%, for RIC and MAC, respectively [9].
Based on these data, for patients with AML and MDS who are either ≥ 65 years of age, or who
have an HCT-CI score ≥ 3 should be considered for RIC allogeneic HSCT.

the conduct at the study sites was adequate and study data derived from clinical sites appeared
reliable.

Grafapex (treosulfan)

5. 1

Executive Summary

Treosulfan (L-Threitol 1,4-bismethanesulfonate) is an alkylating drug and is closely related to
busulfan with the addition of 2 hydroxyl groups to facilitate solubility in water. Since treosulfan
was synthesized 60 years ago and has been widely used as a cytotoxic drug to treat ovarian
cancer for many decades, it is well-characterized in peer-reviewed scientific literature. The
Applicant (Medac GmbH) provided references to published studies in support of the marketing
application. Treosulfan is indicated in combination with fludarabine as a preparative regimen
for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with acute myeloid leukemia
(AML) or myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). Except as specifically identified, all nonclinical data
and information discussed below and necessary for approval of this NDA (#214759) are owned
by Medac GmbH or are data for which Medac GmbH has a right of reference. Any information
or data that Medac GmbH does not own or have a written right to reference is for descriptive
purposes only and is not relied upon for approval of this NDA.
In cellular assays employing primary pediatric leukemic cells and leukemic cell lines, 72-hour
treosulfan exposure had a cytotoxic effect with inhibitory concentration yielding 50% response
(IC50) values ranging from 0.7 to 490 μM. Cells from patients with common acute lymphoblastic
leukemia (cALL) were most sensitive, while cells from a patient with mature B-ALL were most
resistant to treosulfan. In most cases, under the conditions tested, cells were more sensitive to
treosulfan than to busulfan. Immune cells were also sensitive to treosulfan cytotoxicity, as T
cells, NK cells, and stem cells isolated from healthy donors yielded IC50s from <0.4 to 49.7 μM,
with stem cells exhibiting the most sensitive effect.
Treosulfan showed in vivo myeloablative and immunosuppressive activity in a murine
allogeneic bone marrow transplant model. BALB/c mice received 1.5 g/kg/day of treosulfan
intraperitoneally (i.p.) x 3 days. Stem cells (assessed via colony forming units, CFUs) were
rapidly depleted by post-treatment Day 1 and were negligible for the remaining 12 days of the
experiment. Fluorescence activated cell sorting (FACS) analysis also showed decreases in T and
B cells. The number of immune cells producing tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-α or interferon
(IFN)-γ remained constant or decreased slightly, while the percentage of interleukin 2 (IL-2)producing T cells decreased to 50% of control values on Days 6-12. A mixed lymphocyte
reaction test demonstrated that treosulfan-induced T-cell depletion suppressed alloreactivity in
host splenic cells (BALB/c) mixed with C57/black stimulator lymphocytes.
The Applicant also included several references describing treosulfan’s antileukemic and
antineoplastic activity in vivo. Antitumor activity was demonstrated in mice bearing sarcoma or
carcinoma tumors.
In cardiovascular in vitro safety pharmacology studies, treosulfan did not exhibit the potential
to induce QT prolongation. There were also no changes in cardiovascular parameters when
23

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
fasting dogs received 5-200 mg/kg of treosulfan intravenously (i.v.) and were monitored via ECG
for 4 hours. Central nervous system (CNS) functions were not altered in mice that received 100
mg/kg of treosulfan i.p. or in a fasted dog that received 200 mg/kg of treosulfan via oral
administration (p.o.).
To assess the safety of treosulfan, the Applicant conducted a GLP-compliant toxicology study up
to 4 weeks in adult Sprague-Dawley rats. Rats received treosulfan at doses of 10, 50, or 150
mg/kg via i.v. once per day x 28 days, followed by a 2-week recovery period. The Applicant
assessed 2 different formulations of treosulfan, a powdered form and a lyophilisate form.
Toxicokinetic (TK) analysis showed that exposures were dose-proportional and were
comparable between males and females. Exposures and toxicities were also generally
comparable between formulations. There was no apparent accumulation over time. Drugrelated adverse findings included bone marrow suppression, lymphoid atrophy in spleen,
thymic hemorrhage and atrophy, GI tract hyperplasia, muscle necrosis, multi-organ
inflammation, and injection site reactions. Inflammation was observed in the kidney, colon,
urinary bladder, and prostate gland. The male and female reproductive organs (epididymis,
testes, prostate, and ovary) were also targets of toxicity. Other findings included optic nerve
hemorrhage, and Peyer’s patch hyperplasia. There were 2 unscheduled deaths, both animals
were in the high dose group and received the lyophilizate formulation. One female died in the
3rd week of dosing and was found to have edematous lungs and dilated intestines filled with
fluid. The second animal was a male in the recovery cohort that had to be euthanized 1 day
prior to the scheduled sacrifice (Day 42). The male had swelling in several areas including the
nose/snout, paw, and testes that was discovered, upon histology analysis, to be a malignant
leukemia. Males treated with either formulation also had enlarged epididymis and testes, which
was not reversed during the recovery phase. Females treated with the powdered formulation
had dose-dependent shrunken ovaries. Animals receiving either formulation had a dosedependent increase in brain weight.
Treosulfan is genotoxic and induced a concentration-dependent increase in percent of
anaphases with aberrations in onion root tips. The percentage of cells with aberrant anaphases
increased from 3% to 21% when cells were treated with 50 mM of treosulfan or 0.05 mM of
diepoxybutane (DEB), respectively. Dedicated carcinogenicity or fertility studies were not
conducted with treosulfan, as they are not needed to support the use of a drug intended to
treat patients with advanced cancer. Cytotoxic drugs that demonstrate genotoxicity are
generally recognized as being carcinogenic, and in the general toxicology study, one animal
treated with treosulfan presented with leukemia. A 7-month chronic-dose study conducted in
Wistar rats revealed dose-dependent fertility impairment in males and females (i.e. arrested
spermatogenesis and absent corpora lutea and follicles). To assess the potential for
developmental toxicity, the Applicant conducted a juvenile toxicity study in which juvenile rats
received treosulfan dosed at 10, 50, or 100 mg/kg/day i.v. in main and recovery groups during
postnatal development days (PND) 10 to 35. Since the hematopoietic organs are targets of
treosulfan, the findings in juvenile animals were typically comparable to the findings observed
24

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
in adult animals. Juvenile rats had delayed development, observed as reduced body weight and
a dose-dependent reduction in the size of male reproductive organs (testes and prostate).
Moreover, females at 100 mg/kg experienced a significant delay in sexual maturation (vaginal
opening delayed by 1.8 days). A pharmacokinetic assessment of the blood-brain barrier
penetration of treosulfan showed that following single intravenous administration of 500 mg/kg
to juvenile or young adult rats, plasma exposures (Cmax) were comparable between groups but
juvenile rats had exposures 2-fold higher than those observed in young adult rats. A local
tolerance test conducted in New Zealand White rabbits was negative for drug-related findings.
There is no information on the presence of treosulfan in milk. As many drugs are secreted in
breastmilk and because treosulfan has the potential to cause serious toxicities, the label
includes a warning not to breastfeed during treatment with treosulfan and for 1 week after the
final dose. The 1-week recommendation is based on the short half-life of the drug (5 x T1/2 < 1
week).
No embryofetal developmental toxicity was conducted. Drugs that are genotoxic and target
rapidly dividing cells are expected to cause embryofetal lethality or malformations (see ICH S9).
The product label will include this information and will provide a duration of contraception
consistent with the FDA guidance Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and
Labeling Recommendations for genotoxic drugs.
From a pharmacology/ toxicology perspective, there are no outstanding issues that would
prevent the approval of treosulfan in patients undergoing allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell
(b) (4)
transplantation
with AML or MDS; however, there is one nonclinical post-marketing
requirement (PMR). During the review of the NDA, it was determined that the nonclinical data
for treosulfan submitted to the NDA in studies or literature for which the Applicant has the
right to reference did not provide convincing evidence of DNA alkylation and breaks as the
mechanism of action (MOA) of treosulfan. The PMR is to conduct an in vitro study to show
treosulfan binds to DNA and causes DNA breakage.
As the recommendation has been a complete response (see Section 1.2), the pharmacology/
toxicology discipline recommends the Applicant conduct and submit the results of the study at
the time of resubmission.

Treosulfan had myeloablative and immunosuppressive properties in studies conducted in mice.
Sjöö et al. (2006) exposed BALB/c mice to treosulfan at 1.5 g/kg/day via i.p. injection for 3
consecutive days, then conducted stem cell and immune cell assays to characterize the effects.
Stem cell quantity was rapidly depleted by post-treatment Day 1 and remained negligible
through the remainder of the experiment (post-treatment Day 12), as assessed via colony
forming units-granulocyte macrophage (CFU-GM; Error! Reference source not found., top row,
left panel). FACS analysis revealed decreases in splenic B and T cells by post-treatment Day 1,
reaching nadirs of 12.5% and 25% of controls, respectively, that remained suppressed through
post-treatment Day 12 (Error! Reference source not found., top row, middle and right panels).
Immunophenotyping showed comparable decreases in CD4+ T helper and CD8+ T killer cells,
rather than elimination of specific subpopulations (not shown). Treosulfan treatment resulted
in an increase in the percentage of IL-2-producing cells on post-treatment Days 1-3 following 6hour in vitro activation with PMA/ Ionomycin. This IL-2-producing CD3+ T-cell population
decreased to 50% of controls from post-treatment Days 6-12 (Figure 2, bottom row, left panel.
The number of splenic cells producing TNF-α remained constant (Figure 2, bottom row, middle
panel). The percentage of T cells producing IFN-γ only slightly decreased over the 12 posttreatment days (Figure 2, bottom row, right panel). Finally, a mixed lymphocyte reaction
experiment demonstrated that treosulfan-induced T-cell depletion suppressed alloreactivity in
host splenic cells (BALB/c) mixed with C57/black stimulator lymphocytes (data not shown).

Figure 2. Treosulfan Effects on Bone Marrow Stem Cells and Immune Cells

Control = green squares/ dotted line; treosulfan = blue diamonds/ solid line; busulfan = purple circles/

(Adapted from publication)

Treosulfan also had antineoplastic and antileukemic activity in several xenotransplantation
models. In one published study the Applicant referenced, NOD/SCID mice bearing
subcutaneously implanted human pediatric pre-B acute lymphoblastic leukemic (ALL) cells grew
palpable tumors (about 5 mm) in 18 days, and then received a single intraperitoneal (i.p.) bolus
injection of treosulfan at 1000, 2000, or 3000 mg/kg (Fichtner, 2003). Mice in the 1000 mg/kg
group had delayed tumor growth (~7 days) compared to the saline-treated controls. Mice that
received 2000 mg/kg had no evidence of tumors by Day 25, but tumors recurred in all mice by
Day 55. Mice that received 3000 mg/kg also did not show any presence of tumor, but 1/8 had
recurrent tumor by Day 55 (data not shown). In another Applicant-referenced study, NOD/SCID
mice bearing Ewing’s sarcoma (ES) cell line-derived tumors (CHP-100 cells) received an i.p.
bolus injection of 3000 mg/kg of treosulfan. While control mice had a mean tumor volume of
~3500 mm3 on Day 13, treosulfan-treated mice had a mean tumor volume ~100 mm3,
demonstrating activity against ES tumors in vivo (not shown).

None

Cardiovascular function
Study # CYP1269 R11-R12: Test of Treosulfan on hERG, hNav1.5, hCav1.2, hKir2.1 and
hKCNQ1/minK Cardiac Ion Channels Using Automated Patch Approach
Investigators incubated HEK293 cells stably expressing human hERG potassium channel with
treosulfan lyophilisate (200, 1000, or 5000 μM) or 0.3 μM of the reference article, E-4031, and
measured potassium current using the patch-clamp technique in a non-GLP study. For
comparison, the Cmax in adults administered a clinical dose of 10 g/m2 i.v. treosulfan is
approximately 300 μg/mL (~1000 μM). Treosulfan had no effect on hERG tail current (IC50 >
5000 μM). The positive and negative controls behaved as expected. Investigators further
evaluated the effects of 200, 1000, and 5000 μM treosulfan against additional cardiac ion
channels using the patch clamp assay: hNav1.5 (peak and late), hCav1.2, hKir2.1, and
hKCNQ1/mink. The % inhibition of each cardiac ion channel that was recorded when cells were
exposed to 5000 μM is shown in Table 3.

Treosulfan (10 μg/mL) had no effect on contractions of isolated guinea pig ileum produced by
acetylcholine or histamine (Leo, 1978b). It also had no effect on isoprenaline-induced dilation of
isolated cat tracheal muscle following exposure to concentrations ranging from 3 to 20 μg/mL.
In vivo evaluation of cardiovascular effects of treosulfan in female fasting adult mongrel dogs
showed that i.v. dosing of 5-200 mg/kg of treosulfan had no effect on arterial or venous blood
pressure, or the carotid occlusion reflex (Leo, 1978b). Moreover, 4-hour ECG evaluations in
these treosulfan-exposed dogs were unremarkable. Female adult rats exposed to i.v. doses of
treosulfan at 20 mg/kg, then 40 mg/kg 30 minutes later exhibited no changes in blood pressure.
Respiratory and CNS Function
Mice that received 100 mg/kg of treosulfan i.p. were not protected from convulsions induced
by i.v. injection of pentetrazole or from the convulsant effects induced by electroshock (Leo,
1978b). Treosulfan (50 mg/kg p.o.) offered no analgesic effect to mice following i.p. injection of
50 mg/kg of acetic acid. It also offered no conduction anesthesia to electric stimulation in tail
nerves following i.v. injection of 2% treosulfan. Ham/ICR mice administered 150 or 300 mg/kg
of treosulfan i.p. showed no signs of abnormal behavior. A fasted female dog administered 200
mg/kg of treosulfan p.o. exhibited no change in respiration, heart rate, or pupil size for 4 hours
post-dose.

5.4

ADME/PK

The conversion of treosulfan to the epoxide compound is pH-dependent, and negligible <pH 6.
As such, transformation from treosulfan to the mono- or diepoxide species, and ratios of all 3
species, will differ based on the pH in the various in vivo compartments.
Treosulfan transformation into mono- and diepoxide species

I = Treosulfan; II = (2S,3S)-1,2-expoxy-3,4-butanediol + 4-methanesulfonate (MSA);
III = (2S,3S)-diepoxybutane (DEB) + MSA
(Excerpted from Feit, 1970)

6

Clinical Pharmacology

6.1

Executive Summary

The Applicant is seeking approval of treosulfan as part of conditioning treatment prior to
allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute
myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). The proposed dosing regimen
is treosulfan 10 g/m2/day for 3 consecutive days (Days -4, -3, and -2) in combination with
fludarabine 30 mg/m2/day for 5 consecutive days (Days -6 through -2) prior to allogeneic
hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT; Day 0).
Pivotal evidence to support efficacy in the proposed population is based on the results of Study
MC-FLudT.14/L Trial II. Key review issues include the adequacy of the proposed treosulfan
dosing regimen in adult patients, pediatric extrapolation to identify a treosulfan dosing regimen
appropriate for pediatric patients >1 year of age, and evaluation of the Applicant’s proposals
for patients with organ impairment. The Applicant provided pharmacokinetic studies in adults
and pediatric patients, a population PK model of treosulfan, and a dose-response evaluation in
adults to support the dose selection in the general population and specific populations. The
Applicant did not provide adequate data to resolve other review questions including
characterization of treosulfan metabolism and effects of treosulfan on QTc interval.
Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information contained in NDA 214759.
This NDA is not approvable from a Clinical Pharmacology perspective given that the Agency was
not able to independently verify the primary endpoint supporting evidence of effectiveness
(Refer to Section 8). The key review issues with specific recommendations and comments are
summarized below.

46

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
treosulfan dose (14 g/m2) and treosulfan schedule (Days -6, -5, and -4). A planned interim
analysis of Trial I showed that the treosulfan 14 g/m2 + fludarabine arm had poorer survival and
higher infectious complications than the busulfan + fludarabine arm and the trial was stopped
early (Refer to Section 8.1.2). The differing outcomes in Trial I vs Trial II are attributed to the
difference in treosulfan dose which lead to higher incidence of early fatal and/or serious
adverse reactions.
The relationship between exposure and time to neutrophil engraftment [defined as time from
Day 0 to the first of 3 consecutive days with absolute neutrophil count (ANC) >0.5 x109/L] was
assessed in adult patients. No significant E-R relationship between treosulfan AUC versus time
to engraftment was identified; however, the adult E-R analysis had significant limitations
including a small number of patients (24), a single dose level (14 g/m2) with narrow exposure
range, and non-daily blood counts to assess ANC.
Separate exposure-response analyses for safety were conducted using data from 82 pediatric
patients treated with treosulfan (10, 12, or 14 g/m2) + fludarabine in Studies MC-FludT.16/NM
and MC-FludT.17/M. There was no significant relationship identified between treosulfan
exposure (AUC or Cmax) and common adverse events (infection and gastrointestinal disorders).
In addition, no significant relationship was identified between treosulfan exposure and duration
of neutropenia (days with ANC <0.5 x 109/L) in pediatric patients. Limitations of the pediatric ER analysis include administration of thiotepa in some (but not all) patients and non-daily blood
counts to assess ANC.
In summary, treosulfan doses higher than 10 g/m2 are not appropriate for this population due
to the risk of increased early morbidity and mortality with doses higher than recommended; do
not exceed the recommended dosage of treosulfan.
Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based
on intrinsic patient factors?
Limited pharmacokinetic data were available from adult patients treated with treosulfan. Study
MC-FludT.14/L Trial I included a PK Sub-study which collected PK samples from 24 adult
patients after administration of treosulfan 14 g/m2. PK samples were not collected in Study MCFludT.14/L Trial II. A population PK (PPK) model was developed using data from the 24 adult
patients in the Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I PK Sub-study and 83 pediatric patients treated with
treosulfan 10, 12, or 14 g/m2 in two pediatric studies (MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M).
The final PPK model was described by a two-compartment model with linear elimination and
intercompartmental clearance. BSA is included as a significant covariate on CL, V1, V2 and Q in
the final model, supporting a BSA-based dosing regimen. Given the variability of treosulfan PK
parameters (CV% 16.7% for AUC24h and 19.2% for Vd), the proposed treosulfan dosing regimen
of 10 g/m2 is acceptable. Refer to the Pharmacometrics review in Section 16.4.1 for detailed
review of the final PPK model.
52

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
There were no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of treosulfan based on
sex, mild renal impairment (CLcr 60-89 mL/min), or mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin less
than or equal to ULN with AST greater than ULN or total bilirubin greater than 1 to 1.5 times
ULN with any AST).
Pediatric Patients: Given the need for alternatives in pediatric patients, information regarding
an appropriate dose of treosulfan for pediatric patients was evaluated. The Applicant provided
safety and PK data from 115 pediatric patients administered treosulfan prior to alloHSCT in the
following two clinical studies:
• MC-FludT.16/NM: open-label, randomized (1:1) study of treosulfan + fludarabine (n=46)
vs busulfan + fludarabine (n=45) prior to alloHSCT in 91 pediatric patients (age range 4
months to 17 years) with non-malignant diseases. 82.5% (75/88) of treated patients
received an intensified regimen with the addition of thiotepa.
• MC-FludT.17/M: open-label, single-arm study of treosulfan + fludarabine prior to
alloHSCT in 70 pediatric patients (age range 4 months to 17 years) with hematological
malignancies. 92.8% (65/70) of patients received an intensified regimen with the
addition of thiotepa.
In both pediatric studies, the Applicant selected the dose of treosulfan by matching the
treosulfan AUC24h in adults following the 14 g/m2 dosing regimen.

(b) (4)

. Refer to
Section 7.1 for additional description of the two pediatric studies and Section 8.3.6 for a
comparison of safety outcomes for pediatric patients vs adults enrolled in Trial II.
Extrapolation of the proposed indication in adults to include pediatric patients >1 year was
evaluated during the review of this application as an approach to support a pediatric indication.
To support a pediatric full extrapolation approach, FDA requested that the Applicant identify
pediatric doses that would match treosulfan AUC24h in adults after administration of treosulfan
10 g/m2. Given the increased morbidity and mortality observed in adults treated with
(b) (4)
treosulfan 14 g/m2/day x 3 days,
this
higher exposure is not acceptable for a pediatric extrapolation approach. The Applicant utilized
their population PK model to conduct simulations of treosulfan AUC24h after administration of
different doses (8 to 11 g/m2) to pediatric patients with BSA ranging from 0.25 to 2.25 m2 with
patients categorized into three groups based on BSA: <0.7 m2, 0.7 to 1.1 m2, and >1.1 m2. Based
on the Applicant’s simulation results (Error! Reference source not found.), the Applicant
proposed the following dose levels in pediatric patients to obtain comparable treosulfan AUC24h
to an average adult after administration of treosulfan 10 g/m2:
• 9.0 g/m2 if BSA ≤ 0.7 m2 or in the age range 1 to 4 years
• 9.5 g/m2 if 0.7 m2 < BSA ≤ 1.1 m2 or in the age range 5 to 9 years
• 10.0 g/m2 if BSA > 1.1 m2 or in the age range 10 to 18 years

Grafapex (treosulfan)
Table 4. Simulated AUC24h for different age and BSA values by dose regimen. Highlighted
dosing regimens are equal or higher than target AUC24h of average American adult

Source: Applicant population PK study report 2021

Independent simulations conducted by FDA compared the treosulfan AUC24h among different
BSA groups and dosing regimens to evaluate the Applicant’s proposed pediatric dosing
regimens. The simulation results, shown in Figure 3 indicate that the Applicant’s proposed
pediatric dosing regimens are expected to provide similar treosulfan AUC24h compared to the
median treosulfan AUC24h in adult patients after administration of treosulfan 10 g/m2 (target
exposure).
The administration of treosulfan 10 g/m2 in pediatric patients with BSA ≤ 0.7 m2, 0.7 to 1.1 m2,
and >1.1 m2 is predicted to result in treosulfan AUC24h 11.2% higher, 4.7% higher, and 2.8%
lower, respectively, compared to the target exposure in adults (
Figure 3). As such, a simplified dosing regimen of 10 g/m2 for the entire pediatric population
with BSA range of 0.25 to 2.25 m2 is not expected to provide a clinically significant difference in
exposure compared to the target exposure in adults.

Grafapex (treosulfan)

Source: reviewer’s analysis

Exposure-response analyses for safety based on the two pediatric studies did not indicate that
an 11.2% increase in exposure would result significant increase in the probability of common
toxicities (infections and gastrointestinal disorders) given the variability of the drug exposure.
Therefore, for the convenience of dosing and to avoid potential dosing errors with the
Applicant’s proposed dosing regimens, FDA recommends a treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2 for all
pediatric patients ≥1 year.
Geriatric Patients: Pharmacokinetic differences between patients <65 years of age compared to
patients >65 years of age were evaluated using data from the Trial I PK Sub-study. The
geometric mean AUC24h (1,126 µg*h/mL for <65 years vs 1,225 µg*h/mL for >65 years) and Cmax
(554 µg/mL for <65 years vs 556 µg/mL for >65 years) were similar between age groups. Given
the small number of patients >65 years of age with pharmacokinetic data (n=4), the effects of
geriatric age on treosulfan PK were inconclusive. In addition, in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II there
were no significant differences in treosulfan safety or effectiveness observed between patients
65 years of age or older (n=73) and younger adult patients (n=197) on the treosulfan +
fludarabine arm. Refer to Section 8.3.6 for additional information.
Renal Impairment: Treosulfan is excreted in the urine (median 42% recovery unchanged). The
effects of renal impairment on treosulfan PK were evaluated using PK data from the Trial I PK
Sub-study. A trend towards lower treosulfan clearance and higher treosulfan AUC was observed
55

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
in patients with moderate renal impairment (CLcr 30 to <60 mL/min; Figure 4). However, the
observation was limited by the small number of patients with moderate renal impairment
(n=2). No patients with severe renal impairment (CLcr <30 mL/min) were enrolled in the Trial I
PK Sub-study.

The effects of renal impairment on treosulfan safety were evaluated using data from Trial II. No
PK samples were collected in Trial II; therefore, creatinine clearance was utilized as a surrogate
for treosulfan exposure in subgroup safety analyses. CLcr was a statistically significant predictor
of non-relapse mortality (NRM) at 24 months. In addition, a trend towards increased safety
events [including infection-related mortality (IRM) at 24 months, Grade 3+ infection, and Grade
3+ GI toxicity] was observed for patients with renal impairment (Table 5). The evaluation of
safety was limited by the small number of patients with moderate renal impairment (n=3). No
patients with severe renal impairment were enrolled in Trial II.

Given the urinary excretion and observed trends in both PK and safety in patients with renal
impairment, the Applicant should conduct a renal impairment study to identify a safe dose of
treosulfan in patients with moderate renal impairment. Given the rarity of performing alloHSCT
56

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
in patients with severe renal impairment, a study in this population would not be feasible and
will therefore not be required.
Hepatic Impairment: The effects of hepatic impairment on treosulfan safety were evaluated
using data from Trial II. There was no apparent trend in safety events for patients with hepatic
impairment categorized per NCI-ODWG (Table 6). However, the evaluation of safety was
limited by the small number of patients with moderate hepatic impairment (n=4). No patients
with severe hepatic impairment were enrolled in Trial II as these patients are generally not
considered appropriate candidates for allogeneic HSCT. No dose modification is required for
patients with mild hepatic impairment.
