Race and Ethnicity: The Applicant did not collect information on race or ethnicity from Study
MC-FludT14.L Trial I, including the PK sub-study. Race was recorded for pediatric patients in
Studies MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M; however, of the 83 pediatric patients with PK
data, 80 were White, 2 were Asian, and 1 was Black. Therefore, the effects of race and ethnicity
on treosulfan PK are unknown given that there were too few patients with differing races to
assess the effects of race on treosulfan PK. Additional information will be necessary to evaluate
these effects.
Are there clinically relevant food‐drug or drug‐drug interactions, and what is the appropriate
management strategy?
Treosulfan was evaluated in vitro as an inhibitor or inducer of major CYP enzymes and as an
inhibitor of major transporters. Separate in vitro stability studies were conducted to evaluate
the relative concentration of treosulfan and its active metabolites under conditions used in the
in vitro CYP and transporter studies. Based on the stability studies, both treosulfan and its
active metabolites are present in vitro and the relative concentrations change over time
(reduction in parent treosulfan with corresponding increase in metabolites). Therefore, it is not
possible to determine the contribution of each moiety to the observed results.
CYP Enzymes:
In vitro, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits CYP2C19 and CYP3A at
clinically relevant concentrations. For CYP2C19, the treosulfan reversible IC50 was 972 µM and
for CYP3A, the treosulfan reversible IC50 was 1,870 µM (calculated based on initial treosulfan
57

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
concentration). A combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, or CYP2D6 at clinically relevant concentrations (IC50 >10,000 µM
based on initial treosulfan concentration).
Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modelling was used to predict the effects of
treosulfan administration on CYP3A, CYP2C19, and P-gp substrates in vivo. The PBPK model and
mechanistic static model were used to predict the magnitude of enzyme mediated DDI caused
by treosulfan and its metabolite, EBDM, respectively. Results of the analysis showed that the
potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor and a CYP2C19 inhibitor cannot be excluded.
Refer to Section 16.4.2 for detailed review of the Applicant’s PBPK model.
Given the short half-life of treosulfan (2.2 hrs) and context of use (3-day course), no in vivo DDI
study is required. For certain CYP3A or CYP2C19 substrates where minimal concentration
changes may lead to serious or life-threatening toxicities providers should monitor for adverse
reactions and reduce the dosage, as needed, if recommended in the prescribing information of
these substrates.
Evaluation of treosulfan as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A in human hepatocytes
was inconclusive due to cytotoxicity caused by incubation of treosulfan.
Transporter Systems:
In vitro, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites inhibits P-gp. At an initial
treosulfan concentration of 3,000 µM, treosulfan incubation inhibited P-gp mediated transport
of loperamide by 51.1% (IC50 ~3,000 µM). Based on PBPK analysis, the potential DDI of
treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor cannot be excluded; however, the risk of clinically significant DDIs
is low.
In vitro, a combined sample of treosulfan and its metabolites does not inhibit BCRP, BSEP,
MATE1, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, or OCT2 at clinically relevant concentrations
(no concentration-dependent inhibition up to at least 3,000 µM initial treosulfan
concentration).
Has the cardiac safety of treosulfan, including effects on the QT interval, been adequately
characterized?
In vitro, treosulfan does not significantly inhibit hERG at a concentration of 1,000 µM. The
Applicant has not systematically evaluated the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in
patients after administration of treosulfan. A total of 613 patients have received treosulfan in
trials of treosulfan as part of a preparative regimen prior to transplantation. Post-baseline ECGs
were available from only one study (MC-FludT.6/L). Two of 35 evaluable patients had a change
to abnormal ECG post-baseline (one asymptomatic T-wave negativity and one sick sinus/atrial
fibrillation).
58

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Given the mechanism of action (alkylating agent) and context of use (preparative regimen prior
to alloHSCT) for treosulfan, a thorough QT study in healthy volunteers is not feasible. A QT
study designed to exclude large mean increases (>20 msec) in the QT/QTc interval should be
conducted to evaluate the effects of treosulfan on the QT interval in patients receiving the
recommended dosage of treosulfan.

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
The applicant submitted information from 7 clinical trials of treosulfan, in combination with
fludarabine, as preparative conditioning regimen for allogeneic HSCT. The pivotal trial MCFludT.14/L II was relevant to estimating efficacy in the intended population. In addition, study
MC-FludT.14/L I was used to support efficacy. The registry studies from EBMT and CIBMTR were
evaluated for supporting the biological activity.
Studies MC-FludT.6.L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, MC-FludT.14/L II,
MC-FludT.16/NM, and MC-FludT.17/M were included in the integrated summary of safety
submitted by the Applicant. We performed the safety analysis on MC-FludT.6L, MCFludT.7/AML, MC-FLudT.8/MDS, MC-FludT.14/L I, and MC-FludT.14/L II for the adult
population, and on MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M for the pediatric safety analysis.
Summaries of data and safety analyses by the clinical reviewer were performed using JMP
version 15.0 (SAS Institute, Inc., Cary, NC) and MedDRA Adverse Events Diagnostic (MAED, FDA,
Silver Spring, MD), versions 2.5 (through 3/21/2021) and 3.0 (after 3/21/20201). Summaries of
laboratory data were performed using JMP version 15.0 and Palantir (Palantir Technologies,
Inc., Denver, CO, ©2017-2021). Postmarket analysis was conducted using Empirica Signal
(Oracle, Santa Clara, CA, ©2014).

8

Statistical and Clinical Evaluation

8.1

Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy

8.1.1

Study MC-FLudT-14/L Trial II

"Clinical Phase III Trial to Compare Treosulfan-Based Conditioning Therapy With BusulfanBased Reduced-Intensity Conditioning (RIC) Prior to Allogeneic Haematopoietic Stem Cell
Transplantation in Patients with AML Or MDS Considered Ineligible To Standard Conditioning
Regimens - Trial II"
INVESTIGATIONAL PLAN
Trial Design
Design and Objectives
The study was designed as a non-inferiority Phase III trial to compare treosulfan-based
conditioning therapy to busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning therapy in adult patients
with high-risk hematologic malignancies. Patients were randomized between the two arms of
the study.

62

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
The primary endpoint of the study was event-free survival at 2 years after transplantation.
Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death.
Secondary endpoints included overall survival (OS), incidence of relapse (RI), non-relapse
mortality (NRM), and transplant-related mortality (TRM) at 2 years after transplantation.
Additional secondary objectives included CTCAE grade III+ mucositis between days -6 and +28,
engraftment at day +28, donor chimerism at days +28 and +100, acute and chronic GvHD within
2 years of transplantation, and other CTCAE grade III/IV events between days -6 and +28.
Key Eligibility Criteria
The study population consisted of adult patients with AML or high risk MDS who were not
eligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. The study defined this group of patients as
patients who were at least 50 years of age at the time of HSCT or with an HCT-CI score > 2.
Karnofsky performance score was at least 60%.
Treatment Plan Overview
Patients were randomized to receive either RIC with the control (reference) arm of busulfan +
fludarabine, or the test arm of treosulfan + fludarabine (Table 8).
Table 8. Treatment Overview of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II

All patients received fludarabine (30 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -2. Patients on the control arm
received busulfan (0.8 mg/kg) on days -4 and -3. Patients receiving busulfan received antiseizure prophylaxis. Patients on the treatment arm received treosulfan (10 mg/m2/d) on days -4
to -2. Additionally, patients who received a MUD received ATG. The type of ATG and dosing
varied by country.
GVHD prophylaxis was identical in both arms. All patients received level-adapted cyclosporine
from Day -1 through Day +100 and methotrexate on days +1, +3, and +6, with folic acid rescue.

Continuous documentation

Statistical Analysis Plan
The submitted SAP was version 7.0, dated Dec. 03, 2019.
Analysis Set
Full analysis set (FAS): all randomized patients who were treated with at least one dose of study
medication and had at least one efficacy parameter documented after baseline. The patients
within the FAS were to be analyzed with respect to the groups into which they were
randomized (intention to treat principle). Sponsor’s analyses were based on FAS.
Start point for Time-to-event Endpoints
Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation.
Stat Reviewer’s Comment: This FAS population used by Sponsor is not an ITT population. FDA
re-did all analyses based on the ITT population and started from time of randomization.
64

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Sample Size Determination
The noninferiority margin is HR=1.3. No hazard rate of the control arm and accrual rate was
specified in the SAP. According to Page 61 of the DMC Charter, at 1-sided α=2.5%, at most 481
events or 930 patients would be enrolled. The trial would have a power of 80%. Assuming that
roughly 3% of patients have to be excluded from FAS, at most 960 patients would be enrolled.
Clinical TL Comment: In the document "Internal Report. Estimating the treatment effect of
busulfan-based conditioning to justify the non-inferiority margin of the phase III study MCFludT.14/L Trial II" (February 27, 2020) in Module 5.3.5.3, the Applicant provide several cases
of published comparative analyses or randomized studies of HSCT vs chemotherapy for EFS,
RFS, and OS (using various definitions of each) for multiple different populations of patients
with AML or MDS. No particular publication was identified as having a population
exchangeable with that in Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. This document does not provide
justification for the NI margin prespecified in this trial.
Stat Reviewer’s Comment: Under IND 054552, in the Meeting Minutes of Type B Pre-NDA
Meeting held on Apr 02 2020, FDA requested that the report justifying the noninferiority
margin of MC-FludT.14/L (Trial II) should be placed in the Module 5.3.5.4. The Sponsor
clarified that the noninferiority margin was prespecified and cannot be changed. The Agency
acknowledged the Sponsor’s clarifications on the noninferiority margin and reiterated that
the noninferiority margin use and interpretation in efficacy assessment would be a review
issue.
In the NDA submission, no report justifying the noninferiority margin was submitted. However,
because FDA could not independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS for Study MCFludT.14/L II, review was focused on the OS analysis. Therefore, no IR was issued for EFS
noninferiority related issues.
Analysis of Efficacy Endpoints
Primary:
Kaplan-Meier methods was used to calculate the 12-and 24-month EFS rate and the associated
95% CIs. Noninferiority was tested using a Cox proportional hazards regression model stratified
by center with donor type (MUD vs. MRD), risk group (I vs. II) and treatment as factors. The
three factors in the Cox model were stratification factors used in the randomization procedure.
Stat Reviewer’s Comment: FDA recommends stratified log-rank test for testing superiority and
Cox model stratified by stratification factors at randomization to estimate HR and 95% CI.
Secondary:
The Kaplan-Meier method was used to estimate survival probabilities and 95% CIs. The
unstratified log-rank test was used to test for a difference between the two treatment arms.
65

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Cox models similar to the model used for primary endpoint analysis would be used to estimate
the hazard ratio (HR) and 95% CIs.
Interim Analyses for Efficacy
Three interim analyses were pre-specified in the study protocol, which allowed to stop the trial
as soon as non-inferiority has been demonstrated. The interim analyses were scheduled based
on a combination of number of patients and number of events. O’Brien-Fleming boundary was
used.
If significant non-inferiority could be shown at interim or final analysis, the study would stop
enrollment and superiority with respect to the primary study endpoint would be sequentially
tested.

Financial Disclosure
The financial disclosure form for MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was submitted with the application. No
investigators with disclosable financial interests were identified.
Data Quality and Integrity
The quality and integrity of the submitted data were sufficient to review the application for the
secondary endpoint of overall survival. Insufficient data were provided to independently verify
the primary endpoint of event free survival at 2 years.
Patient Disposition
Five hundred seventy patients were randomized to busulfan or treosulfan arms (Figure 5).
Following randomization, subjects in each arm did not receive study drug, primarily due to
screening failures. In addition, two patients in the treatment arm were not evaluable for
efficacy endpoints as they did not receive HSCT. The full analysis set is comprised of 283
patients in the busulfan arm and 268 patients in the treosulfan arm.

Figure 5. Distribution of Patients in MC-FludT.L/14 Trial II

Source: Reviewer’s Analysis, Adapted from MC-FludT.14/L II Clinical Trial Report

Protocol Violations/Deviations
The clinical trial report contained reports of 490 of 570 patients with reported deviations (86%
of total patients). Of these, 201 of 570 patients (35.3%) had a major protocol deviation. Slightly
greater than 17% of patients had a procedural deviation, with missing evaluations at different
timepoints. There were 32 patients (5.6%) with treatment deviations. Table 13 further
characterizes the categories of major protocol deviations.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Conditioning preparative regimens were administered at the research sites by qualified study
personnel. Patients did not receive any experimental treatments within 4 weeks prior to the
study. Concomitant medications for GvHD prophylaxis were prescribed in the trial; all other
prophylactic and concomitant medications were recorded in the CRF.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
The study was designed as an NI study with the option to test superiority at the final analysis.
Enrollment was stopped at the second interim analysis. The final analysis was based on 48.6%
information. Superiority of Treosulfan was not achieved at the second interim analysis but was
at the last analysis, which included 570 patients and 234 events. While the trial showed
treosulfan was superior to the control with respect to the primary endpoint at the final analysis,
EFS [HR was 0.64, (0.49, 0.84)], there are reporting issues that cause FDA to question the
usefulness of the result. The lack of ‘Day +100 bone marrow’ reports calls the EFS evaluation
into question. FDA was not able to independently verify the primary endpoint of EFS.
Therefore, efficacy review and analyses were focused on the overall survival, which was found
to be supportive.
EFS
The first interim analysis happened at 40 EFS events, and DMC recommended to continue
enrollment. One hundred sixty-four events happened at the second interim analysis. Noninferiority was achieved with 476 patients, and enrollment was stopped. The final analysis
included 168 events. Superiority of Treosulfan was not achieved at the second interim analysis
but was at the last analysis, which included 570 patients and 234 events.
Table 16. Actual Stopping boundaries for efficacy and futility monitoring shows the actual
stopping boundaries for efficacy and futility monitoring. Because noninferiority margin was
achieved, the trial stopped at 48.6% information. As well studied in literature, results of early
interim analyses tend to overestimate treatment efficacy. This early stop may result in
insufficient follow-up.

OS
FDA uses a Cox model stratified by the stratification factors of randomization to calculate the
hazard ratio (HR) and 95% confidence interval (CI). All analyses are conducted on the ITT
population, which includes all randomized patients according to their assigned treatment at
randomization. The starting point of OS is time of randomization, not time of transplant.
The median (range) of follow-up time was 29.5 months (0-54.8) months as determined by
reverse Kaplan-Meier method. The HR is 0.65 (0.48, 0.88). Figure 6 shows the Kaplan-Meier
Curve for OS in ITT population.

Clinical TL Review Comment: The endpoints based on hematopoietic recovery, chimerism and
nonrelapse mortality are safety issues rather than efficacy issues. GRFS and CRFS represent a
mixture of safety and efficacy data. The relapse/progression endpoint might be considered as
supportive of efficacy, but the results of analyses using cumulative incidence with competing
risks are difficult to interpret when the incidence of competing risks is so high. As such, none
of the prespecified secondary endpoint provide information to address a determination of
efficacy. Since the patients with MDS were transplanted when not in remission, achievement
of remission might reflect effectiveness, but this was not a prespecified endpoint; however,
results of marrow examinations which would allow for assessment of response were not
collected systematically, so response cannot be assessed.
There were no efficacy-related patient-reported outcomes submitted for review.
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
All FDA analyses were based on the ITT population unless specifically annotated.
Boundary Analysis for OS
There was no alpha-allocation plan for OS or any other secondary endpoints. Therefore, the
76

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
significance of OS is hard to interpret. All p-values are nominal and cannot be included in
labeling since there was no pre-specified plan to control multiplicity.
To facilitate decision making, we calculated O’Brien-Fleming (OBF) boundaries and Pocock
boundaries using the information fraction of EFS. OBF assigns more weight toward later
analysis, while Pocock boundary assigns alpha to each analysis equally. Therefore, OBF is more
conservative compared to Pocock and recommended in analyses for primary and key secondary
endpoints.
For comparison, in a hypothetical well-controlled trial with EFS as primary endpoint and OS as
key secondary endpoint, the fixed sequence testing procedure at each prespecified interim
analysis would be to test EFS first, if EFS achieved statistical significance according to the OBF
boundary, then test OS at the same significance level. Because this trial was designed as a
noninferiority test and used the margin of HR=1.3, which was more liberal than what was
usually recommended by FDA, it stopped early at 48.6% information of EFS. Although not
prespecified, information fraction of OS at the stopping point would be even smaller. We then
applied the less strict Pocock boundary to compare with significance level from OBF.
From this boundary analysis, we wanted to see how significant the OS results would be
assuming the same information fraction as EFS at the time points of interim analyses. Please
note these boundaries are exploratory only and based on a hypothetical exercise.
We conducted a stratified log-rank test for OS at the last analysis. The p-value was 0.0049. At
this point, the OBF boundary is 0.0025 and the Pocock boundary is 0.014. The OS results
crossed the Pocock boundary, but not the OBF boundary. Therefore, there would have been
different conclusions regarding statistical significance based on the different methods. As
previously specified, the OBF boundary is more conservative and assigns more weight toward
later analysis, while the Pocock boundary assigns weights equally to early and late analyses.
These mixed results suggest that it is not clear if OS would have been considered to be
significant; however, no definitive conclusions can be made based on the OS results because
the multiplicity approach was not prespecified.

Subgroup Analysis for OS
We conducted an exploratory subgroup OS analysis to investigate if there is any detrimental
trend in the subgroups that may raise safety concerns. Table 22 summarizes the HR and 95%
CI of OS in these interested subgroups by treatment arms. Figure 9 is a forest plot that
demonstrating OS results in both the subgroups and the overall population. The same Cox
model for OS analysis in the overall population was applied here. The overall HR is 0.65 (0.48,
0.88). In the subgroups, most HRs are less than 1 except for the low or very low risk MDS and
the MRD donor type group. However, the sample size of these two groups are relatively small
and the 95% CI of HRs cover 1. There was no CI that could indicate a clearly detrimental effect
in any subgroup because all confidence intervals were fairly wide. This demonstrates the
uncertainty in the hazard ratio estimates presented.

Analysis of Trial Conduct
To further understand the quality of OS data, we performed the following analyses:
• Informative Censoring for OS
In this analysis we reversed the censoring indicator for death, which means censoring is
now counted as event and death as censoring. We then used Kaplan-Meier method to
make a reversed censoring survival curve for the two treatment arms. If there is any
informative censoring in one of the treatment arms, the survival curves will separate
from each other. Figure 10 shows the reversed K-M curve for OS in the ITT population,
which does not indicate that informative censoring is present.
Figure 10. Reversed censoring curve for OS in ITT population

Source: FDA Analysis
80

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
•

Time from randomization to transplantation
Since the indication is conditioning treatment before transplantation, we wanted to
assess if there was any difference in time from randomization to transplantation in
different treatment arms. Table 24 summarizes days between randomization and
transplantation by treatment arms. Figure 11 shows the histogram of days from
randomization to transplantation by treatment arms. To further compare difference in
the two treatment arms, we made a Kaplan-Meier curve for transplantation. In this
curve, we treated transplantation as event, and calculated time from randomization to
transplantation. Patients who did not receive transplant were censored at the last visit.
Figure 12 shows the results, which does not indicate any imbalance in the distribution of
time from randomization to transplantation between the two arms.

8.1.2 Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial I
"Clinical Phase III Trial To Compare Treosulfan-Based Conditioning Therapy With BusulfanBased Reduced-Intensity Conditioning (RIC) Prior To Allogeneic Haematopoietic Stem Cell
Transplantation In Patients With AML Or MDS Considered Ineligible To Standard
Conditioning Regimens - Trial I"
INVESTIGATIONAL PLAN
Trial Design
Design and Objectives
The study was designed as a non-inferiority Phase III trial to compare treosulfan-based
conditioning therapy to busulfan-based reduced-intensity conditioning therapy in adult patients
with high-risk hematologic malignancies. Patients were randomized between the two arms of
the study.
The primary endpoint of the study was event-free survival at 2 years after transplantation.
Events were defined as disease relapse, graft failure, or death.
Secondary endpointss included overall survival (OS), incidence of relapse (RI), non-relapse
mortality (NRM), and transplant-related mortality (TRM) at 2 years after transplantation.
Additional secondary objectives included CTCAE grade III+ mucositis between days -6 and +28,
engraftment at day +28, donor chimerism at days +28 and +100, acute and chronic GvHD within
2 years of transplantation, and other CTCAE grade III/IV events between days -6 and +28.
Key Eligibility Criteria
The study population consisted of adult patients with AML or high risk MDS who were not
eligible for myeloablative conditioning regimens. The study defined this group of patients as
patients who were at least 50 years of age at the time of HSCT or with an HCT-CI score > 2.
Karnofsky performance score was at least 60%.
Treatment Plan Overview
Patients were randomized to receive either RIC with the control (reference) arm of busulfan +
fludarabine, or the test arm of treosulfan + fludarabine (Table 26). All patients received
fludarabine (30 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -2. Patients on the control arm received busulfan (0.8
mg/kg) on days -4 and -3. Patients receiving busulfan received anti-seizure prophylaxis. Patients
on the treatment arm received treosulfan (10 mg/m2/d) on days -6 to -4. Additionally, patients
who received a MUD received ATG. The type of ATG and dosing varied by country.

Financial Disclosure
The financial disclosure form for MC-FludT.14/L Trial II was submitted with the application. No

Data Quality and Integrity
The quality and integrity of the submitted data were sufficient to review the application for the
secondary endpoint of overall survival and safety.
Patient Disposition
Three hundred thirty patients were randomized to busulfan or treosulfan arms (Figure 13).
Following randomization, subjects in each arm did not receive study drug. Following treatment
administration 1 patient in the control arm and two patients in the treatment arm were not
evaluable for efficacy endpoints. The evaluable patient set is comprised of 112 patients in the
busulfan arm and 112 patients in the treosulfan arm.

Protocol Violations/Deviations
The clinical study report contained reports of 236 of 330 patients with reported protocol
deviations (72% of total patients) (Table 30). Of these, 120 (75.5%) were treated with busulfan
and 116 (67.8%) were treated with treosulfan. Of note, there were more deviations regarding
administration of the investigational drug product in patients receiving busulfan (12.6%) than in
patients receiving treosulfan (3.5%). Six patients did not receive any busulfan, and one patient
did not receive any treosulfan.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Conditioning preparative regimens were administered at the research sites by qualified study
personnel. Patients did not receive any experimental treatments within 4 weeks prior to the
study. Concomitant medications for GvHD prophylaxis were prescribed in the trial; all other
prophylactic and concomitant medications were recorded in the CRF.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
The trial discontinued after planned interim analysis. It was determined that dose should be
lower in this elderly population because neutropenic phase was much longer than control. A
higher mortality was also found in the Treosulfan group. As early as 28 days after
transplantation, 7 patients (4.2%) of the Treosulfan treatment group, and none of the patients
of the Busulfan treatment group had died.
As stated in the clinical report: “Consistently with the longer duration of neutropenia in patients
receiving Treosulfan, a higher frequency of patients with CTCAE Grade III/IV infections was
observed in the Treosulfan treatment group (54.8% vs. 36.2%). This observation is also reflected
in the higher frequency of SAEs reported for the Treosulfan treatment group (11.3% vs. 5.9%),
most notably infections (6.5% vs. 2.0%). Likewise, the cumulative incidence of infection related
TRM on Day +100 was higher in the Treosulfan treatment group (7.9%) compared to the
Busulfan treatment group (4.0%, one-sided p=0.0630, adjusted for donor type, risk group and
centre using Cox regression model).
The Treosulfan group had a higher cumulative incidence of NRM and a significantly higher
cumulative incidence of TRM on Day +100 for patients receiving Treosulfan (10.2%) compared
to patients receiving Busulfan (5.3%, p=0.077).”
Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
Not applicable.

Subpopulations
Because this trial was stopped due to safety concern, subpopulation analysis on OS was

(95%) of OS by selected subgroups. In this trial, treosulfan had detrimental effect on OS. This
effect was consistent within analyzed subgroups and no significantly larger detrimental effect
was found in the subgroup analysis.

8.1.3 Registry Studies
8.1.3.1 Retrospective EBMT-PD/IE-WP analysis:
Re-analysis of EBMT-registry data on Fludarabine/Melphalan and Busulfan/
Cyclophosphamide based conditioning treatment compared to Fludarabine/Treosulfan based
conditioning of adult AML and MDS patients treated in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II phase III by
matched pairs.
Key study objectives were comparison of OS, relapse (RI), and NRM, all at 2 years after alloHSCT. Comparison patients (N = 1493) received conditioning with either Flu-Mel or Bu/Cy
without TBI. The MC-FludT.14/L Trial II cohort consisted of 252 patients, with patients < 50
years of age excluded from the analysis. The EBMT patient cohort had a lower HCT-CI score,
higher Karnofsky score, lower recipient age, and higher donor age than the MC-FludT.14/L Trial
II cohort. Four matched comparisons were performed as follows:
1.
2.
3.
4.

MDS: Flu/Mel vs Flu/Treo
MDS: Bu/Cy vs Flu/Treo
AML: Flu/Mel vs Flu/Treo
AML: Bu/Cy vs Flu/Treo

In MDS, Flu/Treo was statistically superior to Bu/Cy in OS. In MDS, no other comparisons were
statistically significant. In AML, Flu/Treo was statistically superior to Flu/Mel and Bu/Cy in OS
and NRM.
Multivariable sensitivity analyses were also performed. In MDS, Flu/Treo again was statistically
superior to Bu/Cy, with no other analyses reaching significance. In AML, Flu/Treo was
statistically superior to Flu/Mel and Bu/Cy in NRM and OS. Relapse incidence was comparable
among groups.
Stat Reviewer’s Comment: Patient level data were not submitted. Therefore, FDA could not
independently verify the results.
8.1.3.2 CIBMTR SC17-04b
Comparison of allogeneic transplantation outcomes for acute myeloid leukemia and
myelodysplastic syndrome with full or less intensity busulfan-fludarabine regimens and
busulfan-cyclophosphamide regimen in the United States to a treosulfan-containing regimen
in a medac-sponsored European clinical trial MC-FludT.14/L (Trial II).
The treosulfan “trial” population is identical to the population above in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (N
= 268). The comparison population included patients from 96 transplant centers. Conditioning
regimens for this analysis included the following:
92

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
RIC:
•
•

Busulfan + fludarabine (Flu/Bu2) (N = 318)
Flu/Bu + ATG (N = 199)

MAC
•
•
•

Busulfan + fludarabine (Flu/Bu4) (N = 327)
Flu/B4 + ATG (N = 169)
Busulfan + cyclophosphamide (Cy/Bu4) (N = 317)

The primary endpoint for the analysis was 2-year OS. Secondary endpoints included 2-year DFS,
2-year EFS, relapse, TRM/NRM, and incidence of aGVHD and cGVHD. There were no
differences in HCT-CI scores across the groups. Cy/Bu4 was more likely to be given to younger
patients than the other regimens. OS, DFS, and EFS were higher for patients in MC-FludT.14/L
Trial II than those in the CIBMTR database. This is likely due to higher relapse rates with the RIC
and Flu/B4 + ATG regimens and higher TRM with the other MAC regimens. aGVHD did not differ
between the groups. cGVHD was higher in the trial regimen when compared to the RIC and
ATG-containing regimens but was comparable to the rate observed in the Cy/Bu4 population.
Stat Reviewer’s Comment: Patient level data were not submitted. Therefore, FDA could not
independently verify the results.
8.1.4 Additional Studies in AML or MDS
MC-FludT.6/L
Allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with advanced hematological
malignancies after treosulfan-based conditioning therapy - A clinical phase II study
Study MC-FludT.6/L was a multicenter single-arm dose-escalation trial to test the tolerability of
treosulfan and fludarabine as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched
sibling, 1 antigen-mismatched sibling, or HLA-matched unrelated donor. Eligible patients were
>= 18 years old with a "chemosensitive" hematological malignancy indicated for HSCT but who
were considered at high risk for toxicities with standard MAC regimens. Patients were treated
in sequential cohorts (n=15 each) at treosulfan dose levels of 10 gm/m2, 12 gm/m2, or 14
gm/m2 (see Table 12 for details of the regimen). The aim of the protocol was to determine a
dose and schedule for later trials. The primary efficacy endpoint was hematopoietic recovery.
Overall survival was an additional endpoint.
There were 55 patients accrued, including 19 with AML and 6 with MDS among the various
dose cohorts. The median age was 50 years (range, 18 - 66 years); 60% were male and 40%
were female. Table 34 shows the selected efficacy outcomes by dose as reported by the
Applicant.

The Applicant concluded that efficacy and safety of the combination of treosulfan and

MC-FludT.7/AML
Clinical phase II trial to evaluate the safety and efficacy of treosulfan based conditioning prior
to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with acute myeloid
leukaemia
Study MC-FludT.7/AML was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine
as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling or HLA-matched
unrelated donor. Eligible patients were 18-60 years old with AML indicated for HSCT. The
preparative regimen consisted of treosulfan at a dose level of 14 gm/m2 in combination with
fludarabine (see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was "evaluation of
engraftment". Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The target accrual was 75 patients.
The sample size was calculated to allow for at least 65 patients eligible to assess the safety
outcomes in comparison to historical data using busulfan. Seventy-five patients were enrolled.
The median age was 45 years (range, 19 - 59 years); 51% were male and 49% were female. At
study entry, 80% were in first CR and the remainder in later CR. The Applicant reported that
neutrophil recovery at Day 28 was achieved by 93%, complete chimerism at Day 100 was
achieved by 92%, Day-100 nonrelapse mortality was 3%, and overall survival at 1 year was 77%
(Study MC-FludT.7/AML Clinical Study Report Tables 11.4.1.1A, 11.4.1.2A, 14.2.4A, and
14.2.6A). The Applicant concluded that the regimen "has myeloablative potential as
demonstrated by the fast and durable eradication of the physiologic recipient haematopoiesis.
Long-term outcome data are in good accordance with data from the literature on AML
transplantation after standard or myeloablative conditioning. " (Study MC-FludT.7/AML Clinical
Study Report Section 13).

MC-FludT.8/MDS
Clinical phase II trial to evaluate the safety and efficacy of treosulfan based conditioning prior
to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in patients with myelodysplastic
syndrome (MDS)
Study MC-FludT.8/MDS was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine
as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched sibling or HLA-matched
unrelated donor. Eligible patients were 18-60 years old with MDS indicated for HSCT. The
preparative regimen consisted of treosulfan at a dose level of 14 gm/m2 in combination with
fludarabine (see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was "evaluation of
engraftment". Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The target accrual was 45 patients.
The sample size was calculated to allow for assessment of safety outcomes in comparison to
historical data using busulfan. Forty-six patients were enrolled; one patient was subsequently
diagnosed as AML and excluded from the outcome analyses. The median age was 50 years
(range, 22 - 63 years); 47% were male and 53% were female. At study entry, 47% had excess
blasts. The Applicant reported that neutrophil recovery at Day 28 was achieved by 96%,
complete chimerism at Day 100 was achieved by 93%, Day-100 nonrelapse mortality was 9%,
and overall survival at 1 year was 82% (Study MC-FludT.8/MDS Clinical Study Report Tables
11.4.1.1A, 11.4.1.2A, 14.2.4A, and 14.2.6A). A response assessment using the standard
response criteria was not provided. The Applicant concluded that the regimen "has myeloablative potential as demonstrated by the fast and durable eradication of the physiologic
recipient haematopoiesis. Long-term outcome data seemed to be in favour of this new regimen
when compared with data from the literature." (Study MC-FludT.8/MDS Clinical Study Report
Section 13).
MC-FludT.17/M
Clinical Phase 2 trial to describe the safety and efficacy of Treosulfan-based conditioning
therapy prior to allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation in paediatric patients
with haematological malignancies
Study MC-FludT.17/M was a multicenter single-arm trial to test treosulfan and fludarabine as a
preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT from an HLA-matched or 1-allele mismatched related
or unrelated donors. Eligible patients were 28 days to < 18 years old with ALL, AML, MDS or
JMML indicated for HSCT. The preparative regimen consisted of BSA-adapted treosulfan dosing
equivalent to exposure from 14 gm/m2 in adults in combination with fludarabine and thiotepa
(see Table 7 for details of the regimen). The primary objective was to estimate freedom from
transplant-related mortality at Day 100. Overall survival was an additional endpoint. The
target accrual was 70 patients. The sample size was calculated to allow a descriptive analysis of
hematopoietic recovery, toxicity and PK parameters as part of an agreed PIP. Seventy patients
were included in the analyses. The median age was 9.5 years (range, < 1 - 17 years); 63% were
95

haematological diseases." (Study MC-FludT.17/M Clinical Study Report Section 13).

8.2

Integrated Review of Effectiveness

8.2.1 Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
Methods
The Applicant proposed an indication for treosulfan "as part of conditioning treatment prior
to allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT) in adult patients with acute
myeloid leukemia (AML) and myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS).” The proposed dose of
(b) (4)
treosulfan was of 10 g/m2/dose for use in the combination described.

The clinical development program included two randomized control trials, three single-arm
trials and two registry studies (Table 35). All clinical trials were conducted outside the US.

Grafapex (treosulfan)
There were several issues identified with the elements of the clinical development program:
•

Patient level data were not submitted for the registry studies. Therefore, FDA cannot
independently verify the results. As such, the registry studies do not contribute to the
determination of the effectiveness of treosulfan.

•

EFS and OS are the acceptable endpoints for approval of a drug for use in a preparative
regimen for allogeneic HSCT for patients with AML or MDS. Since Studies MC-FludT.7/AML
and MC-FludT.8/MDS are single-arm trials, they were not designed adequately to assess the
time-to-event endpoints of EFS and OS. As such, Studies MC-FludT.7/AML and MCFludT.8/MDS do not contribute to the determination of the effectiveness of treosulfan.

•

Similarly, the single-arm trial Study MC-FludT.17/M is not designed adequately to assess EFS
or OS and would not support an indication in the pediatric patients with AML or MDS.

•

Studies MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (Trial 14 - I; treosulfan 14 g/m2/dose) was failed trial. The
study was stopped early at the first interim analysis which reported an EFS HR=1.23 (95% CI
0.84, 1.80) and an OS HR=1.30 (0.85, 2.00) in the Full Analysis Set. The trial was stopped
early because of a safety concern.

The Applicant proposed to use Study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Trial 14 - II; treosulfan 10
g/m2/dose) to support the proposed indication. Trial 14 - II was a multicenter, randomized,
active-controlled trial comparing treosuflan to busulfan in combination with fludarabine as a
preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. Eligible patients included adults 18 to 70 years old
with AML or MDS, Karnofsky performance status > 60%, and age ≥ 50 years or hematopoietic
cell transplantation comorbidity index [HCT CI] score > 2. The patients were randomized to
receive treosulfan 10 g/m² daily on days -4, -3 and -2 or to busulfan 0.8 mg/kg every 6 hours on
days -4 and -3 in combination with fludarabine 30 mg/m2 daily on days -6, -5, -4, -3 and -2, and
hematopoietic stem cell transplantation on day 0. Antithymocyte globulin was administered as
well to patients with unrelated donors.
In general, a randomized controlled trial with a survival-based endpoint is appropriate to
support an indication for a drug in a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT. FDA frequently
requires two trials to determine efficacy, but one trial might be sufficient if the trial is
adequately-designed and well-conducted, and the results are internally consistent, clinically
meaningful, and statistically very persuasive Potential issues with the design of Trial 14 - II in
particular included:
•

Trial 14 - II was designed as a noninferiority trial with a primary endpoint of EFS within 2
years of follow-up. FDA was not involved in the review of this protocol prior to conduct of
the study, and there was no pre-existing agreement on an acceptable noninferiority margin
in this setting.
97

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
•

For Trial 14 - II, EFS was defined as the interval from Transplant Day 0 to relapse, graft
failure or death (whatever occurs first); patients alive without an event were censored at 2
years from HSCT.
− For patients with AML transplanted in remission, relapse-free survival (RFS) would be
the usual endpoint to assess efficacy.
− For patients with MDS transplanted not in remission, EFS might be an acceptable
endpoint if defined to reflect the need to achieve remission.

•

OS was a secondary endpoint of Trial 14 - II. The analysis of OS was planned as exploratory;
the timing of the OS analysis was not event-driven, and there was no prespecified alphaspending plan to correct for multiplicity.

•

In Trial 14 - II, the efficacy analyses were planned to be conducted in the subgroup of
patients who were randomized, received at least one dose of study drug, and had at least
one efficacy assessment postbaseline (Full Analysis Set; FAS) and in a subgroup of the FAS
without selected protocol deviations (Per Protocol Set; PPS). However, for a randomized
trial, the ITT population is the preferred analysis set.

•

Trial 14 - II compared fludarabine in combination with 3 days of treatment with treosulfan
to a combination with 2 days of busulfan. Any conclusions regarding superiority would
need to take into account the difference between arms in the number of days of treatment
with treosulfan vs the number of days of treatment with busulfan.

•

AML and MDS are different diseases, so any trial used to support both indications would
need to test for efficacy in each individually. Subgroup analyses of efficacy by diagnosis
were prespecified in Trial 14 - II.

•

Due to differences in minor and other unmeasured major antigens, HLA-matched unrelated
donors have a higher degree of histoincompatibility with the recipient than HLA-identical
sibling donors, so the minimum intensity of the preparative regimen needed for successful
HSCT may differ by donor type. Any trial used to support an indication using both related
and unrelated donors would need to test for efficacy by each individually. Subgroup
analyses of efficacy by donor type were prespecified in Trial 14 - II.

•

Additionally, in Trial 14 - II, patients with unrelated donors also received ATG in the
preparative regimen. ATG dosing varied by geographic region. Any conclusions related to
the subgroup analysis by donor would need to take into consideration the differences in
treatment regimens by donor type.

98

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Primary Endpoint
Event-Free Survival
For Trial 14 - II, the Applicant's last analysis of EFS for submission was the last analysis at 234
events (total 48.6% information level). At the prespecified second interim analysis (164 events,
34.1% information level), the Data Monitoring Committee (DMC) recommended to stop
recruitment because non-inferiority was achieved. Recruitment continued until interim analysis
results became available for the DMC. Therefore, the last analysis had more events than the
second interim analysis. The Applicant provided results for the full follow-up period with
median follow-up of 29.7 months in the treosulfan arm and 29.4 months in the busulfan arm.
The HR for EFS was 0.64 (0.48, 0.87) in the PPS and 0.64 (0.48,0.87) in the FAS. The Applicant
concluded that the results supported noninferiority, and in sequential testing, superiority of
treosulfan.
However, the Applicant did not submit all CBC and bone marrow results, so FDA was unable to
verify the primary endpoint independently and could not confirm that this was a positive trial.
Although, in general, FDA does not accept clinical trials for which the primary endpoint cannot
be confirmed, for this submission the Division Director indicated that the OS endpoint would be
an acceptable alternative for the assessment of efficacy. 1
Secondary and Other Endpoints
Overall Survival
For Trial 14 - II, the analysis of OS was considered exploratory. FDA assessed OS in the ITT
population with using the full follow-up period. Table 36 shows the results of FDA's analyses of
OS with a median follow-up of 29.5 months (range, 0 - 54.8 months). No p-value is shown, as
this analysis was exploratory.

Additional Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant prespecified several secondary endpoints for Trial 14 - II, described in 8.1.1, but
most of these were safety endpoints and none was suitable to use in support of the OS
demonstrating effectiveness for treatment of AML or MDS.
For treatment of AML in remission, RFS would be a suitable endpoint to support a finding of
superiority in OS. Because CBC and marrow examination data were not collected systematically
in Trial 14 - II, RFS cannot be assessed, and there are no secondary endpoints to corroborate
the OS outcome for the patients with AML.
For treatment of MDS not in remission, EFS (defined suitably) or CR that is durable might be
suitable to assess activity. Because CBC and marrow examination data were not collected
systematically in this protocol, EFS and CR cannot be assessed, and there are no secondary
endpoints to corroborate an efficacy outcome for the patients with MDS.
Subpopulations
Exploratory subpopulations prespecified for analysis of OS in Trial 14 - II included donor type,
risk group, combination of donor type and risk group, disease (AML and MDS), age group (< 50
years vs. >= 50 years), HCT-CI Score (<= 2 vs. > 2), remission status in AML (CR1 vs. > CR1),
disease status at study entry in MDS (untreated vs. treated), risk group within AML patients,
and risk group within MDS patients. Race and ethnicity were not included in the subpopulation
analyses as data on race and ethnicity were not collected. This deficiency leaves some
uncertainty about the generalizability of the efficacy outcomes with respect to the US
population.
Table 22 in Section 8.1.1 shows the results of the FDA subpopulation analysis of OS for Trial 14 II. In general, where the subgroups were large enough for credible analyses, the results
demonstrated consistency in the subpopulations with the result in the ITT population overall.
The exception was the subpopulation by donor type. Table 37 shows the results of FDA's
analysis of OS by donor for the ITT population and in patients with AML or with MDS separately.

The results in Table 37 show that the treatment effect appears to be driven by the
subpopulation of patients with unrelated donors (HR 0.56), and the treatment effect appears
to be consistent with patients with unrelated donors whether they have AML (HR 0.51) or MDS
(HR 0.65). For the patients with related donors, the treatment effect is much smaller or
negative than for those with related donors, and the HR is inconsistent across the disease
subgroups, although the number of patients with MDS and related donors is small. Of note, as
shown in Appendix Figure 16.5.1, within a disease population, the busulfan arms appear to
have similar OS (e.g., 16.5.1 A vs B red lines for patients with AML), but for either disease, the
OS appears to be worse with treosulfan for patients with related donors in comparison to those
with unrelated donors. The biological basis for this observed difference is unclear; it may be
related to the use of ATG that was limited to those with unrelated donors, or to some
unmeasured genetic element in the older related donors. Additional information is needed to
assess this potential lack of effectiveness.
Dose/Dose Response
The Applicant reported that a) for autologous HSCT, the single-dose MTD of treosulfan was
47 gm/m2, and b) in an investigator-initiated pilot study of treosulfan 10 g/m2/day x 3 in
combination with fludarabine was tolerable as a preparative regimen for allogeneic HSCT and
produced a high rate of complete donor chimerism ( Study MC-FludT.6/L Clinical Study Report
Section 9.4.4). For Study MC-FludT.6/L, the dose-escalation trial for development of the
proposed preparative regimen, the Applicant studied treosulfan doses 10, 12 and 14 g/m2/day
x 3 in combination with fludarabine. As discussed in Section 8.1.4, hematopoietic recovery and
high-grade donor chimerism were achieved at all dose levels, and the 1-year OS was worst in
the 14 g/m2/day x 3 cohort. Nonetheless, the Applicant chose the 14 g/m2/day x 3 regimen for
the initial pivotal trial, MC-FludT.14/L Trial I (Trial 14 - I).
As discussed in Sections 8.1.2 and 8.3.5, Trial 14 - I was terminated early due to delayed
hematopoietic recovery and increase early treatment-related mortality. FDA's analysis of the
final data cut (1-year follow-up) showed no evidence of a treatment effect for the overall
population or when assessed individually for patients with AML or with MDS. The second
pivotal trial, Trial 14 - II, commenced using the lower treosulfan dose of 10 g/m2/day x 3.

Extrapolation to Pediatric Patients
(b) (4)

MC-FludT.16/NM was a phase 2 study for nonmalignant
diseases that randomized patients to a 14 g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan or standard
busulfan. Because this was not performed in a disease that is being considered for an indication
for treosulfan, it was not evaluated for efficacy. The second study, MC-FludT.17/M, was a
nonrandomized study for pediatric patients with malignant diseases; all patients received a 14
(b) (4)
g/m2/dose equivalent of treosulfan.

8.2.2 Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
In the absence of verification of the primary endpoint, and without prespecification of Type-1
error for multiple comparisons, Study 14-II does not provide considerable evidence of
effectiveness.

8.3

Review of Safety

8.3.1 Safety Review Approach
The FDA reviewed the safety data for 1,206 patients in clinical trials (Table 39). These included
728 patients treated with treosulfan and 478 treated with busulfan (active control). FDA utilized
the following cohorts for this safety review:
•

Cohort 1: The 270 patients treated with treosulfan in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II were used to
assess the safety profile of treosulfan in the intended population, as compared to the
standard RIC of busulfan + fludarabine.

•

Cohort 2: The 168 patients treated with treosulfan in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I were used to
assess potential safety signals at the treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2, as compared to the
standard RIC of busulfan + fludarabine.

•

Cohort 3: The 290 patients treated with treosulfan at 10 g/m2 (MC-FludT.6/L and MCFludT.14/L Trial II) and the 305 patients treated with treosulfan at 14 g/m2 (MCFludT.6/L, MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trial I) were
compared to assess differences in dose-related toxicity.

•

Cohort 4: The 158 pediatric patients (treosulfan, N = 115; busulfan, N = 43) enrolled in
MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M were assessed for safety signals within the
pediatric population. The 12 pediatric patients who received treosulfan + fludarabine
(without thiotepa) were compared to the 10 g/m2 population from Cohort 3.

All analyses were performed using the ISS datasets (12/11/2020, SDN 0017).

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Treosulfan is an alkylating agent, and it is related to busulfan. The common toxicities for
busulfan in the setting of allo-HSCT are myelosuppression, hepatitis veno-occlusive disease
(VOD), nausea, vomiting, stomatitis, diarrhea, fever, abdominal pain, anxiety, anorexia,
insomnia, and electrolyte abnormalities. Of note, busulfan has an increased risk for seizures,
which has not been identified for treosulfan.
As treosulfan is closely related to busulfan, and is thought to act as an alkylating agent, the
known safety profile for busulfan was vital for this analysis.
Animal toxicity studies revealed that the target organ of treosulfan is the bone marrow, with
leukopenia, neutropenia, reticulocytopenia, thrombocytopenia, and lymphopenia observed in
the repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats, dogs, and monkeys. There was no evidence of doselimiting functional or structural changes to the liver, kidneys, heart, lungs, or central nervous
system.
The main safety signal that arose during the development program occurred during MCFludT.14/L Trial I (treosulfan dose 14 g/m2). Because of higher than anticipated early morbidity
and mortality in the treosulfan arm of the study, the study was paused. An amendment was
submitted, and the protocol reopened with a lower dose of treosulfan (MC-FludT.14/L Trial II,
treosulfan dose 10 g/m2).

Relevant characteristics of the safety population
The demographics of the safety population for the cohorts 1, 2, and 3 are shown in Table 41. In
general, more male patients than female patients were enrolled on these. Nearly all of the
patients in these cohorts were in the 18-64 year age group, with 20% of patients in the 65-74
year age group, and no patients age 75 or greater.

The key characteristics of cohort 4 (pediatric population) is shown in Table 42. More male
patients than female patients were enrolled on MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. Most
patients were in either the 2-11 year or 12-16 year age groups. Five patients were 17 or older
and are analyzed separately, but they are not included with the adult patients above because of
differences in the preparative regimen. Data on race were collected with the pediatric studies,
with over 90% of the patients identifying as “White.” Most pediatric patients received thiotepa,
in addition to fludarabine and either busulfan or treosulfan as part of their preparative regimen
and are excluded from comparison with cohort 1.

Adequacy of the Safety Database
The safety database was assessed for adequacy with the following conclusions:
•

Because data on race and ethnicity were not collected, the safety database was not
adequate to extrapolate to the demographics of the United States population.

•

There were sufficient patient numbers to assess for rare adverse reactions.

•

There were not sufficient patients in the “≥75 years of age” age group to assess safety
signals in that population.

•

There were sufficient patients to evaluate differences by sex.

•

There were sufficient patients treated at the 10 and 14 g/m2 dose levels to assess for a
dose-toxicity relationship.

8.3.3 Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The doses of concomitant medications were not provided in cm.xpt or adcm.xpt. Table 43
shows the duration of follow-up for mortality, SAE reporting, TEAE reporting, and laboratory
testing for each of the studies included in the ISS. MC-FludT.6/L, which was the dose finding
Phase 2 study, had the longest follow-up for SAE, TEAE, and safety labs. Additionally, Cohort 4
had longer SAE and SAR follow-up than the other studies included in Cohorts 1, 2, and 3.

Categorization of Adverse Events
Adverse events were reported down to the verbatim term. The adverse events were coded
using MedDRA version 20.0 for all studies and for the pooled safety population. Where
indicated in the tables or text, some adverse events are presented as grouped terms (Table 44).
Regimen-related toxicity (RRT) is defined as directly caused by the conditioning regimen.
Bearman’s RRT criteria are used unless otherwise specified. Treatment-emergent adverse
events (TEAEs) included events that started from the start of study drug administration through
HSCT day +30, unless otherwise indicated.

Routine Clinical Tests
See 8.1.1 and 8.1.2 for descriptions of the frequency of clinical assessments for studies MCFludT.14/L Trials I and II. The frequency of clinical assessments is adequate to assess the risks of
serious safety signals identified by HSCT day +30. There are late RRTs associated with HSCT that
occur rarely but can be serious; other than graft versus host disease (GVHD), these were not
captured reliably in the pivotal study MC-FludT.14/L Trial II (Cohort 1).

Deaths from Cohort 1 were assessed and causes of death were adjudicated (Table 46). In MCFludT.14/L Trial II, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) from fatal adverse reactions due to treosulfan by
Day +30; there were two additional deaths that were adjudicated as transplant-related
mortality. In the busulfan arm, there were two deaths prior to Day +30, both of which were
adjudicated as adverse reactions due to busulfan. In the treosulfan arm, there were 17 deaths
(6%) by transplant day +100, compared to 19 deaths (7%) by transplant day + 100 in the
busulfan arm. However, we note that for six patients, we did not have sufficient information to
adjudicate the cause of death.

Grafapex (treosulfan)
Deaths in the treosulfan arm from Table 46 that are due to regimen-related toxicity that began
prior to Day +30 after HSCT are detailed below.
Subject
was a 63-year-old woman with AML in CR1 (2% bone marrow blasts), with a past
medical history significant for acute renal impairment (resolved), hypertension, and diabetes.
The subject received treosulfan + fludarabine as conditioning for allo-HSCT and received a PBSC
as prescribed by the protocol with a matched related donor. On Day + 11, the subject is
reported to have developed a lung infection, based on physical symptoms of pneumonia. On
Day +12, the subject developed hemolysis and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage. The subject
died on Day +13. The lung infection and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage were grade 5. The
Applicant considered lung infection and bronchopulmonary hemorrhage as possibly related to
treosulfan.
(b) (6)

Reviewer’s Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary
based on the time to event analysis and the narrative provided by the Applicant, which did
not provide a microbial etiology.
Subject
was a 56-year-old man with MDS who received a conditioning regimen with
treosulfan + fludarabine and a PBSC allo-HSCT from a matched unrelated donor. On Day +1, the
subject developed grade 3 lung infection, with symptoms of dyspnea, fever, CRP elevation, and
respiratory insufficiency. This was further specified as pneumonia and progressed to grade 4.
On Day +7, the subject developed grade 2 febrile neutropenia. On Day +9, the patient
developed grade 5 sepsis, with symptoms of hypotension, acidosis, kidney failure, and
respiratory insufficiency. The subject died on Day +14. The Applicant assessed the cause of
death as sepsis with lung infection as life-threatening. Both were considered by the Applicant as
possibly related to treosulfan.
(b) (6)

Reviewer’s Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary
based on the narrative provided by the Applicant. No microbial etiology was provided.
Subject
was a 66-year-old man with AML who received a conditioning regimen with
treosulfan + fludarabine and a PBSC allo-HSCT from a matched related donor. On Day +11, the
patient experienced grade 4 lung infection, which worsened on Day +19 with progression to
life-threatening respiratory fatigue. On Day +22, the patient experienced grade 5 sepsis and
grade 5 kidney failure. The Applicant considered lung infection and sepsis as unlikely to be
related to treosulfan.
(b) (6)

Reviewer’s Comment: The FDA adjudicated the cause of death to be AR RRT – pulmonary. This
is based on the time to event and the narrative provided by the Applicant, which did not
provide a microbial etiology.

Serious Adverse Events
In MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, 23 patients (8.5%) who received treosulfan had at least one serious
adverse event (SAE), compared to 20 patients (7.1%) who received busulfan. The most common
SAE was infection, with thirteen patients on the treosulfan arm (4.8%) and eight patients on the
busulfan arm (2.8%) experiencing this SAE by D+30 (Table 47).

Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects

Significant Adverse Events
See Section 8.3.5 for a discussion of acute and chronic graft versus host disease (GVHD).
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
Treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) through HSCT Day +30 in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II by
system organ class (SOC) are shown in Table 49. Most TEAEs by SOC occur at similar rates
between the treosulfan arm and the busulfan arm. Of note, cardiac disorders were more
common in among patients who received treosulfan.

When evaluating TEAEs by Preferred Term (PT), the most common TEAEs were those expected
after allo-HSCT (Table 50). The most common adverse reactions (≥10%) on the treosulfan arm
of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia, nausea, edema,
infection, vomiting, rash, diarrhea, headache, febrile neutropenia, abdominal pain,
hypertension, hemorrhage, fatigue, constipation, tachycardia, and hepatotoxicity. The most
common grade 3-5 ARs (≥10%) on the treosulfan arm were febrile neutropenia and infection.
Rare but potentially serious events that occurred on the treosulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II
included ventricular arrythmias (including tachycardia, see Grouped Term in Table 44 above,
and extrasystoles; N = 5, 1.852%), erythema multiforme (N = 8, 2.963%), palmar-plantar
erythrodysaesthesia syndrome (N = 5, 1.852%), and cardiac failure (N = 8, 2.963%).

Reviewer’s Comment: The FDA notes that the Grouped Term of musculoskeletal pain was not
considered to be a related AE by the Applicant. However, the timing of this GT indicates that
it is an adverse reaction following treosulfan conditioning. We note higher rates of infectious
complications in patients following treosulfan-based preparative regimens compared to
busulfan-based preparative regimens. We also note a higher rate of fatal ARs through Day
+30 after HSCT in patients who received treosulfan-based preparative regimens.
Laboratory Findings
As treosulfan is used as part of a conditioning regimen for allogeneic HSCT, cytopenias occur in
nearly 100% of patients. Other laboratory abnormalities are listed below (Table 52). There were
no significant differences in the maximum grades of laboratory abnormalities between the
treosulfan and busulfan arms of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II.

Vital Signs
Vital signs were provided for the following timepoints: within 3 weeks of HSCT, day 0, day 28,
day +100, month 6, month 9, month 12, month 15, month 18, and month 24. There were no
vital signs submitted from the time periods around treosulfan or busulfan infusions to assess
for evidence of infusion reactions. There were not sufficient vital signs collected to assess for
outliers within the 30 days following HSCT.
QT/Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Formal QT/QTc testing has not been completed. Under “Investigations,” “electrocardiogram
abnormality” was identified in one patient on the busulfan arm of MC-FludT.14/L Trial II and
zero patients on the treosulfan arm. Ventricular arrythmias were identified as rare but
potentially serious ARs and are discussed in in the TEAE and AR section above.
Table 53 shows other electrocardiographic abnormalities in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II through HSCT
Day +30.

Immunogenicity

8.3.5 Analysis of Submission‐Specific Safety Issues
Treosulfan Dose (14 g/m2)
Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I used a treosulfan dose of 14 g/m2 for patients randomized to receive
treosulfan. This study was stopped early due to increased transplant-related morbidity and
mortality observed in the treosulfan arm. Treatment-emergent adverse events through HSCT
day +30 are shown in Table 54. There were increased TEAEs of hepatotoxicity, stomatitis,
musculoskeletal pain, infection, and febrile neutropenia in the treosulfan cohort, including
grades 3-5 TEAEs.

Graft versus Host Disease
MC-FludT-14.L Trial II was further analyzed for differences between occurrence of acute
(aGVHD) and chronic (cGVHD) graft versus host disease between the treosulfan and busulfan
arms (Table 55). For analysis of both aGVHD and cGVHD, there was no censoring of the
population for death or other events. The rate of aGVHD is defined as the proportion of the
safety population diagnosed with aGVHD by HSCT Day +100. There was no risk difference of
>10% for any grade of aGVHD. The rate of cGVHD is defined as the proportion of the safety
population diagnosed with cGVHD through month +24. There was no risk difference of ≥ 10 for
either limited or extensive cGVHD between the treosulfan and busulfan arms.

8.3.6 Safety Analyses by Subgroups
Drug-Demographic Interactions
TEAEs by Sex
TEAEs with an RD of >5% between female and male patients are shown in Table 56. Women
were more likely to experience GI-related toxicities, including nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and
constipation than men in Trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial II. Men receiving treosulfan were more likely
to have hepatotoxicity and renal impairment. Vomiting was significantly different between
women and men (p = 0.00082). Vomiting was not more likely in women receiving treosulfan
(33%) compared to women receiving busulfan (28%, RD 4.5).

TEAEs by Age
TEAEs with an RD of >5% between adult (ages 17-64) and elderly (ages 65-74) patients are
shown in Table 57. When comparing TEAEs by age group, only infection was more likely to
occur in elderly patients (those ages 65-74) compared to younger adults with an RD of >10%. In
comparing elderly patients who received treosulfan to those who received busulfan-based
conditioning, infection occurred more frequently in the treosulfan arm (32% vs 22%, RD 9.45).

TEAEs by Race
All studies were performed in Europe. No data on race or ethnicity were collected as part of any
study. Therefore, no comparisons can be made.
Drug-Disease Interactions
Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients with the diagnoses of AML
and MDS. As these are separate diseases, TEAEs were assessed in the treosulfan arm of MCFludT.14/L Trial II in the AML and MDS populations separately (Table 58). No TEAEs were more
common in patients with AML. Patients with an underlying diagnosis of MDS were statistically
(p<0.05) more likely to have TEAEs of hemorrhage, hyperglycemia, cough, edema,
hyponatremia, and encephalopathy.

Drug-Donor Interactions
Treosulfan was used as part of the conditioning regimen for patients undergoing both matched
related donor and matched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT. TEAEs were assessed in the

8.3.8 Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
As MC-FludT.14/L Trial I, in which patients randomized to treosulfan received a dose of 14 g/m2,
was stopped early because of increased transplant-related morbidity and mortality, a
comparison was performed between all adult patients who received 10 g/m2 and those who
received 14 g/m2 of treosulfan. We observed higher rates of hepatotoxicity, GI toxicity,
infection (including febrile neutropenia), and musculoskeletal AEs in patients receiving the 14
g/m2 dose of treosulfan, as seen in Table 61.

Reviewer’s Comment: The FDA notes that there is notably more toxicity, including infectious
complications, GI toxicity, and hepatotoxicity, in patients who received 14 g/m2/dose of
treosulfan compared to 10 g/m2/dose of treosulfan. In addition, the higher rates of early fatal
adverse reactions in MC-FludT.14/L Trial I underscore the increased risk of morbidity and
mortality with the higher dose of treosulfan.
Time Dependency for Adverse Events
There was insufficient follow-up with detailed safety results to allow an assessment of late
toxicities.
8.3.9 Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
An analysis of the SOC “Neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (including cysts and
polyps)” in MC-FludT.14/L Trial II revealed one patient in the treosulfan arm and one patient in
the busulfan arm with these events.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Because of the proposed mechanism of action of treosulfan, pregnancy and reproduction are
discouraged. There have been no studies or literature reports of subjects who became pregnant
while receiving treosulfan, or of children who were born of parents who were receiving
treosulfan.

Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
Two pediatric studies were submitted as part of this application. All pediatric patients received
doses of treosulfan that were equivalent to 14 g/m2 dosing Safety analysis was performed
comparing the pediatric patients to the recommended dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2). The
majority of pediatric patients received thiotepa in addition to fludarabine and treosulfan for
their conditioning. Analyses comparing pediatric and adult studies (Cohorts 4 and 1) excluded
patients who received thiotepa.
An analysis between the treosulfan arm and the busulfan arm in the Phase 2 randomized study
MC-FludT.16/NM is seen in Table 62. Nearly all of the patients in this analysis received a
preparative regimen of either treosulfan or busulfan, fludarabine, and thiotepa. The majority of
TEAEs were similar between the two arms. Notable exceptions included higher rates of
abdominal pain, rash, pruritis, hemorrhage, and infusion-related reactions in the treosulfan arm
of this study.

8.3.10 Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
Empirica Signal was used to evaluate the postmarket experience of treosulfan (Figure 15). The
128

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
fifty highest PTs postmarket reports were further analyzed. Forty-eight were known
complications of allo-HSCT and deemed TRM. A case series of retinitis was further investigated;
all reported cases of retinitis (optic neuritis) were due to infectious causes and are also deemed
TRM. Additionally, case reports of respiratory failure were also further investigated. Of the 28
case reports, three were adjudicated to be within 30 days of HSCT and an AR due to the
conditioning regimen. The other cases were due to infection or complications from venoocclusive disease.

Figure 15. Heat Map of Postmarketing Safety Signals by SOC
Source: Reviewer's Analysis, Empirica Signal

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety in the postmarket setting is anticipated to be similar to that observed in the pivotal trial
submitted and reviewed in this application. It is recognized that treosulfan may be used in
populations other than those included in the indication statement. Additionally, patients with
lower performance status, higher HCT-CI score, and inadequate organ function may receive
treosulfan outside of a clinical trial. For these reasons, the predictability of treosulfan in the
postmarket setting is not completely predictable.
129

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
8.3.11 Integrated Assessment of Safety
The primary data in support of safety for the proposed indication of treosulfan came from study
MC-FludT.14/L Trial II, in which 270 patients with either AML or MDS were exposed to
treosulfan as part of a conditioning regimen prior to allogeneic HSCT. All patients received two
doses of treosulfan on days -4 and -3 prior to HSCT, at a dose of 10 g/m2/dose.
The study population was monitored for death, adverse events, serious adverse events, and
laboratory abnormalities (Table 65). By Day +30 following HSCT, there were 4 deaths (1.5%) on
the treosulfan arm, all due to fatal pulmonary adverse reactions, as compared to 2 deaths
(0.7%) on the busulfan (control) arm.
Adverse events with treosulfan were mainly similar to those observed with busulfan-based
conditioning. Common TEAEs (≥20%) included musculoskeletal pain, stomatitis, pyrexia,
nausea, edema, infection, and vomiting. Of those, musculoskeletal pain (12% higher), edema
(11% higher), and infection (5% higher) occurred at a rate at least 5% higher in the treosulfan
arm than the busulfan arm. The most common grade 3-5 AEs (≥5%) in the treosulfan arm were
febrile neutropenia, infection, hypertension, stomatitis, and musculoskeletal pain. Of those,
infection occurred at a rate of >5% compared to the busulfan arm (6% higher in the treosulfan
arm).
Safety data supporting the dose of treosulfan (10 g/m2/dose) were from studies MC-FludT.6/L,
MC-FludT.7/AML, MC-FludT.8/MDS, and MC-FludT.14/L Trials I and II. In this comparison, 290
patients were dosed at 10 g/m2/dose, and 305 patients were dosed at 14 g/m2/dose.
Summary of Major Safety Events
Table 65. Summary of Major Safety Events from MC-FludT.14/L Trial II through Day +30

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
8.4 Statistical Issues
Pivotal Trial 14-II
Table 66 summarizes statistical issues for Study 14-II and our assessment for them. Because the
primary endpoint EFS could not be independently verified by FDA, the review and analysis were
focused on OS.
Table 66. Summary of Statistical Issues of Pivotal Trial 14-II

•

Analysis set: ITT and FAS population are similar outcome

•

Interpretation of p-values for OS: Since there was no pre-specified plan to control
multiplicity, p-values are nominal and cannot be included in labeling. To facilitate decision
making, we calculated OBF boundaries and Pocock boundaries using information fraction of
EFS as an exploratory analysis. As stated in section 8.1.1, the OS result (nominal pvalue=0.0049) did not cross the OBF boundary (alpha=0.0025) but crossed the Pocock
boundary (alpha=0.014).

•

Trial conduct: We investigated the informative censoring for OS, time from randomization
to transplant and time from diagnosis to randomization between treatment arms. We did
not find any trial conduct issue that may impact the data quality or potentially introduce
bias.
131

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Trial 14-I
This trial discontinued after planned interim analysis. It was determined that dose should be
lower in this elderly population because neutropenic phase was much longer than control.
This trial preceded Trial 14-II and used the same SAP, therefore all analyses reported by sponsor
was based on the FAS population. We did subgroup analysis on OS to investigate any
significantly larger detrimental effect on OS comparing to the overall effect. FDA used the ITT
population in all analyses. No significantly larger detrimental effect was found in subgroups we
analyzed.

8.5 Conclusions and Recommendations
Study 14-II is the sole pivotal trial submitted to support efficacy. Because the primary endpoint
EFS could not be independently verified by FDA for Study 14-II, no conclusions can be made
based on the primary endpoint. Therefore, the statistical review and analysis were focused on
OS for Study 14-II.
There was no prespecified multiplicity approach to test OS after EFS, but there would have
been different conclusions regarding statistical significance for OS based on Pocock or O’BrienFleming boundaries. These mixed results suggest that it is not clear if OS would have been
considered to be significant if there was a prespecified approach; however, no definitive
conclusions can be made based on the OS results because a multiplicity approach was not
prespecified. The hazard ratio was 0.62 with 95% CI (0.41, 0.92) in AML patients and 0.62 with
95% CI (0.34,1.10) in MDS patients.
Regarding other statistical issues, the ITT and FAS population were similar and no trial conduct
issues were found. From the statistical perspective, the effect of treosulfan as observed in the
OS results appears to be supportive; however, interpreting this data is challenging because
there was no prespecified plan to control multiplicity.
In the absence of verification of the primary endpoint, and without prespecification of Type-1
error for multiple comparisons, Study 14-II does not provide considerable evidence of
effectiveness. There is insufficient evidence of effectiveness to outweigh any risks.

16.4 OCP Appendices
16.4.1 Pharmacometrics Review
A population pharmacokinetics (PPK) model was developed by the Applicant to characterize the
PK of treosulfan in adults and pediatric patients received allogeneic transplant. Patients intrinsic
and extrinsic factors which influence the PK and PK variability of treosulfan were identified. The
exposure-response (E-R) relationships for efficacy and safety were explored using multivariate
analyses. In this review, FDA Pharmacometrics Reviewer verified the Applicant’s analyses, as
well as conducted independent analyses to evaluate the Applicant’s dose selection.
1. Population PK Analyses
Originally, the Applicant developed PPK model based on seven historical studies (from mostly
adults and only a limited number of children,
Table 67) and 2 new pediatric studies (Table 68). However, due to the invalidated bioanalytical
methods for the quantification of treosulfan, all PK data of the non-medac-sponsored studies
were removed from the PPK analysis upon the FDA request. The updated dataset for PPK
analysis included 1 historical medac sponsored trial (MC-FludT.14/L Trial I with 24 adult
subjects) and 2 new pediatric medac trials (MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FLudT.17/M with 83
subjects). The summary of the continuous covariates for the subjects included in the updated
final PPK model are shown in
Table 69. In addition, the dataset included 63 male and 44 female subjects.
