Based on the GOF and VPC plots, the Applicant’s updated final PPK analysis is acceptable for
describing the PK of treosulfan in adult and pediatric patients. In the Applicant’s PPK model
development, multiple parameters could not be precisely estimated, and the effect of renal
impairment on CL was removed. Thus, the Reviewer conducted independent analysis to
evaluate the impact of renal function on treosulfan clearance and exposure in adults. As shown
in Figure 20, a trend of lower BSA-adjusted CL and higher AUCinf (but not significant) in
moderate renal impairment subjects (n=2) was observed compared to subjects with normal
renal function (n=10). Mild renal impairment (n=12) appears to have no impact on CL and
AUCinf of treosulfan. However, it is important to note that the number of subjects in the
moderate renal impairment category was very limited. In addition, the lowest CrCL included in
the analysis was 47 mL/min, which is still much higher than the lower cutoff of the moderate
renal impairment (30 mL/min).

2. Exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy
2.1 Adult patients
The Applicant evaluated the potential relationship between exposure and time to engraftment
(defined as time from Day 0 to neutrophil engraftment (first of 3 consecutive days with ANC
>0.5 x109/L)) in adult patients based on linear regression model, Kaplan-Meier curves and
multivariate analyses. The original analyses were based on both historical trials (SOLCA3 and
SOLCA5 were excluded due to lack of information on baseline demographic and disease
characteristics) and medac-sponsored trials with the dose range from 10 g/m2 single dose to 14
g/m2 multiple dose. No significant E-R relationship between AUC versus time to engraftment
was identified. The updated E-R analyses based on updated PPK model only included 24 adult
patients following the dosing regimen of 14 g/m2 in the historical medac-sponsored trial MCFludT.14/L Trial I. No PK data were collected from Trial II at the recommendated dosing regimen
of 10 g/m2. Thus, with a limited number of subjects and narrow exposure range at a higher than
recommended dosing regimen, the updated E-R analyses in adult patients are not considered
informative.
2.2 Pediatric patients
Upon FDA’s request, the Applicant conducted E-R analyses for time to engraftment based on 2
pediatric trials using Cox proportional hazards models with AUC and Cmax estimated by the
updated PPK analyses. No significant relationship between treosulfan expsosure and time to
engraftment was demonstrated. In addition, no evidence was observed with regard to a
potential effect of baseline demographic and disease characteristic variables on time to
engraftment. However, because some of the pediatric patients in the studies FludT.16/NM and
MC-FLudT.17/M received thiotepa, the E-R analyses results should be interpreted with caution.
145

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
3. Exposure-response analyses for safety
Applicant original E-R analyses for safety were based on pooled data from adult and pediatric
patients included in the original PPK analysis. After the update by removing the invalid PK data,
only 24 adult patients in trial MC-FludT.14/L Trial I were retained, and all of them were dosed
with 14 g/m2 treosulfan. In addition, the safety in adult patients were compared between MCFludT.14/L Trial I (14 g/m2/day × 3 days of treosulfan on -6 to -4 day) and Trial II (10 g/ m2/day ×
3 days of treosulfan on -4 to -2 day). Compared to the proposed dosing regimen (10 g/m2/day
x3 days on -4 to -2), the higher dose regimen (14 g/m2/day × 3 days of treosulfan on -6 to -4
day) resulted in longer duration of neutropenia (17.5 vs. 14 days), higher infection rate (11.9%
vs 8.1%) and higher infection-related death (16.1% vs 9.3%). Thus, given the available doseresponse relationship for safety as well as the narrow exposure range within the 24 adult
patients with valid PK data, no addtional exposure-safety analyses in adults were conducted
based on updated PK parameters. For details on dose-response relationship for safety, refer to
sections 6.3.2 and 8.3.5 in the unireview.
For pediatric patients, the Applicant conducted multivariate E-R analyses for safety endpoints,
including duration of neutropenia, infections, infection-related death and non-relapse mortality
based on the updated PK parameters in patients (n=82) from trials MC-FludT.17/M and MCFludT.16/NM, upon FDA’s request. Both AUC and Cmax were assessed. The potential effects of
age,
, disease type, donor type and Karnofsky/Lansky performance score were
investigated. Treosulfan dose in pediatric PK patients (10, 12, or 14 g/m²) was BSA-adapted
according to trial protocols MC-FludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M. Again, because some of the
pediatric patients in the studies FludT.16/NM and MC-FLudT.17/M received thiotepa, the E-R
analyses results should be interpreted with caution.
Infection-related mortality
Out of 82 children from both malignant and non-malignant diseases trials, only 1 pediatric
patient (1.2%) with the event of infection-related death was observed. This patient had a
significant increased AUC and Cmax. Because of the very limited number of subject (n=1), the
statistically significant impact of AUC and Cmax are not considered meaningful.
Infection
Out of 82 pediatric patients, 33 (40.2%) subjects with infections with at least CTCAE grade III
were observed, and they were equally distributed between malignant and non-malignant
diseases. No significant effect of treosulfan AUC or Cmax, baseline demographic and disease
characteristic variables could be detected by logistic regression models.
Gastrointestinal (GI) disorders
Out of 82 patients, 41 (50.0%) subjects with GI disorder with at least CTCAE grade III were
observed. No significant effect of treosulfan AUC or Cmax was detected by logistic regression
models. Only the age of patients had a significant effect on risk for GI disorder.
146

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
Duration of neutropenia
In the 80 pediatric patients, no significant effect of treosulfan AUC or Cmax, baseline
demographic and disease characteristic variables could be detected by linear regression
models. In addition, no evidence was observed with regard to a potential effect of baseline
demographic and disease characteristic variables.
4. PK Simulation to Support Pediatric dose selection
The Applicant proposed the following dose levels in pediatric patients to obtain comparable
treosulfan AUC24h to an average adult after administration of treosulfan 10 g/m2 (refer to
section 6.3.2):
• 9.0 g/m2 if BSA ≤ 0.7 m2 or in the age range 1 to 4 years
• 9.5 g/m2 if 0.7 m2 < BSA ≤ 1.1 m2 or in the age range 5 to 9 years
• 10.0 g/m2 if BSA > 1.1 m2 or in the age range 10 to 18 years
The Applicant’s pediatric dosing regimen was identified to provide similar exposure in pediatrics
to adult patients administrated with 10 g/m2 of treosulfan (
APPEARS THIS WAY ON
Table 71).

Table 71. Simulated AUC24h for different BSA and age groups by dose regimen. Highlighted

Source: Applicant’s population PK report 2021.

147

Reference ID: 4834011

Grafapex (treosulfan)
To evaluate whether the Applicant proposed pediatric dosing regimen is reasonable, the
Reviewer conducted independent simulation and compared the predicted AUC24h among
different BSA groups and dosing regimens. Average BSA per age group was calculated by the
Applicant from average population body weight and height surveyed in the United States. The
simulation was conducted for 1000 replicates to generate a reasonable number of virtual
subjects (n=28000) for each dosing regimen. The BSA distribution included for each simulation
is shown in Figure 21.
Simulation was performed using the Applicant developed final PPK model. The simulations used
1000 replicates of the Applicant generated virtual population as discussed above with 28
subjects per replicate. The software used to perform the simulation was NONMEM 7.4. The
simulation result shows that the Applicant proposed pediatric dosing regimen is expected to
produce similar AUC24h compared to the median AUC24h in adult following 10 g/m2 dosing
(target exposure). However, a simplified dosing regimen of 10 g/m2 for the entire pediatric
population with BSA range of 0.25 to 2.25 m2 is also predicted to provide comparable AUC24h
without clinically significant difference compared to the target exposure in adults. For pediatric
patients with BSA ≤ 0.7 m2, AUC24h is expected to be 11.2% higher than the target exposure;
for pediatric subjects with BSA within 0.7 to 1.1 m2, AUC24h is expected to be 4.7% higher than
the target exposure; for pediatric subjects with BSA > 1.1 m2, AUC24h is expected to be 2.8%
lower than the target exposure in adults, as shown in Figure 22. Thus, for the convenience of
dosing and to avoid potential dosing error, we recommend the 10 g/m2 dosing regimen for all
pediatric patients with the age ≥ 1 year.

16.4.2 PBPK Review
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s PBPK report, entitled
“Treosulfan DDI” to predict the effects of treosulfan on the PK of 1) midazolam (a CYP3A4
substrate), omeprazole (a CYP2C19 substrate) and digoxin (a P-gp substrate).
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report, supporting modeling files, and
the Applicant’s response to the FDA’s information requests (IRs) submitted on December 21,
2020, March 3, 2021 and April 27, 2021 and concluded the followings.
o The treosulfan PBPK model is adequate to predict the treosulfan PK profiles following 2
hours infusion of 14 g/m2 for three consecutive days.
o There is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited
an inhibitory effect on CYP enzymes and P-gp in in vitro incubation systems.
o The PBPK model and mechanistic static model were used to predict the magnitude of
enzyme mediated DDI caused by treosulfan and its metabolite, EBDM, respectively. Results
149

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
of analysis showed that the potential DDI of treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor and a CYP2C19
inhibitor cannot be excluded.
o The PBPK model was used to predict the magnitude of P-gp mediated DDI caused by
treosulfan. Results of analysis showed that the potential DDI of treosulfan as a P-gp
inhibitor cannot be excluded. This could be important for P-gp substrates with a narrow
therapeutic index.
Applicant’s PBPK Modeling Effort
PBPK software
PK-Sim V8.0 (Open Systems Pharmacology) was used by the Applicant to develop the PBPK
models and for DDI predictions. The reviewer used the PK-Sim V 9.0 for analyses.
Model development
Treosulfan
The treosulfan PBPK model was developed for intravenous dosing in healthy subjects. The
plasma protein binding of treosulfan is considered to be negligible. The population
pharmacokinetic model estimated volume of distribution is about 40 L, indicating that
treosulfan may distribute into both extracellular and intracellular fluid. The standard PK-Sim 3compartment model (intracellular, interstitial and vascular space) with a single permeation
barrier between the interstitial and intracellular space in each organ was used to characterize
the distribution of treosulfan.
Treosulfan is a water-soluble prodrug that is converted non-enzymatically at pH 7.4 and 37 oC
to two active metabolites (EBDM and DEB) along with the release of methanesulfonic acid.
Treosulfan’s nonenzymatic activation is pH- and temperature- dependent and is stopped at pH
<5.0 2. This activation process occurs in both blood and tissue compartments. Treosulfan is
excreted in the kidney via glomerular filtration, and the fraction of unchanged treosulfan
excreted in urine is about 15-40%. The fraction excreted via glomerular filtration was estimated
based on the observed accumulative amount of unchanged treosulfan excreted in the urine.
The observed plasma PK of treosulfan following the intravenous infusion was used to estimate
the systemic transformation rate of treosulfan.
In vitro studies indicated that treosulfan was a reversible inhibitor for CYP2C19 with an IC50
value of 972 µM. Treosulfan was a reversible inhibitor for CYP3A4 using midazolam as a
substrate with an IC50 value of 1870 mM but did not show inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 when
using testosterone as a substrate. In vitro transport studies showed that treosulfan inhibited Pgp-mediated transport of loperamide with an IC50 value of ~3000 mM.
2 Michał Romański , Jacek Wachowiak , Franciszek K Główka .Treosulfan Pharmacokinetics and its Variability in Pediatric and

Adult Patients Undergoing Conditioning Prior to Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation: Current State of the Art, In-Depth
Analysis, and Perspectives. Clin Pharmacokinet. 2018 Oct;57(10):1255-1265.

150

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
and without rifampicin (D), itraconazole (E), or clarithromycin (F) coadministration 5, and
omeprazole PK profiles with and without fluvoxamine coadministration in homozygous
extensive metabolizers (G), or with and without fluvoxamine coadministration in homozygous
poor metabolizers (H) 6.
Esomeprazole and Romeprazole
Omeprazole is a proton pump inhibitor. It is administered as a racemic mixture of its two
enantiomers, esomeprazole and R-omeprazole. Omeprazole is metabolized mainly by CYP2C19
and CYP3A4. Both enantiomers are competitive and time-dependent inhibitors of CYP2C19. The
omeprazole PBPK models were used to predict the DDI potential of treosulfan as a CYP2C19
inhibitor. The Applicant’s esomeprazole and R-omeprazole PBPK models were based on
Kanacher et al. 7. The DDI between omeprazole and CYP2C19 inhibitors was validated with 2
clinical studies. Representative model validation and DDI evaluation results were shown in
Figure 23.
Digoxin
Digoxin is a P-gp substrate. The digoxin PBPK model was used to predict the DDI potential of
treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor. The Applicant’s digoxin PBPK model was based on Hanke et al. 8.
The DDI between digoxin and P-gp inhibitors was validated with 12 clinical studies.
Representative model validation and DDI evaluation results were shown in Figure 23.
FDA’s assessment
The Applicant conducted in vitro studies to evaluate the inhibitory effect of treosulfan toward
CYPs and transporters and the in-vitro Ki values for CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-gp were
incorporated into the PBPK model to evaluate the DDI potential of treosulfan as a perpetrator
(b) (4)
with CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-gp substrates.
information requests were issued

Grafapex (treosulfan)
requesting the Applicant to evaluate the time dependent treosulfan stability in incubation
media to better understand the in vitro inhibition study results.
Applicant’s response to FDA’s IR
(b) (4)

FDA’s assessment
1. CYP inhibition study
The in vitro treosulfan buffer stability study indicated that both treosulfan and EBDM were
present in the in vitro incubation system, even at the beginning of the incubation. The
concentration of EBDM at time 0 was 2121 mM in 10,000 mM treosulfan solution in
phosphate buffer in the in vitro buffer stability study, where the samples were prepared and
analyzed directly. In the in vitro CYP inhibition study, a time interval would be expected
153

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
between the solution preparation and initiation of pre-incubation, thus the concentration of
EBDM at time 0 may be higher than 2121 mM in 10,000 mM treosulfan solution in
phosphate buffer. In addition, the EBDM concentrations in the phosphate buffer appeared
to reach a plateau at around 2 hours post-incubation. Therefore, the fact that observed
inhibitory effect was not altered after pre-incubation for 30 min would not be adequate to
justify that the inhibitory effect was not caused by EBDM. There is an uncertainty in regard
to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP enzymes in
in vitro. For further discussion regarding the DDI potential of treosulfan as a CYP inhibitor,
please refer to the result section.
2. P-gp inhibition study
Both treosulfan and its two metabolites, EBDM and DEB were present in the in vitro
transport system after 60 min incubation. In addition, P-gp preferentially transports more
lipophilic substrates. Given the ranked lipophilicity is DEB>EBDM>treosulfan, there is also an
uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its metabolites) exhibited inhibitory
effect on P-gp in in vitro system. For further discussion regarding the DDI potential of
treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor, please refer to the result section.
PBPK model application
The developed PBPK model was used to simulate the DDI for treosulfan in the following
scenarios.
o To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate) following
multiple intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and a single oral dose
administration of midazolam (2 mg) on day 2 in healthy subjects.
o To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of omeprazole (a CYP2C19 substrate) following
multiple intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and multiple oral dose
administration of omeprazole (10 mg, QD for 3 days) in healthy subjects.
o To predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of digoxin (a P-gp substrate) following multiple
intravenous infusion of treosulfan (14 g/m2, QD for 3 days) and multiple oral dose
administration of digoxin (1 mg, QD for 3 days) in healthy subjects.
Results
1. Can treosulfan PBPK model describe treosulfan PK in healthy subjects?
Yes. The treosulfan model was able to capture the observed treosulfan PK profiles and
accumulative amount of unchanged treosulfan in the urine following 2 hr intravenous infusion
of 14 g/m2 on three consecutive days (Figure 24 and Table 72).

154

Reference ID: 4834011

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 214759
Grafapex (treosulfan)
2. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of midazolam (a
CYP3A4 substrate)?
As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its
metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 in in vitro incubation system. Two
potential scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of CYP3A4.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of
midazolam after incorporating the in vitro obtained Ki value of 1213 mM (calculated based
on in vitro measured IC50 value of 1870 mM) against CYP3A4. About 2-fold increase in
midazolam exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The effect of a
time difference in dosing of midazolam at 1 and 2 hours before treosulfan treatment or 0, 1,
2, 3 and 4 hours after treosulfan treatment was also evaluated. Applicant’s analysis showed
that an about 2-fold increase in midazolam exposure would be expected if midazolam is
given within 2 hours after the treosulfan administration, otherwise, the midazolam exposure
change would be expected to be less than 1.5-fold. The reviewer further conducted a
sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of CYP3A4 Ki values on the predicted midazolam
exposure. The model predicted a 6-fold increase in midazolam exposure when a 10-fold
lower Ki value was applied.
Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of CYP3A4.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition
potential of EBDM on a CYP3A substrate (such as midazolam) cannot be evaluated using a
PBPK modeling approach. Using a mechanistic static model described in the FDA guidance
on in vitro drug interaction studies 9, the reviewer’s analysis showed that midazolam AUC
ratios were less than 1.2 and about 3 when the Ki value of EBDM on CYP3A pathway was set
as the same value as the in vitro Ki value and a tenth of in vitro value, respectively. The
assumptions for these calculations are 1) fmCYP3A4 for midazolam was assumed to be 1.0;
2) the EBDM Ki value against CYP3A4 was assumed to be the same as the EBDM
concentration that was formed from treosulfan at a concentration of 1870 uM (IC50) in the
phosphate buffer at time 0 in the in vitro buffer stability study.
In conclusion, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its
metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 in in vitro system. The potential DDI of
treosulfan as a CYP3A4 inhibitor cannot be excluded.

3. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of omeprazole (a
CYP2C19 substrate)?
As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its
metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on CYP2C19 in in vitro incubation system. Two
potential scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of CYP2C19.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of
omeprazole after incorporating the in vitro derived Ki value of 778 mM (calculated based on
in vitro measured IC50 value of 972 mM) against CYP2C19. About 1.6-fold increase in
omeprazole exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The effect of a
time difference in dosing of omeprazole at 1 and 2 hours before treosulfan treatment or 0, 1,
2, 3 and 4 hours after treosulfan treatment was also evaluated. The simulated results
showed that an about 1.6-fold increase in omeprazole exposure would be expected if
omeprazole is given within 1 hour before or after the treosulfan administration, otherwise,
the omeprazole exposure change would be expected to be less than 1.3-fold. The reviewer
further conducted a sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of CYP2C19 Ki values on the
predicted omeprazole exposure. The simulated omeprazole AUC ratios were about 3.0 and
4.0 using a 10-fold lower Ki value for both CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 and a 40-fold 10 lower
CYP2C19 and a 10-fold lower CYP3A4 Ki value than the in vitro determined, respectively,
when coadministered with treosulfan.
Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of CYP2C19.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition
potential of EBDM on a CYP2C19 substrate (such as omeprazole) cannot be evaluated using a
PBPK modeling approach. Using a mechanistic static model 11, the reviewer’s analysis showed
that omeprazole AUC ratios were about 1.3 and 3 when the Ki value of EBDM on CYP2C19
pathway was set as the same value as the in vitro value and a tenth of in vitro value,
respectively. The assumptions for these calculations are 1) fmCYP2C19 for omeprazole was
assumed to be 0.86 12; 2) the EBDM in vitro Ki value against CYP2C19 was assumed to be the
same as the EBDM concentration that was formed from treosulfan at a concentration of 972
mM (IC50) in the phosphate buffer at time 0 in the in vitro buffer stability study.

Caiping Yao, Kent L Kunze, William F Trager, Evan D Kharasch, René H Levy. Comparison of in vitro and in vivo inhibition
potencies of fluvoxamine toward CYP2C19. Drug Metab Dispos. 2003 May;31(5):565-71.

4. Can treosulfan PBPK model predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of digoxin (a P-gp
substrate)?
As aforementioned, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its
metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on P-gp in in vitro incubation system. Two potential
scenarios are discussed in more detail as follows.
Scenario 1: Treosulfan is a more potent inhibitor of P-gp.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model was used to predict the effect of treosulfan on the PK of
digoxin after incorporating the in vitro obtained Ki value of 1774 mM (calculated based on in
vitro measured IC50 value of 3000 mM) against P-gp. About 1.1-fold increase in digoxin
exposure was predicted when coadministered with treosulfan. The reviewer further
conducted a sensitivity analysis to assess the effect of P-gp Ki values on the predicted digoxin
exposure. The simulated digoxin AUC ratios were about 1.35 and 1.56 using a 20-fold and
94-fold lower P-gp Ki values 13 than the in vitro determined, respectively, when
coadministered with treosulfan.
Scenario 2: The metabolite, EBDM, is a more potent inhibitor of P-gp.
The Applicant’s treosulfan model did not include the metabolite, EBDM. Thus, the inhibition
potential of EBDM on a P-gp substrate cannot be evaluated using a PBPK modeling
approach. The P-gp mediated interaction by EBDM would be expected to be lower than that
caused by treosulfan, given the ratio of EBDM to treosulfan concentration in the in vitro
systems (~0.2) was significantly higher than those observed in vivo (~0.01 in study MCFludT.16/NM and MC-FludT.17/M).
In conclusion, there is an uncertainty in regard to which moiety (treosulfan or its
metabolites) exhibited inhibitory effect on P-gp in in vitro system. The potential DDI of
treosulfan as a P-gp inhibitor cannot be excluded. This could be important for P-gp
substrates with a narrow therapeutic index.
